# Debate record: Part V, What the Thunder Said (lines 322 to 434)

20 September 2026. Ethan asked for an Eliot scholar and a reader to debate every note until it is clear, well worded and real, after finding the site's commentary written in the voice of a model. This file is the record for Part V: every gloss, way in, pathway stop and drafts record hung on lines 322 to 434, then a shorter pass over the library records, voice notes and card notes those lines open, which belong to the site thread and were handed to it as proposals (shared-proposals-part5.md). A summary of the change and the list of what was not settled are at the end.

How it ran. Two agents were briefed with Ethan's message, his two examples, a description of the faults they point to, and the plainness of Ricks and McCue, Southam and Rainey as the target. The scholar checked every claim against the sources it could reach (the 1922 text and notes, the Gospels, Shackleton's South, Weston, the Upanishad in Müller's translation, Bradley, Hesse, Dante, the Pervigilium, Nerval, Kyd, Webster, Chapman, Whitman, Tennyson, Gomme, the 1925 Poems) and the later scholarship where it could be found, and asked of each note whether it was true, whether it said what is known and how, and whether a reading was passed off as fact. The reader asked whether each note was clear, whether it helped with the line, whether a person would say it, and whether it was showing off. The editor rewrote after round one, both read again, and a third round was the final read; on substance the scholar had the last word, on wording the reader. Both signed off.

Each note below is given as it was, then each side's words on it in each round, then as it now is. The editor's decisions between rounds are in the three "editor" sections after the notes. The agents' text is their own.

## The notes, one by one

### gloss:torchlight: The Passion

**Before** (title: The Passion, in nine lines, without the resurrection)

Gethsemane by torchlight (the arrest, John 18:3), "the frosty silence in the gardens," "the agony in stony places," the shouting of the crowd, "Prison and palace" (Caiaphas's house, Pilate's), the thunder and earthquake at the crucifixion. It is Good Friday, told in the past tense by people who were there. "He who was living is now dead / We who were living are now dying / With a little patience." No Easter follows. Eliot's headnote to Part V names three themes: "the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous (see Miss Weston's book) and the present decay of eastern Europe." He wrote the section at Lausanne in a few weeks, and, unlike everything before it, Pound barely touched it: his note at its head reads "OK from here on I think." Eliot told Bertrand Russell in October 1923 that Part V was "not only the best part, but the only part that justifies the whole, at all."

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- John 18:3 confirmed: Judas comes "with lanterns and torches and weapons" (KJV). The garden and the agony are Luke 22:39–46 ("being in an agony he prayed more earnestly", 22:44). "Prison and palace" as Caiaphas's house and Pilate's is a reading; the texts have "the palace of the high priest, who was called Caiaphas" (Matthew 26:3, 26:57) and Pilate the governor (27:2). Keep it, but as a reading.
- "the thunder and earthquake at the crucifixion" is wrong on the thunder. Matthew 27:51: "the veil of the temple was rent in twain from the top to the bottom; and the earth did quake, and the rocks rent." Mark and Luke have the darkness and the veil (Mark 15:33–38, Luke 23:44–45), no earthquake. John has none of it. The only thunder near the Passion in the Gospels is John 12:29, where the crowd hears a voice from heaven and "said that it thundered", days earlier. The poem's "reverberation / Of thunder of spring" is Eliot's own.
- "It is Good Friday, told in the past tense by people who were there" and "No Easter follows" are readings. The second is fair (the section's Christ is dead and the Emmaus stranger is never recognised); say it as a reading.
- Headnote quoted correctly.
- "He wrote the section at Lausanne in a few weeks": right. Eliot was at the Hotel Ste Luce, Lausanne, from 22 November 1921 to 2 January 1922, under Vittoz, and Part V was written there; Parts I and II were drafted in London and Part III at Margate and Lausanne.
- "Pound barely touched it": right in substance. The reviewer of the facsimile in the Spectator (1 January 1972) wrote that Part V "has hardly a Poundian mark upon it. At most he nips out the redundant articles from 'The sweat is dry and the feet are in the sand'". The words "OK from here on I think" I could not verify online (see "Could not verify"); the phrase is widely quoted from the facsimile but I cannot give the page. Until someone checks the facsimile, say what is certain: Pound left Part V almost untouched.
- "Eliot told Bertrand Russell in October 1923 that Part V was 'not only the best part, but the only part that justifies the whole, at all'": the date is right (15 October 1923). The words are "It gives me very great pleasure to know that you like the Waste Land, and especially Part V which in my opinion is not only the best part, but the only part which justifies the whole, at all" (quoted from the Letters in Leithauser, "Bertrand Russell and T. S. Eliot: their dialogue"). Change "that" to "which".
Missing: The facsimile prints an earlier fragment, "After the turning of the inspired days", in which "the torchlight red on sweaty faces", the frosty gardens and the crying already stand; lines 322–330 are built from it. I could not check the facsimile pages online (see "Could not verify"), but if the drafts record is going to say that some passages predate the poem, this is the one a reader of line 322 wants. Add only once confirmed against the facsimile (or Ricks and McCue, who print the fragment).
Proposed text:
Lines 322–330 follow the Passion without naming it: the torches of the arrest (John 18:3, "lanterns and torches and weapons"), the garden and the agony (Luke 22:39–46), the trial, the crucifixion. The earthquake is Matthew's (27:51); no Gospel has thunder at the crucifixion, and the "thunder of spring" is the poem's own. No resurrection follows. Eliot's headnote names the section's three themes: "the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous (see Miss Weston's book) and the present decay of eastern Europe." He wrote Part V at Lausanne between 22 November 1921 and 2 January 1922, and Pound, who had cut Parts III and IV hard, left it almost untouched. Eliot wrote to Bertrand Russell on 15 October 1923 that Part V was "not only the best part, but the only part which justifies the whole, at all."

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title, "The Passion, in nine lines, without the resurrection": the standard's own example of a tour-guide heading. It counts, and it withholds. Replace with a plain name for what the note covers: "The Passion".
- "Gethsemane by torchlight (the arrest, John 18:3), "the frosty silence in the gardens," "the agony in stony places," the shouting of the crowd, "Prison and palace" (Caiaphas's house, Pilate's), the thunder and earthquake at the crucifixion." A sentence with no verb and nine items, some quoted from the poem and some glossed, so I cannot tell which words are the poem's and which are the explanation. I cannot say back what "the frosty silence in the gardens" refers to, because the note only repeats the phrase. Write sentences that say: this phrase refers to this event.
- "It is Good Friday, told in the past tense by people who were there." Turned for sound, and not quite right: "After the ..." is not a tense, lines 328 to 330 are in the present, and the arrest was the night before. The plain point is that the lines look back on the events as over.
- "No Easter follows." A two-word fragment placed for effect. I am also not sure it is true: Eliot's headnote says the first theme of this part is the journey to Emmaus, which is an Easter story. Say that these lines stop at the death and let the note on 360 take Emmaus.
- The Lausanne, Pound and Russell sentences are plain and sourced. Keep them. "unlike everything before it" is a small overstatement (Pound cut heavily, but not everything); "Pound, who had cut heavily in the earlier parts" is what is meant.
Questions for the scholar: (1) Is thunder in the gospel accounts of the crucifixion? The earthquake is; I do not remember thunder, and "reverberation / Of thunder of spring over distant mountains" may not be a crucifixion detail at all. (2) Is "frosty" from John 18:18, where the servants make a fire because "it was cold"? If so the note should say so; that is the kind of detail a reader can use. (3) "Stony places": Gethsemane, Golgotha, or neither?
Proposed text:
Title: The Passion
The lines follow the arrest, trial and crucifixion of Christ. The torchlight is the arrest in the garden at night (John 18:3); the gardens are Gethsemane; "The shouting and the crying" is the crowd; "Prison and palace" are the high priest's house and Pilate's; the "agony in stony places" and the "reverberation / Of thunder" belong to the crucifixion [scholar to confirm both]. The lines look back on all of it as finished, and they stop at the death: "He who was living is now dead." Eliot's headnote to Part V names three themes for what follows: "the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous (see Miss Weston's book) and the present decay of eastern Europe." He wrote the section at Lausanne in November and December 1921. Pound, who had cut heavily in the earlier parts, wrote at its head "OK from here on I think." Eliot told Bertrand Russell in October 1923 that Part V was "not only the best part, but the only part that justifies the whole, at all."

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- John 18:18 confirmed: "And the servants and officers stood there, who had made a fire of coals; for it was cold: and they warmed themselves: and Peter stood with them, and warmed himself." But this is in the high priest's courtyard after the arrest (18:15–18), not in the garden. "The gardens are Gethsemane ... where, John says, 'it was cold'" puts the cold in the wrong place. The verse still serves "frosty": it is the night of the arrest that was cold.
- "Prison and palace has been read as the high priest's house and Pilate's": right as a reading.
- "The earthquake at the crucifixion is Matthew's (27:51). No Gospel has thunder there": confirmed (Matthew 27:51; Mark 15:33–38 and Luke 23:44–45 have darkness and the veil only; John nothing).
- "Pound ... wrote at the head of the typescript 'OK from here on I think.'" The words now have a second, independent quotation from the facsimile: Patrick J. Keane, "Convergences: Memories Involving The Waste Land Manuscript" (Numéro Cinq, 22 February 2011), on Pound looking at the manuscript in 1969: "except when it came to the fifth and unimprovable final section, which he had marked, 'OK from here on I think.'" With the Spectator's 1972 review ("hardly a Poundian mark upon it") that is enough to print; the page of the facsimile is still to be recorded when someone has the book.
- "between late November 1921 and the first days of 1922": right (22 November to 2 January).
- Russell letter: see "Still unsettled". The rewrite's "which" follows the one transcription I can reach.
- The reader's question on "stony places" (Gethsemane, Golgotha, or neither) was not answered in round 1. Neither is named by the poem. The phrase itself is the King James Bible's, in the parable of the sower: "Some fell upon stony places, where they had not much earth" (Matthew 13:5, again 13:20). That is a verbal fact, not one of Eliot's notes, and the gloss can leave it out; if it is used, mark it as an echo.
Proposed text:
[replace the second and third sentences] The torchlight is the arrest in the garden at night, when Judas comes "with lanterns and torches and weapons" (John 18:3). The gardens are Gethsemane, where he prayed "being in an agony" (Luke 22:44); John says of that night, when Peter warmed himself at the fire in the high priest's court, that "it was cold" (18:18).

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "The earthquake at the crucifixion is Matthew's (27:51). No Gospel has thunder there; the "thunder of spring over distant mountains" is the poem's own." The earthquake sentence explains something that is not in the poem; it is left over from the round-1 argument about the thunder. Fold it into the thunder sentence as the nearest thing the Gospels have.
- "Pound, who had cut hard in the earlier parts": "cut hard" is a turn; "cut heavily" is what a person says.
- Length: thirteen sentences is long for a card, but this is the head of the section and each sentence does a job. If it must be shorter, cut the Russell sentence; the "OK from here on" way carries it.
Proposed text:
Lines 322 to 330 look back on the arrest, trial and death of Christ without naming him. The torchlight is the arrest in the garden at night, when Judas comes "with lanterns and torches and weapons" (John 18:3). The gardens are Gethsemane, where he prayed "being in an agony" (Luke 22:44) and where, John says, "it was cold" (18:18). "The shouting and the crying" is the crowd; "Prison and palace" has been read as the high priest's house and Pilate's. No Gospel has thunder at the crucifixion (Matthew has an earthquake, 27:51); the "thunder of spring over distant mountains" is the poem's own. The lines stop at the death: "He who was living is now dead." Eliot's headnote to Part V names three themes for what follows: "the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous (see Miss Weston's book) and the present decay of eastern Europe." He wrote the section at Lausanne between late November 1921 and the first days of 1922. Pound, who had cut heavily in the earlier parts, wrote at the head of the typescript "OK from here on I think." Eliot wrote to Bertrand Russell on 15 October 1923 that Part V was "not only the best part, but the only part which justifies the whole, at all."

**The reader, round 3**

Findings:
- "of that night John says "it was cold" (18:18), when Peter stood warming himself in the high priest's courtyard." The "when" clause hangs off "it was cold", so it reads as if the cold is timed by Peter's standing there. Two sentences, with the courtyard attached to "that night", say it plainly.
Proposed text (the third sentence; the rest unchanged):
The gardens are Gethsemane, where he prayed "being in an agony" (Luke 22:44). John says of that night, when Peter stood warming himself at a fire in the high priest's courtyard, that "it was cold" (18:18).

**After** (title: The Passion)

Lines 322 to 330 look back on the arrest, trial and death of Christ without naming him. The torchlight is the arrest in the garden at night, when Judas comes "with lanterns and torches and weapons" (John 18:3). The gardens are Gethsemane, where he prayed "being in an agony" (Luke 22:44). John says of that night, when Peter stood warming himself at a fire in the high priest's courtyard, that "it was cold" (18:18). "The shouting and the crying" is the crowd; "Prison and palace" has been read as the high priest's house and Pilate's. No Gospel has thunder at the crucifixion (Matthew has an earthquake, 27:51); the "thunder of spring over distant mountains" is the poem's own. The lines stop at the death: "He who was living is now dead." Eliot's headnote to Part V names three themes for what follows: "the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous (see Miss Weston's book) and the present decay of eastern Europe." He wrote the section at Lausanne between late November 1921 and the first days of 1922. Pound, who had cut heavily in the earlier parts, wrote at the head of the typescript "OK from here on I think."

### gloss:no-water: The road through the mountains

**Before**

Twenty-nine lines with almost no punctuation and no full stops, driven by the two words "rock" and "water" as if the speaker could think of nothing else, because he can't. It is the road to Emmaus, the approach to the Grail chapel through a waste land, the retreat through the Carpathians of a beaten army, and a man's own dry mouth: "Dead mountain mouth of carious teeth that cannot spit." The hymn of "If there were water" that follows (lines 346–358) is the poem's only stretch of pure longing, and it is built on a sound the speaker cannot hear.

Eliot's note on the hermit-thrush is the most personal in the apparatus: <i>Turdus aonalaschkae pallasii</i>, "which I have heard in Quebec County," and Chapman's <i>Handbook of Birds of Eastern North America</i> on its "purity and sweetness of tone." Its "water-dripping song" is "justly celebrated": "Drip drop drip drop drop drop drop / But there is no water." The thrush is also Whitman's, from the lilac poem behind line 2, the bird that sings the carol of death in the swamp. Listen to a recording and the poem's onomatopoeia turns out to be exact.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- The counts hold: lines 331–359 are twenty-nine lines; in the 1922 text they carry no punctuation but hyphens and no full stop; "rock" occurs nine times and "water" eleven.
- "It is the road to Emmaus, the approach to the Grail chapel through a waste land": these two are Eliot's headnote. "the retreat through the Carpathians of a beaten army" is nobody's that I can find; it is not in the headnote, the notes, or Hesse, and no critic I know of says Carpathians. Cut it or name its source.
- "the poem's only stretch of pure longing" is the site's reading, stated as fact.
- Eliot's note is quoted correctly ("which I have heard in Quebec County"; Chapman on "purity and sweetness of tone"). Chapman's Handbook of Birds of Eastern North America (1895), p. 400, entry "759b. Turdus aonalaschkae pallasii (Cab.). Hermit Thrush", confirmed: "it is most at home in secluded woodland and thickety retreats"; "Its notes are not remarkable for variety or volume, but in purity and sweetness of tone and exquisite modulation they are unequaled."
- But "water-dripping song" is not Chapman's. The phrase occurs nowhere in the Handbook (checked the full text). Eliot's note presents it as celebrated; a Notes and Queries article (March 2014, "Did Eliot Mis-ascribe 'the water-dripping song' in The Waste Land?") finds it in no ornithological writing and reports that the hermit-thrush's song, a series of flute-like phrases, does not sound like dripping water. So "Listen to a recording and the poem's onomatopoeia turns out to be exact" is contradicted by the ornithology and should go. What can be said: the note's phrase is Eliot's, not the field guide's.
- Whitman: "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd" confirmed ("Solitary the thrush, / The hermit withdrawn to himself"; "Song of the bleeding throat"; "he sang the carol of death"). The connection with lines 2 and 357 is a critics' reading, not Eliot's note; Eliot wrote in 1928 that he "did not read Whitman until much later in life" (introduction to Pound's Selected Poems). Attribute the reading to commentators and let the reader weigh it.
- "the most personal in the apparatus" is opinion; harmless, but it is the voice.
Missing: the year and page of Chapman (1895, p. 400); that Eliot's copy was a gift from his mother on his fourteenth birthday, 1902 (his own later inscription, quoted on the Eliot estate's Gloucester page), which is what makes the note personal.
Proposed text:
Lines 331–359 have no punctuation but hyphens and no full stop. Two words carry them: "rock" nine times, "water" eleven. Eliot's headnote gives the journey to Emmaus and the approach to the Chapel Perilous through a waste land; the dry mouth of line 339 is the poem's own. Lines 346–358 turn to a sound of water that is not there, and end on the hermit-thrush.

Eliot's note names the bird, <i>Turdus aonalaschkae pallasii</i>, "which I have heard in Quebec County", and quotes Chapman's <i>Handbook of Birds of Eastern North America</i> (1895), p. 400, on the "purity and sweetness of tone" of its song. Eliot's copy of Chapman was a birthday present from his mother in 1902. The phrase "water-dripping song", which the note calls "justly celebrated", is not in Chapman; the hermit-thrush's song is a series of flute-like phrases, and ornithologists do not describe it as dripping water. Commentators also hear behind the line the hermit-thrush of Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd", the elegy behind line 2, which sings "the carol of death" in the swamp. Eliot said in 1928 that he did not read Whitman until late.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "Twenty-nine lines with almost no punctuation and no full stops, driven by the two words "rock" and "water" as if the speaker could think of nothing else, because he can't." A count, then a fragment, then a joke ("because he can't"). The fact under it, that there is no full stop from 331 to 359 and the lines keep returning to "rock" and "water", is worth one plain sentence.
- "It is the road to Emmaus, the approach to the Grail chapel through a waste land, the retreat through the Carpathians of a beaten army, and a man's own dry mouth: ..." The "X is a, b, c and d" sentence, four readings asserted with "is". Two are Eliot's (Emmaus, the Chapel Perilous); one adds specifics on top of Eliot's "present decay of eastern Europe" that I cannot find anywhere in the materials (the Carpathians, a beaten army); one is the writer's own. Give Eliot's three themes as his and say the road can be read as any of them.
- "The hymn of "If there were water" that follows (lines 346–358) is the poem's only stretch of pure longing, and it is built on a sound the speaker cannot hear." "Only stretch of pure longing" ranks and tells me how to feel; "built on a sound the speaker cannot hear" is a phrase turned for itself. Say what the lines do: imagine water, and end with a bird whose song sounds like it.
- "Eliot's note on the hermit-thrush is the most personal in the apparatus". A ranking. Say what the note says: that he heard the bird himself.
- "The thrush is also Whitman's, from the lilac poem behind line 2, the bird that sings the carol of death in the swamp." Assumes I already know Whitman is "behind line 2". Name the poem, and mark the connection as one critics make; Eliot's notes do not.
- "Listen to a recording and the poem's onomatopoeia turns out to be exact." An instruction and a confident verdict. Say a recording is in the listening room and stop.
- Title, "The road through the mountains": plain. Keep.
Questions for the scholar: Where do "the Carpathians" and "a beaten army" come from? If from Hesse or a critic, name it; if not, cut. Is the Whitman connection anywhere in Eliot, or is it the critics'? Whose?
Proposed text:
From here to line 359 there is no full stop, and the lines keep returning to two words, "rock" and "water." Eliot's headnote gives the first part of the section three themes: the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous, and "the present decay of eastern Europe." The road through the mountains can be read as any of the three. Lines 346 to 358 imagine the water that is not there, and end with the sound of a bird.

Eliot's note on line 357 names the bird, the hermit-thrush, "which I have heard in Quebec County," and quotes Chapman's <i>Handbook of Birds of Eastern North America</i> on the "purity and sweetness" of its notes; its "water-dripping song," the note says, "is justly celebrated." Line 358 is Eliot's rendering of it. The hermit thrush is also the bird of Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd," the elegy critics have set behind the lilacs of line 2; Eliot's notes do not mention it. A recording is in the listening room.

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- "ornithologists describe the song as a series of flute-like phrases": Cornell Lab of Ornithology (All About Birds) has "ethereal, paired phrases, long, flute-like notes backed by complex, reedy phrases"; Audubon and BirdNote say "flutelike". Fair.
- "His copy of Chapman was a present from his mother for his fourteenth birthday, in 1902": Eliot's own later inscription, "A much coveted birthday present on my 14th birthday", quoted by the Eliot estate (tseliot.com, "T. S. Eliot's Gloucester"). Right.
- "Eliot said in 1928 that he had not read Whitman until late in life": the words are "I did not read Whitman until much later in life, and had to conquer an aversion to his form, as well as to much of his matter, in order to do so" (introduction to Pound's Selected Poems, 1928). The paraphrase is fair. The reader asked whether "rarely admitted" was right: Eliot had reviewed Emory Holloway's Whitman under the title "Whitman and Tennyson" in the Nation and Athenaeum, 18 December 1926, so by 1926 he had read him; the 1928 sentence is about his formative years. The rewrite no longer says "rarely admitted", so nothing to change; the library-record proposal (below) can add the 1926 review if it wants.
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "there is no punctuation and no full stop": a full stop is punctuation; say it once.
- "Commentators also hear behind the line the hermit thrush of Whitman's ..., the elegy whose lilacs are often set behind line 2": "behind" twice, meaning "as a source for"; a reader may not know the idiom. Also "hermit thrush" here against "hermit-thrush" two sentences earlier; use Eliot's hyphen throughout except inside a quotation.
- The rest is clear, and the birthday present is the kind of thing a person would tell you. Keep.
Proposed text (the first sentence and the Whitman sentence; the rest unchanged):
From line 331 to line 359 there is no punctuation, and the lines keep returning to two words, "rock" and "water."
[...]
Commentators also point to the hermit-thrush of Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd," the elegy often cited for the lilacs of line 2; Eliot said in 1928 that he had not read Whitman until late in life.

**The scholar, round 3**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "the phrase is Eliot's, not Chapman's" says more than is known. Established: "water-dripping song" is nowhere in Chapman's Handbook (full text checked) and, per Irmscher (Partial Answers 12.2, 2014, 231–49), in no ornithological writing. Not established: that Eliot made the phrase. Sources have been proposed for it, Henley's In Hospital (Chandran, English Language Notes 43.1, September 2005, p. 59) and a children's story about the saw-whet owl (Notes and Queries 61.1, March 2014), and none is agreed. "Not Chapman's" is the fact; "Eliot's" is an inference presented as one.
- The same overstatement stands in the way "Hear the hermit-thrush" and in the chapman-birds proposal, and in the final/ copies of both files.
Proposed text (third sentence of the gloss; the rest unchanged):
The note calls the bird's "water-dripping song" justly celebrated; the phrase is not Chapman's, and no source for it is agreed; ornithologists describe the song as a series of flute-like phrases.
Way "Hear the hermit-thrush" (one clause): "water-dripping" is not Chapman's phrase.
Proposal chapman-birds (first sentence): The phrase "water-dripping song" in Eliot's note is not Chapman's.

**After**

From line 331 to line 359 there is no punctuation, and the lines keep returning to two words, "rock" and "water." Eliot's headnote gives the first part of the section three themes: the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous, and "the present decay of eastern Europe." The road through the mountains can be read as any of them. Lines 346 to 358 imagine the water that is not there, and end with the sound of a bird.

Eliot's note on line 357 names the bird, the hermit-thrush, "which I have heard in Quebec County," and quotes Chapman's <i>Handbook of Birds of Eastern North America</i> (1895) on the "purity and sweetness of tone" of its song. His copy of Chapman was a present from his mother for his fourteenth birthday, in 1902. The note calls the bird's "water-dripping song" justly celebrated; the phrase is not Chapman's, and no source for it is agreed; ornithologists describe the song as a series of flute-like phrases. Commentators also point to the hermit-thrush of Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd," the elegy often cited for the lilacs of line 2; Eliot said in 1928 that he had not read Whitman until late in life. Recordings of the bird are in the listening room.

### gloss:third: Emmaus, and the men on South Georgia

**Before**

Luke 24: two disciples walking to Emmaus on the third day are joined by a stranger who explains the scriptures to them and whom they recognise only when he breaks bread, at which point he vanishes. "Their eyes were holden that they should not know him." Eliot's note adds the second source, from memory ("I forget which, but I think one of Shackleton's"): the crossing of South Georgia in May 1916 by Shackleton, Worsley and Crean at the end of the <i>Endurance</i> expedition, told in Shackleton's <i>South</i> (1919), where each of the three men privately felt a fourth walking with them. The hooded figure, "I do not know whether a man or a woman," is the Hanged Man that Madame Sosostris could not find, and also Tiresias, and also nobody: "When I count, there are only you and I together." The question is asked and, as in the Grail castle, not answered.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Luke 24 confirmed: "that same day" (24:13), i.e. the day of the resurrection, "to day is the third day" (24:21); "their eyes were holden that they should not know him" (24:16); "he expounded unto them in all the scriptures" (24:27); "he took bread, and blessed it, and brake, and gave to them. And their eyes were opened, and they knew him; and he vanished out of their sight" (24:30–31). The summary is accurate.
- Shackleton confirmed, South (1919), chapter X, "Across South Georgia": "I know that during that long and racking march of thirty-six hours over the unnamed mountains and glaciers of South Georgia it seemed to me often that we were four, not three. I said nothing to my companions on the point, but afterwards Worsley said to me, 'Boss, I had a curious feeling on the march that there was another person with us.' Crean confessed to the same idea." The crossing began at 2 a.m. on Friday 19 May 1916 and ended at Stromness on the 20th. "May 1916", "Shackleton, Worsley and Crean", "end of the Endurance expedition" all right. "each of the three men privately felt a fourth" is a fair paraphrase.
- "The hooded figure ... is the Hanged Man that Madame Sosostris could not find, and also Tiresias, and also nobody." Three claims of identity, of unequal standing. The Hanged Man is Eliot's own association, in the note on line 46: "I associate him with the hooded figure in the passage of the disciples to Emmaus in Part V." Tiresias is a critics' suggestion (the line "I do not know whether a man or a woman" recalls the seer of two sexes) and has no note behind it. "Nobody" is a reading of line 361. Say which is which.
- "as in the Grail castle, not answered": in the Grail story the fault is that the question is not asked; here it is asked and not answered. The inversion is a reading; fine if marked.
Missing: the note on line 46 should be quoted, since it is Eliot's only word on who the third is.
Proposed text:
Luke 24:13–31: on the day of the resurrection two disciples walking to Emmaus are joined by a stranger, "but their eyes were holden that they should not know him"; he expounds the scriptures to them, breaks bread, and "vanished out of their sight". Eliot's note gives the second source from memory ("I forget which, but I think one of Shackleton's"): the thirty-six-hour crossing of South Georgia by Shackleton, Worsley and Crean on 19–20 May 1916, at the end of the <i>Endurance</i> expedition, told in <i>South</i> (1919): "it seemed to me often that we were four, not three." Worsley and Crean told him afterwards that they had felt the same. Eliot's note on line 46 says he associates the Hanged Man of the Tarot "with the hooded figure in the passage of the disciples to Emmaus". Some readers also hear Tiresias in "I do not know whether a man or a woman"; that is a reading, not a note. The question of line 360 is not answered.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "Emmaus, and the men on South Georgia": names the two sources. Fine.
- The first two sentences (Luke 24; Eliot's note and Shackleton) are plain, sourced, and exactly what I want here. Keep as they are.
- "The hooded figure, "I do not know whether a man or a woman," is the Hanged Man that Madame Sosostris could not find, and also Tiresias, and also nobody: "When I count, there are only you and I together."" The confident "is" on a list of three that ends in a flourish ("and also nobody"). The Hanged Man link is Eliot's own, from his note on line 46, and should be given as his. Tiresias is a reading; say whose or drop it. "Nobody" is a joke on line 361; cut.
- "The question is asked and, as in the Grail castle, not answered." A closing bow that also assumes I know about the unasked question in the Grail story. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: Whose reading is Tiresias here, and is it worth keeping?
Proposed text: (first two sentences unchanged, then:)
Eliot's note on the Tarot pack at line 46 says he associates the Hanged Man "with the hooded figure in the passage of the disciples to Emmaus in Part V"; this is the card Madame Sosostris could not find. "I do not know whether a man or a woman" has also been read as pointing to Tiresias [whose reading].

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- "on 19 and 20 May 1916": the crossing began at 2 a.m. on Friday 19 May and reached Stromness on the 20th (South, chapter X). Right.
- "said nothing of it until afterwards": Shackleton's words ("I said nothing to my companions on the point, but afterwards Worsley said to me ..."). Right.
- The reader asked whose reading Tiresias is. It is a commonplace of the commentaries (the seer of two sexes behind "I do not know whether a man or a woman"); I cannot name a first proposer, and "some readers" is honest.
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "he expounds the scriptures to them, breaks bread at supper, and "vanished out of their sight."" The quotation is in the past tense inside a present-tense sentence; give it its own clause.
- "that is a reading, and Eliot's notes do not make it": stiff; see the note at the head.
Proposed text:
In Luke 24, on the day of the resurrection, two disciples walking to Emmaus are joined by a stranger, "but their eyes were holden that they should not know him"; he expounds the scriptures to them, breaks bread at supper, and is gone: "he vanished out of their sight." Eliot's note gives the second source from memory ("I forget which, but I think one of Shackleton's"): the crossing of South Georgia by Shackleton, Worsley and Crean on 19 and 20 May 1916, at the end of the <i>Endurance</i> expedition, told in Shackleton's <i>South</i> (1919). Each of the three men felt that a fourth was walking with them, and said nothing of it until afterwards. Eliot's note on the Tarot pack at line 46 says he associates the Hanged Man "with the hooded figure in the passage of the disciples to Emmaus in Part V"; that is the card Madame Sosostris could not find. Some readers also hear Tiresias in "I do not know whether a man or a woman"; Eliot's notes do not say so.

**After**

In Luke 24, on the day of the resurrection, two disciples walking to Emmaus are joined by a stranger, "but their eyes were holden that they should not know him"; he expounds the scriptures to them, breaks bread at supper, and is gone: "he vanished out of their sight." Eliot's note gives the second source from memory ("I forget which, but I think one of Shackleton's"): the crossing of South Georgia by Shackleton, Worsley and Crean on 19 and 20 May 1916, at the end of the <i>Endurance</i> expedition, told in Shackleton's <i>South</i> (1919). Each of the three men felt that a fourth was walking with them, and said nothing of it until afterwards. Eliot's note on the Tarot pack at line 46 says he associates the Hanged Man "with the hooded figure in the passage of the disciples to Emmaus in Part V"; that is the card Madame Sosostris could not find. Some readers also hear Tiresias in "I do not know whether a man or a woman"; Eliot's notes do not say so.

### gloss:hordes: Eastern Europe, 1920

**Before**

Hermann Hesse's <i>Blick ins Chaos</i> (1920; <i>In Sight of Chaos</i> in the English translation of 1923), three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky, quoted in Eliot's note. Eliot admired the book enough to visit Hesse at Montagnola in May 1922 and to print him in the first number of <i>The Criterion</i> that October, alongside the poem. The "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" are the refugees and armies of the collapse of the Russian and Austro-Hungarian empires; the "maternal lamentation" is the women at the cross ("Daughters of Jerusalem, weep not for me," Luke 23:28) and every mother in Europe in 1919. Then the roll-call: "Jerusalem Athens Alexandria / Vienna London," the cities that were each in turn the centre of the world, each "Unreal," the word from line 60 now standing on its own with no city after it. London is the last name on the list.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Hesse, Blick ins Chaos (Bern: Seldwyla, 1920): the title page reads "Drei Aufsätze". The three are "Die Brüder Karamasoff oder Der Untergang Europas", "Gedanken zu Dostojewskis 'Idiot'", and "Gespräch über die Neutöner", a dialogue about the young poets (checked in the 1920 text on archive.org). So "three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky" is right. The passage Eliot quotes is the close of the Karamazov essay; Hesse's text reads "in heiligem Wahn" and "Über"; Eliot's "im heiligem" is his misprint, kept in Poems 1909–1925.
- In Sight of Chaos, translated by Stephen Hudson (Sydney Schiff), Zurich: Seldwyla, 1923, contains the two Dostoevsky essays only. Say so if the English title is given.
- "Eliot admired the book enough to visit Hesse at Montagnola in May 1922": Eliot wrote to Hesse on 13 March 1922 praising the book and visited him at Montagnola, near Lugano, on 28 May 1922 (Letters, vol. I, p. 509 n., as reported on Rickard Parker's Hesse page; I did not see the Letters themselves). "and to print him in the first number of The Criterion that October, alongside the poem": right; the piece was "Recent German Poetry" (Criterion I.1, October 1922), a translation of the third essay.
- "The 'hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains' are the refugees and armies of the collapse of the Russian and Austro-Hungarian empires": the headnote says "the present decay of eastern Europe" and the note quotes Hesse on "the half of eastern Europe ... on the way to chaos"; the empires are the site's gloss. State it as the usual reading of the headnote.
- "the 'maternal lamentation' is the women at the cross ('Daughters of Jerusalem, weep not for me,' Luke 23:28) and every mother in Europe in 1919": Luke 23:27–28 confirmed ("women, which also bewailed and lamented him ... Daughters of Jerusalem, weep not for me"). The identification is a reading; "every mother in Europe in 1919" is rhetoric.
- "the cities that were each in turn the centre of the world" is a reading (Vienna especially). "the word from line 60 now standing on its own": "Unreal City" is lines 60 and 207; fine.
- The quote field reproduces Eliot's misprint "im heiligem"; the library record corrects it. Either is defensible; be consistent about which text the quote field is.
- The translation "singing drunken hymns" compresses "singt betrunken und hymnisch" (sings drunkenly and hymn-like); acceptable, but "sings, drunk and hymn-like, as Dmitri Karamazov sang" is closer.
Missing: nothing beyond the above.
Proposed text:
Hermann Hesse's <i>Blick ins Chaos</i> (Bern, 1920) is three essays, two on Dostoevsky and one on the new poets. Eliot's note quotes the close of the first, "The Brothers Karamazov, or the Downfall of Europe"; Stephen Hudson's English version, <i>In Sight of Chaos</i> (Zurich, 1923), has the two Dostoevsky essays. Eliot wrote to Hesse in March 1922 to praise the book, visited him at Montagnola on 28 May 1922, and printed him ("Recent German Poetry") in the first number of <i>The Criterion</i>, October 1922, beside <i>The Waste Land</i>. The headnote gives "the present decay of eastern Europe" as the section's third theme, and the "hooded hordes" are usually read with it. "Maternal lamentation" has been read with the women who "bewailed and lamented" Christ on the way to the cross (Luke 23:27–28). "Jerusalem Athens Alexandria / Vienna London": five cities, then "Unreal", the word of lines 60 and 207, now with no city after it.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "Eastern Europe, 1920": plain. Keep.
- "Hermann Hesse's Blick ins Chaos (1920; ...), three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky, quoted in Eliot's note." Facts I want, in a sentence with no main verb. Give it one.
- The Montagnola and Criterion sentence: factual and useful. Keep.
- "The "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" are the refugees and armies of the collapse of the Russian and Austro-Hungarian empires". A reading stated as fact with "are". Eliot's headnote says "the present decay of eastern Europe" and his note quotes Hesse; give those and let the rest be a reading.
- "the "maternal lamentation" is the women at the cross ("Daughters of Jerusalem, weep not for me," Luke 23:28) and every mother in Europe in 1919." "Is" again, and "every mother in Europe in 1919" is written for the sound. Offer Luke 23:28 as a reading; drop the flourish.
- "Then the roll-call: "Jerusalem Athens Alexandria / Vienna London," the cities that were each in turn the centre of the world, each "Unreal," the word from line 60 now standing on its own with no city after it." "Roll-call" is a metaphor about the poem; "each in turn the centre of the world" is a claim I cannot check (Vienna? Alexandria?). The cross-reference to "Unreal City" at line 60 is the useful part.
- "London is the last name on the list." The standard's example. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: Is "Unreal City" also in Part III (line 207)? If so cite both. Is Luke 23:28 a critic's reading or the site's?
Proposed text:
Eliot's note on these lines quotes Hermann Hesse's <i>Blick ins Chaos</i> (1920; in English as <i>In Sight of Chaos</i>, 1923), three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky, on a Europe "on the way to chaos." Eliot visited Hesse at Montagnola in May 1922 and printed him in the first number of <i>The Criterion</i> that October, alongside the poem. His headnote calls this theme "the present decay of eastern Europe." The "maternal lamentation" has been read with the women who weep for Christ on the way to the cross (Luke 23:28). Lines 375 to 377 name five cities and then "Unreal," the word of line 60, "Unreal City," this time with no city after it.

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- Quote field: the 1920 Seldwyla text reads, letter for letter, "fährt betrunken in heiligem Wahn am Abgrund entlang und singt dazu, singt betrunken und hymnisch wie Dmitri Karamasoff sang. Über diese Lieder lacht der Bürger beleidigt, der Heilige und Seher hört sie mit Tränen." (archive.org copy, p. 26 of the essay). The quote field now matches it exactly. Eliot's note (materials B) keeps his "im heiligem" and "Ueber"; that is his text and stays.
- "three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky": title page "Drei Aufsätze"; contents "Die Brüder Karamasoff oder Der Untergang Europas", "Gedanken zu Dostojewskis 'Idiot'", "Gespräch über die Neutöner". Right. In Sight of Chaos (1923) with the two Dostoevsky essays: right (WorldCat title: "The Brothers Karamazoff ... Thoughts on Dostoevsky's 'Idiot'").
- 28 May 1922 for the visit: from the Letters, vol. I, p. 509 n., as reported second-hand; not seen in the printed volume.
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "Stephen Hudson's English version, In Sight of Chaos (1923), has the two Dostoevsky essays." Bibliography, not help with the line; it belongs in the library record, whose title already gives the English name. Cut here and add there (see source:hesse-blick-ins-chaos below).
- "are usually read with it" and "has been read with the women": the idiom; say what is meant.
Proposed text:
Eliot's note on these lines quotes Hermann Hesse's <i>Blick ins Chaos</i> (Bern, 1920), three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky; the passage is the close of the first, "The Brothers Karamazov, or the Downfall of Europe." Eliot wrote to Hesse in March 1922 to praise the book, visited him at Montagnola on 28 May 1922, and printed him in the first number of <i>The Criterion</i>, October 1922, beside <i>The Waste Land</i>. The headnote calls this theme "the present decay of eastern Europe," and the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" are usually taken to refer to it. "Maternal lamentation" has been connected with the women who "bewailed and lamented" Christ on the way to the cross (Luke 23:27–28). Lines 375 to 377 name five cities and then "Unreal," the word of "Unreal City" at lines 60 and 207, this time with no city after it.

**After**

Eliot's note on these lines quotes Hermann Hesse's <i>Blick ins Chaos</i> (Bern, 1920), three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky; the passage is the close of the first, "The Brothers Karamazov, or the Downfall of Europe." Eliot wrote to Hesse in March 1922 to praise the book, visited him at Montagnola on 28 May 1922, and printed him in the first number of <i>The Criterion</i>, October 1922, beside <i>The Waste Land</i>. The headnote calls this theme "the present decay of eastern Europe," and the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" are usually taken to refer to it. "Maternal lamentation" has been connected with the women who "bewailed and lamented" Christ on the way to the cross (Luke 23:27–28). Lines 375 to 377 name five cities and then "Unreal," the word of "Unreal City" at lines 60 and 207, this time with no city after it.

### gloss:bats: From an earlier fragment

**Before** (title: A nightmare from an older notebook)

These eight lines predate the rest of the poem. They come from a fragment in Eliot's papers that begins "So through the evening, through the violet air," printed in the facsimile edition, and the images are Gothic and painterly: the woman fiddling on her own hair, the bats with baby faces, the towers upside down in air, bells that "kept the hours" like St Mary Woolnoth's, and voices "singing out of empty cisterns and exhausted wells," which is Jeremiah's "broken cisterns, that can hold no water." The violet hour of Part III has become "violet light." The poem's phrase for the whole passage would be the one it used of the pictures beside Philomel's: "withered stumps of time," pictures on the wall of the mind.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "These eight lines predate the rest of the poem. They come from a fragment in Eliot's papers that begins 'So through the evening, through the violet air,' printed in the facsimile edition": the fragment exists and is printed in the facsimile (pp. 112–15 as usually cited) and by Ricks and McCue; its images (the woman fiddling on her hair, the bats with baby faces, the towers upside down, the bells, the voices from cisterns and wells) are the source of lines 378–385. I could not reach the facsimile to confirm its date; Valerie Eliot placed it among the early fragments, and the usual dating is 1914 or thereabouts, but that needs the page (see "Could not verify"). "Predate the rest of the poem" overstates: other passages also predate 1921 (see draft:ok-from-here).
- "bells that 'kept the hours' like St Mary Woolnoth's": line 67 confirmed ("Saint Mary Woolnoth kept the hours").
- "which is Jeremiah's 'broken cisterns, that can hold no water'": Jeremiah 2:13 confirmed in those words. But "is" claims an identity; the echo is a reading, and I know of no note of Eliot's for it. Write "recalls".
- "The violet hour of Part III has become 'violet light'": lines 215 and 220, fine.
- "The poem's phrase for the whole passage would be ... 'withered stumps of time'": line 104; the application is the site's.
- "Gothic and painterly" is the voice.
Missing: nothing a reader needs beyond a sourced date for the fragment.
Proposed text:
Lines 378–385 rework an earlier fragment that begins "So through the evening, through the violet air", printed in the 1971 facsimile among the poems that fed the drafts; the woman fiddling on her hair, the bats with baby faces, the inverted towers, the bells and the voices from cisterns and wells are already there. "Kept the hours" repeats line 67, where Saint Mary Woolnoth kept them; the "violet hour" of lines 215 and 220 becomes "violet light". "Empty cisterns and exhausted wells" recalls Jeremiah 2:13, "broken cisterns, that can hold no water"; that is a reading, not one of Eliot's notes.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title, "A nightmare from an older notebook": "nightmare" tells me how to feel and "notebook" is a picturesque guess at what the papers are. Say what it is.
- "These eight lines predate the rest of the poem." Plain, but by how much? A date would help.
- "the images are Gothic and painterly: the woman fiddling on her own hair, the bats with baby faces, the towers upside down in air, bells that "kept the hours" like St Mary Woolnoth's, and voices "singing out of empty cisterns and exhausted wells," which is Jeremiah's "broken cisterns, that can hold no water."" One sentence, six items, most of which only repeat the poem back to me. The useful parts (the bells recall St Mary Woolnoth in Part I; the cisterns may recall Jeremiah) are buried in it, and "which is Jeremiah's" is a confident "is" for an echo Eliot did not note.
- "The violet hour of Part III has become "violet light."" Plain and useful. Keep, with line numbers.
- "The poem's phrase for the whole passage would be the one it used of the pictures beside Philomel's: "withered stumps of time," pictures on the wall of the mind." A metaphor about the poem, borrowed from the poem, tied in a bow. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: When is the "So through the evening" fragment dated? The rights rule allows a phrase from the 1971 facsimile: is a whole first line a phrase? (The same line is quoted in draft:ok-from-here.) Which lines are St Mary Woolnoth (67?) and the violet hour (215, 220?), so the note can cite them? Is Jeremiah 2:13 an editor's note or the site's own?
Proposed text:
Title: From an earlier fragment
These lines are older than the rest of the poem. They come from a fragment among Eliot's papers, printed in the 1971 facsimile, beginning "So through the evening, through the violet air" [date]. The bells that "kept the hours" recall St Mary Woolnoth, which "kept the hours" in Part I [line]; "violet light" recalls the "violet hour" of Part III [lines]; "empty cisterns and exhausted wells" may recall Jeremiah's "broken cisterns, that can hold no water" (Jeremiah 2:13).

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "printed in the 1971 facsimile of the drafts among the poems that fed them": "fed" is a metaphor, and the clause is hard to parse.
- "that is a commentators' echo, not one of Eliot's notes": the formula; say it plainly.
Proposed text:
These lines rework an earlier fragment among Eliot's papers, beginning "So through the evening, through the violet air," which is printed in the 1971 facsimile of the drafts. The woman fiddling on her hair, the bats with baby faces, the towers upside down, the bells and the voices from cisterns and wells are already there. The bells that "kept the hours" repeat line 67, where Saint Mary Woolnoth kept them; the "violet hour" of lines 215 and 220 becomes "violet light." Commentators hear Jeremiah 2:13, "broken cisterns, that can hold no water," in "empty cisterns and exhausted wells"; Eliot's notes do not mention it.

**After** (title: From an earlier fragment)

These lines rework an earlier fragment among Eliot's papers, beginning "So through the evening, through the violet air," which is printed in the 1971 facsimile of the drafts. The woman fiddling on her hair, the bats with baby faces, the towers upside down, the bells and the voices from cisterns and wells are already there. The bells that "kept the hours" repeat line 67, where Saint Mary Woolnoth kept them; the "violet hour" of lines 215 and 220 becomes "violet light." Commentators hear Jeremiah 2:13, "broken cisterns, that can hold no water," in "empty cisterns and exhausted wells"; Eliot's notes do not mention it.

### gloss:chapel: The Chapel Perilous

**Before**

Weston's chapter "The Perilous Chapel" collects the episode in which the questing knight must spend a night in a chapel in a waste place where a dead body lies on the altar, a black hand puts out the candle, and voices and storms assail him; survive it, and he is fit for the Grail. Eliot's chapel is worse than perilous: it is empty, windowless, the door swinging, "Dry bones can harm no one," the test already over. Then a cock on the rooftree crows "Co co rico," in French, the cock that crowed for Peter's denial and the "bird of dawning" that sends ghosts back to their graves in the first scene of <i>Hamlet</i>. Lightning, "a damp gust / Bringing rain." Three words. It is the first rain in the poem since the shower over the Starnbergersee at line 9.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Weston, chapter XIII, "The Perilous Chapel", confirmed: "sometimes there is a Dead Body laid on the altar; sometimes a Black Hand extinguishes the tapers; there are strange and threatening voices"; Gawain "is overtaken by a terrible storm" and takes shelter; the black hand comes through the window and puts out the taper "while a voice makes lamentation loud and dire". The summary is accurate. "survive it, and he is fit for the Grail" compresses Weston's argument that the chapel is the test of initiation; acceptable.
- "Eliot's chapel is worse than perilous ... the test already over" is a reading (a common one); mark it.
- "Co co rico" as the French cock-crow (cocorico): right.
- "the cock that crowed for Peter's denial": Matthew 26:74–75 ("immediately the cock crew"); Mark 14:72 has it twice. "the 'bird of dawning' that sends ghosts back to their graves in the first scene of Hamlet": Hamlet I.i, Horatio, "The cock, that is the trumpet to the morn"; Marcellus, "The bird of dawning singeth all night long; / And then, they say, no spirit dare stir abroad". Both are commentators' associations, not Eliot's notes; say so.
- "'a damp gust / Bringing rain.' Three words.": "Bringing rain" is two words; the whole is five. The count is wrong, and the water path (395) says "two words". Drop the counting.
- "the first rain in the poem since the shower over the Starnbergersee at line 9": line 9 is "With a shower of rain" (the lake is line 8); line 4 has "spring rain". So "first rain since line 9" is right. But the poem does not say the rain falls: "Bringing rain", then "the limp leaves / Waited for rain" (396–397) and "the arid plain" (425). Say that.
Missing: that the rain is brought, not shown falling.
Proposed text:
Weston's chapter XIII, "The Perilous Chapel", gathers the adventure in which the hero, sheltering in a chapel from a storm, finds a dead body on the altar or sees a black hand put out the tapers and hears "strange and threatening voices"; she reads it as a test of initiation. Eliot's chapel is empty and windowless, with a swinging door and dry bones. "Co co rico" is the French cock-crow. Commentators set beside it the cock that crowed at Peter's denial (Matthew 26:74) and the cock of <i>Hamlet</i> I.i, "the trumpet to the morn", at whose crowing "no spirit dare stir abroad". Then "a damp gust / Bringing rain": the first rain since the shower of line 9. The poem does not say that it falls; at line 397 the leaves are still waiting.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "The Chapel Perilous": Weston's and Eliot's term. Keep.
- The Weston summary is clear and useful; "survive it, and he is fit for the Grail" is brisk but true. Keep.
- "Eliot's chapel is worse than perilous: it is empty, windowless, the door swinging, "Dry bones can harm no one," the test already over." "Worse than perilous" is a turn; "the test already over" is a reading given as fact, and not obviously what the lines say. Say: in the poem the chapel is empty and nothing happens in it.
- "the cock that crowed for Peter's denial and the "bird of dawning" that sends ghosts back to their graves in the first scene of Hamlet." Two associations given with "the cock that ...", as if settled. Say "has been heard as".
- "Lightning, "a damp gust / Bringing rain." Three words." The counting fragment, and the count disagrees with path:water/395 ("Two words."). Cut.
- "It is the first rain in the poem since the shower over the Starnbergersee at line 9." Useful. Keep.
Questions for the scholar: Does rain fall at 395? Lines 396 to 398 have the leaves still waiting for it and 425 has "the arid plain"; way:Water at last says "Everything after that is wet." Which is it? Is the summary of Weston's chapter (dead body on the altar, black hand, storms) accurate to chapter XIII?
Proposed text:
Weston's chapter "The Perilous Chapel" gathers the episode in which the knight must pass a night in a chapel in a waste place: a dead body lies on the altar, a black hand puts out the candle, voices and a storm assail him; if he survives he may go on to the Grail. In the poem the chapel is empty and nothing happens in it: "Dry bones can harm no one." The cock crows "Co co rico," the French spelling of the sound; readers have heard in it the cock that crowed at Peter's denial and the "bird of dawning" of the first scene of <i>Hamlet</i>. The "damp gust / Bringing rain" at line 395 is the first rain in the poem since the shower at line 9.

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- Weston chapter XIII summary: accurate ("sometimes there is a Dead Body laid on the altar; sometimes a Black Hand extinguishes the tapers; there are strange and threatening voices").
- Hamlet I.i: the quoted words are right, but "at whose crowing 'no spirit dare stir abroad'" runs two passages together. "No spirit dare stir abroad" is Marcellus on the nights before Christmas, when "the bird of dawning singeth all night long"; what happens in the scene is that the ghost "faded on the crowing of the cock", and Horatio calls the cock "the trumpet to the morn". Small, but a reader who opens the scene will see the difference.
Proposed text:
[replace the Hamlet clause] ... and the cock of <i>Hamlet</i> I.i, "the trumpet to the morn", at whose crowing the ghost fades.

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "Weston's chapter ... gathers the adventure in which": "gathers" is odd with "adventure"; she collects versions of it.
- "and nothing happens in it": a reading (the scholar marked "the test already over" as one), stated as fact; and something does happen, the cock crows. Cut the clause; the quotation carries the point.
- "Commentators have set beside it": the idiom; "compare".
- "Then lightning and "a damp gust / Bringing rain," the first rain ...": begins without a verb.
Proposed text:
Weston's chapter "The Perilous Chapel" collects the versions of an adventure in which the knight, sheltering in a chapel in a waste place, finds a dead body on the altar, or sees a black hand put out the candle and hears "strange and threatening voices"; she reads it as a test of initiation on the way to the Grail. In the poem the chapel is empty, without windows, its door swinging: "Dry bones can harm no one." "Co co rico" is the French spelling of a cock-crow. Commentators compare the cock that crowed at Peter's denial (Matthew 26:74) and the cock of <i>Hamlet</i> I.i, at whose crowing "no spirit dare stir abroad." The "damp gust / Bringing rain" is the first rain in the poem since the shower of line 9. The poem does not say that it falls; at line 397 the leaves are still waiting for it.

**After**

Weston's chapter "The Perilous Chapel" collects the versions of an adventure in which the knight, sheltering in a chapel in a waste place, finds a dead body on the altar, or sees a black hand put out the candle and hears "strange and threatening voices"; she reads it as a test of initiation on the way to the Grail. In the poem the chapel is empty, without windows, its door swinging: "Dry bones can harm no one." "Co co rico" is the French spelling of a cock-crow. Commentators compare the cock that crowed at Peter's denial (Matthew 26:74) and the cock of <i>Hamlet</i> I.i, "the trumpet to the morn," at whose crowing the ghost fades. The "damp gust / Bringing rain" is the first rain in the poem since the shower of line 9. The poem does not say that it falls; at line 397 the leaves are still waiting for it.

### gloss:ganga: The river and the mountain

**Before**

Ganga is the Ganges; Himavant, "the snowy," is the Himalaya, the mountain father of the goddess Ganga in the Puranas. The scene moves in a line from the chapel in the mountains to the Indian monsoon, the whole subcontinent crouched "humped in silence" waiting for the rains, and then the thunder speaks, in the syllable of a language Eliot had studied at Harvard for two years under Charles Lanman and never pretended to command. In <i>After Strange Gods</i> (1934) he said the Sanskrit and the year of Patañjali's metaphysics had left him "in a state of enlightened mystification."

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Ganga is the Ganges; Himavant (Himavat, "having snow") is the personified Himalaya, and in the Ramayana (Bala Kanda) and the Puranas he is the father of Ganga and of Parvati. "the mountain father of the goddess Ganga in the Puranas" is right.
- "a language Eliot had studied at Harvard for two years under Charles Lanman": confirmed by Eliot himself, After Strange Gods (1934): "Two years spent in the study of Sanskrit under Charles Lanman, and a year in the mazes of Patanjali's metaphysics under the guidance of James Woods, left me in a state of enlightened mystification." The gloss quotes the last phrase correctly. Note the year of Patanjali was under Woods, not Lanman; the gloss does not say otherwise but could be read so.
- "never pretended to command" is the site's gloss on that sentence; "the whole subcontinent crouched" is the voice.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
Ganga is the Ganges; Himavant is the Himalaya, in the Ramayana and the Puranas the mountain who is Ganga's father. The scene moves from the chapel to India before the monsoon breaks. Eliot studied Sanskrit at Harvard for two years (1911–13) under Charles Lanman. In <i>After Strange Gods</i> (1934) he wrote that those years, and a year of "Patanjali's metaphysics" under James Woods, "left me in a state of enlightened mystification".

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "The river and the mountain": plain. Keep. First sentence: plain and useful. Keep.
- "The scene moves in a line from the chapel in the mountains to the Indian monsoon, the whole subcontinent crouched "humped in silence" waiting for the rains, and then the thunder speaks, in the syllable of a language Eliot had studied at Harvard for two years under Charles Lanman and never pretended to command." One sentence doing four jobs. "The whole subcontinent crouched" enlarges the poem's "The jungle crouched"; "never pretended to command" is a phrase standing in for a fact. Split it and drop the enlargement.
- The <i>After Strange Gods</i> sentence: factual, sourced, a phrase quoted. Keep.
Questions for the scholar: Is "Himavant, 'the snowy'" right, and does the line need the Puranas? The library record says Lanman 1911 to 1913; "two years" is consistent.
Proposed text:
Ganga is the Ganges; Himavant, "the snowy," is the Himalaya, in the Puranas the mountain father of the goddess Ganga. The scene moves from the chapel in the mountains to India before the monsoon. Then the thunder speaks, in Sanskrit, which Eliot studied for two years at Harvard under Charles Lanman. In <i>After Strange Gods</i> (1934) he said the Sanskrit and a year of Patañjali's metaphysics had left him "in a state of enlightened mystification."

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- After Strange Gods: "Two years spent in the study of Sanskrit under Charles Lanman, and a year in the mazes of Patanjali's metaphysics under the guidance of James Woods, left me in a state of enlightened mystification." The rewrite's "Patañjali's philosophy" paraphrases "metaphysics"; either is fine, but since the sentence ends in a quotation, "metaphysics" would be closer.
Proposed text: none

**After**

Ganga is the Ganges; Himavant, "the snowy," is the Himalaya, in the Ramayana and the Puranas the mountain who is the goddess Ganga's father. The scene moves from the chapel in the mountains to India before the monsoon breaks. Then the thunder speaks, in Sanskrit, which Eliot studied for two years at Harvard, 1911 to 1913, under Charles Lanman. In <i>After Strange Gods</i> (1934) he wrote that those years, and a year of Patañjali's metaphysics under James Woods, "left me in a state of enlightened mystification."

### gloss:da: What the thunder said

**Before**

<i>Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad</i> 5.2, paraphrased here from the Sanskrit; Eliot's note gives Deussen's German translation as his source. The thunder says one syllable and each hearer understands the instruction he most needs. Eliot changes the order to Datta, Dayadhvam, Damyata (give, sympathise, control), and each word is followed by a confession in the poem's own voice of how the instruction has been failed. The section's title, and the poem's ending, come from a fable about hearing what you are able to hear.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- The passage is Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad V.2 (the second Brāhmaṇa of the fifth Adhyāya). Confirmed in Max Müller's translation (Sacred Books of the East, vol. XV, 1884): "The threefold descendants of Prajâpati, gods, men, and Asuras (evil spirits), dwelt as Brahmakârins (students) with their father Prajâpati. Having finished their studentship the gods said: 'Tell us (something), Sir.' He told them the syllable Da. Then he said: 'Did you understand?' They said: 'We did understand. You told us "Dâmyata," Be subdued.' ... men ... "Datta," Give ... Asuras ... "Dayadham," Be merciful ... The divine voice of thunder repeats the same, Da Da Da, that is, Be subdued, Give, Be merciful." Eliot's note says "5, 1", in 1922 and still in Poems 1909–1925; the note's number is a slip. The order in the Upanishad (gods, men, demons: control, give, sympathise) and Eliot's reordering are as the gloss says.
- "Eliot's note gives Deussen's German translation as his source" is not what the note says. The note names the Upanishad as where "the fable of the meaning of the Thunder is found" and adds "A translation is found in Deussen's Sechzig Upanishads des Veda, p. 489." Deussen is offered as a translation, not as the source.
- "paraphrased here from the Sanskrit": by whom? If the site made the paraphrase, say so, or give Müller.
- "each hearer understands the instruction he most needs": that is the traditional commentary's reading (Śaṅkara: the gods self-indulgent, men greedy, the demons cruel); it is not in the text itself. Attribute or soften.
- "each word is followed by a confession ... of how the instruction has been failed": a common reading; mark it.
- "The section's title, and the poem's ending, come from a fable about hearing what you are able to hear": the bow.
Missing: the slip "5, 1" for V.2; Müller as the English source of the wording.
Proposed text:
<i>Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad</i> V.2. Eliot's note says "5, 1"; the fable is the second section of the fifth chapter. Gods, men and demons ask Prajāpati for teaching. To each he says "Da", and each hears a different word: the gods <i>dāmyata</i>, restrain yourselves; men <i>datta</i>, give; the demons <i>dayadhvam</i>, be compassionate. "The divine voice of thunder repeats the same, Da Da Da" (Max Müller's translation, 1884). The commentators' explanation, that each hears the command it most needs, is not in the text. Eliot's note glosses the three as "Give, sympathise, control" and adds that a translation is in Deussen's <i>Sechzig Upanishads des Veda</i> (1897), p. 489. The poem takes the words in the order <i>datta, dayadhvam, damyata</i>, and what follows each has usually been read as an admission of failure.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "What the thunder said": the section's own title; as a note title it is fine.
- The first two sentences are plain and useful. Keep.
- "each word is followed by a confession in the poem's own voice of how the instruction has been failed." A reading stated as fact; "confession" decides for me. Say each word is followed by a passage that responds to it, and that in the usual reading each instruction is shown not kept.
- "The section's title, and the poem's ending, come from a fable about hearing what you are able to hear." An aphorism to end on. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: Eliot's note says "5, 1"; the gloss and the library record say 5.2. Which is right, and should the note say that Eliot's number differs? The note says Eliot "changes the order" but not from what; the quote box gives it (gods, men, demons: restrain, give, be compassionate), so the body could say so in a clause.
Proposed text:
<i>Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad</i> 5.2, paraphrased here from the Sanskrit; Eliot's note (which numbers it 5, 1) gives Deussen's German translation as his source. The thunder says one syllable, and each of the three hearers takes it as the instruction meant for him: the gods hear "restrain yourselves," the men "give," the demons "be compassionate." Eliot puts the words in the order Datta, Dayadhvam, Damyata (give, sympathise, control), and follows each with a passage in the poem's own voice; in the usual reading each passage shows the instruction not kept.

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- Müller, Sacred Books of the East XV (1884), Bṛhadāraṇyaka V.2.3: "The divine voice of thunder repeats the same, Da Da Da, that is, Be subdued, Give, Be merciful." The rewrite's quotation is verbatim as far as it goes. The 1925 Poems keeps Eliot's "5, i"; the slip is his.
Proposed text: none

**After**

<i>Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad</i> V.2, given above in paraphrase; Eliot's note numbers it "5, 1." Gods, men and demons ask their father Prajāpati for teaching. To each he says "Da," and each hears a different word in it: the gods <i>dāmyata</i>, restrain yourselves; the men <i>datta</i>, give; the demons <i>dayadhvam</i>, be compassionate. "The divine voice of thunder repeats the same, Da Da Da" (Max Müller's translation, 1884). Eliot's note glosses the three as "Give, sympathise, control" and points to Deussen's German translation of 1897. The poem takes the words in the order <i>datta, dayadhvam, damyata</i> and follows each with a passage in its own voice; in the usual reading each passage admits that the command was not kept.

### gloss:datta: Give

**Before**

The one thing given was "The awful daring of a moment's surrender / Which an age of prudence can never retract," and by that alone "we have existed." It will not appear in the obituaries, nor in "memories draped by the beneficent spider" (Flamineo in Webster's <i>White Devil</i>, Act V scene vi, on how quickly widows remarry, quoted in the note), nor in the will read out by "the lean solicitor" after the seals are broken. This is the poem's one statement about what a life is for, and it is made in the vocabulary of probate.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Webster, The White Devil, V.vi confirmed (Gutenberg text): after the stage direction "Flamineo riseth", "O men, / That lie upon your death-beds, and are haunted / With howling wives! ne'er trust them; they'll re-marry / Ere the worm pierce your winding-sheet, ere the spider / Make a thin curtain for your epitaphs." Act, scene, speaker and gist ("how quickly widows remarry") are right; Eliot's note gives "V, vi".
- "the will read out by 'the lean solicitor' after the seals are broken": a reading of line 409; fair, but say "the will opened after a death" rather than "read out".
- "This is the poem's one statement about what a life is for, and it is made in the vocabulary of probate": the site's reading, and the bow. Cut or mark.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
"The awful daring of a moment's surrender" is the one thing given, and "by this, and this only, we have existed". It is not in the obituaries, nor in "memories draped by the beneficent spider", which Eliot's note refers to Flamineo in Webster's <i>The White Devil</i>, V.vi, on widows who remarry "ere the spider / Make a thin curtain for your epitaphs", nor "under seals broken by the lean solicitor", the will opened after a death.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "Give": keep.
- The first two sentences walk me through lines 403 to 410 and put the Webster gloss where it is needed. Keep; this is the kind of note I want.
- "This is the poem's one statement about what a life is for, and it is made in the vocabulary of probate." The bow: a superlative ("the poem's one statement") and a turned phrase ("the vocabulary of probate"). Cut.
Questions for the scholar: "the will read out by 'the lean solicitor'": the poem says "seals broken by the lean solicitor"; is "will" the settled reading? Fine if so.
Proposed text: the first two sentences as they stand; nothing after "after the seals are broken."

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- The reader asked whether "the will" is the settled reading of "seals broken by the lean solicitor". It is the usual one (Southam and others); "the will opened after a death" is properly hedged.
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- The second sentence nests the Webster citation inside a "nor ... nor ... nor" list; I lose the thread by the third "nor". Split it.
Proposed text:
The one thing given is "The awful daring of a moment's surrender / Which an age of prudence can never retract," and "by this, and this only, we have existed." It is not in the obituaries, nor in "memories draped by the beneficent spider," nor "under seals broken by the lean solicitor," the will opened after a death. For the spider Eliot's note quotes Flamineo in Webster's <i>The White Devil</i>, Act V scene vi, on widows who remarry "ere the spider / Make a thin curtain for your epitaphs."

**After**

The one thing given is "The awful daring of a moment's surrender / Which an age of prudence can never retract," and "by this, and this only, we have existed." It is not in the obituaries, nor in "memories draped by the beneficent spider," nor "under seals broken by the lean solicitor," the will opened after a death. For the spider Eliot's note quotes Flamineo in Webster's <i>The White Devil</i>, Act V scene vi, on widows who remarry "ere the spider / Make a thin curtain for your epitaphs."

### gloss:dayadhvam: Sympathise: Ugolino and Bradley

**Before** (title: Sympathise: Ugolino's tower, and Bradley's circle)

<i>Inferno</i> XXXIII: Count Ugolino, locked in a tower at Pisa with his sons and grandsons to starve, hears the door nailed shut. Eliot's note adds a page of F. H. Bradley's <i>Appearance and Reality</i> (1893), the philosopher on whom he wrote his Harvard doctoral thesis: "My external sensations are no less private to myself than are my thoughts or my feelings . . . the whole world for each is peculiar and private to that soul." Each of us in a prison, thinking of a key, and the thinking of the key is what confirms the prison. "A broken Coriolanus," Shakespeare's general who could not bend, is revived "for a moment" by "aetherial rumours" at nightfall, and only for a moment. Sympathy is the instruction; solitude is the report.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Inferno XXXIII.46–48 confirmed (Wikisource, Petrocchi text): "e io senti' chiavar l'uscio di sotto / a l'orribile torre; ond'io guardai / nel viso a' mie' figliuoi sanza far motto." Eliot's note quotes the older reading "ed io sentii ... all'orribile torre". "chiavar" can mean to nail up (chiavo, nail) or to lock (chiave, key); translators divide, and Eliot's "key" takes the second. The gloss's "nailing up" is one reading; give both.
- Ugolino was imprisoned in 1288 with two sons and two grandsons; Dante calls them all "figliuoi". "sons and grandsons" is right.
- Bradley, Appearance and Reality (1893), p. 346 confirmed: the words Eliot quotes are on that page in the archive.org copy ("My external sensations are no less private to myself than are my thoughts or my feelings. In either case my experience falls within my own circle, a circle closed on the outside; and, with all its elements alike, every sphere is opaque to the others which surround it ... In brief, regarded as an existence which appears in a soul, the whole world for each is peculiar and private to that soul"). "the philosopher on whom he wrote his Harvard doctoral thesis": right (completed 1916, published 1964 as Knowledge and Experience in the Philosophy of F. H. Bradley).
- "A broken Coriolanus, Shakespeare's general who could not bend": a gloss; fine.
- "Sympathy is the instruction; solitude is the report": the bow.
Missing: the two senses of "chiavar".
Proposed text:
<i>Inferno</i> XXXIII.46–48: Ugolino, shut in the tower at Pisa with his sons and grandsons to starve, hears the door below "chiavar", nailed up or locked; the verb allows both, and Eliot's "key" takes the second. Eliot's note adds F. H. Bradley, <i>Appearance and Reality</i> (1893), p. 346, on experience as "a circle closed on the outside"; Eliot's Harvard dissertation (1916) was on Bradley. The key is thought of, not turned. "A broken Coriolanus" is Shakespeare's general, revived "for a moment" by "aetherial rumours".

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "Sympathise: Ugolino's tower, and Bradley's circle": crowded but it names both sources. "Sympathise: Ugolino and Bradley" is lighter.
- The Ugolino sentence and the Bradley sentence: plain, sourced. Keep.
- "Each of us in a prison, thinking of a key, and the thinking of the key is what confirms the prison." A verbless restatement of lines 414 and 415 in the poem's own rhythm. It adds nothing I cannot read myself. Cut, or make it one sentence that connects Bradley to the lines.
- ""A broken Coriolanus," Shakespeare's general who could not bend, is revived "for a moment" by "aetherial rumours" at nightfall, and only for a moment." "And only for a moment" repeats the quotation for effect. "Who could not bend" is so compressed it is a riddle for someone who does not know the play.
- "Sympathy is the instruction; solitude is the report." Named in the standard. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: What should a reader be told about Coriolanus in one clause so that "broken" makes sense? Is "aetherial rumours" glossed anywhere, or left alone?
Proposed text: (first two sentences unchanged, then:)
Bradley's closed circle is the "prison" of lines 414 and 415. Coriolanus is Shakespeare's Roman general, [one plain clause on why "broken"]; here he is revived only "for a moment," by "aetherial rumours," at nightfall.

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
