- Coriolanus: "banished by the city he had fought for and unable to bend to it" is half right. He is banished (III.iii) because he will not bend to the people, and he leads the Volscians against Rome; but in V.iii he does bend, to his mother's plea, spares the city, and is killed for it by Aufidius's conspirators (V.vi). "Unable to bend" contradicts the end of the play. "Broken" fits either moment; the clause should not decide it wrongly.
- "aetherial rumours" (the reader's question): Eliot gives no note; the commentaries leave it alone or take "rumours" in the old sense of murmurs. Nothing to add.
- "Bradley's closed circle is the 'prison' of lines 414 and 415": stated as fact, but Eliot's note makes the pairing, so it can stand; "Eliot's note sets Bradley's closed circle beside the 'prison'" would be exact.
Proposed text:
[replace the Coriolanus sentence] Coriolanus is Shakespeare's Roman general, banished from Rome for his contempt of its people; he leads its enemies against it, yields at last to his mother's plea to spare it, and is killed for yielding. "A broken Coriolanus" is revived here only "for a moment," by "aetherial rumours," at nightfall.

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "adds a page of F. H. Bradley's Appearance and Reality (1893), the philosopher on whom he wrote his Harvard dissertation": as written, "the philosopher" is in apposition to the book. Reorder.
Proposed text (the second sentence; the rest unchanged):
Eliot's note adds a page of <i>Appearance and Reality</i> (1893) by F. H. Bradley, on whom Eliot wrote his Harvard dissertation: "My external sensations are no less private to myself than are my thoughts or my feelings . . . the whole world for each is peculiar and private to that soul."

**After** (title: Sympathise: Ugolino and Bradley)

<i>Inferno</i> XXXIII, 46 to 48: Count Ugolino, shut in a tower at Pisa with his sons and grandsons to starve, hears the door below "chiavar," which can mean nailed up or locked; Eliot's "key" takes the second sense. Eliot's note adds a page of <i>Appearance and Reality</i> (1893) by F. H. Bradley, on whom Eliot wrote his Harvard dissertation: "My external sensations are no less private to myself than are my thoughts or my feelings . . . the whole world for each is peculiar and private to that soul." Eliot's note sets Bradley's closed circle beside the "prison" of lines 414 and 415, where the key is thought of, not turned. Coriolanus is Shakespeare's Roman general, banished from Rome for his contempt of its people; he leads its enemies against it, yields at last to his mother's plea to spare it, and is killed for yielding. "A broken Coriolanus" is revived here only "for a moment," by "aetherial rumours," at nightfall.

### gloss:damyata: Control

**Before** (title: Control: a boat off Cape Ann)

The third instruction gets the poem's only memory of things going well: a sailing boat answering "Gaily, to the hand expert with sail and oar," the sea calm. Eliot had sailed off Gloucester and Cape Ann as a boy, and the Dry Salvages of his later poem lie off that coast. Then the grammar turns conditional: "your heart would have responded / Gaily, when invited." Would have. The most intimate address in the poem, to a "you" who is never named, is a sentence about what did not happen.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "the poem's only memory of things going well": a reading (the hyacinth garden is another candidate); say "the one passage in which something answers as it should", or mark it.
- "Eliot had sailed off Gloucester and Cape Ann as a boy, and the Dry Salvages of his later poem lie off that coast": the Eliots summered at Eastern Point, Gloucester, and he sailed there; the Dry Salvages are off Cape Ann. Right. "as a boy" and "the summers of his boyhood" (path eliot/419) are fair; he sailed there into his twenties too.
- "Would have." The fragment is the voice; the observation (the sentence is conditional) is right.
Missing: the draft read "your heart responded"; the conditional "would have responded" is Eliot's revision, and two lines after "To controlling hands" were cut (facsimile, typescript of Part V). This is the fact a reader of these lines wants and it is missing. I could not reach the facsimile to confirm the words; add once confirmed (Ricks and McCue's textual notes will have it).
Proposed text:
The third command is answered by a boat that "responded / Gaily, to the hand expert with sail and oar". Eliot sailed off Gloucester and Cape Ann in his youth; the Dry Salvages of his later poem lie off that coast. Then the tense changes: "your heart would have responded". [If confirmed against the facsimile: The typescript read "your heart responded"; the conditional is Eliot's revision.]

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title, "Control: a boat off Cape Ann": puts a biographical guess in the title as if it were in the poem. "Control" is enough.
- "The third instruction gets the poem's only memory of things going well". "Gets" is chatty; "the poem's only memory of things going well" is a claim I could argue with (the hyacinth garden, the sled) and it tells me how to feel about the lines.
- "Eliot had sailed off Gloucester and Cape Ann as a boy, and the Dry Salvages of his later poem lie off that coast." The first half is worth having if it is marked as a reading; the Dry Salvages is an aside about another poem.
- "Would have." Named in the standard. Cut.
- "The most intimate address in the poem, to a "you" who is never named, is a sentence about what did not happen." A superlative and a bow. The plain part, that the "you" is not named and the sentence is conditional, takes one line.
Questions for the scholar: Who connects this boat with Eliot's boyhood sailing? A letter, or a biographer's reading?
Proposed text:
Title: Control
The third word is answered by a boat under control: "The boat responded / Gaily, to the hand expert with sail and oar," on a calm sea. Eliot sailed off Cape Ann, Massachusetts, as a boy, and the boat has been read as a memory of it [whose reading]. The sentence that follows is conditional: "your heart would have responded / Gaily, when invited." The "you" is not named.

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- The reader asked who connects the boat with Eliot's sailing. The sailing is documented (Eliot's letters from Gloucester; Crawford, Young Eliot); the link to line 419 is the commentators', made by way of "The Dry Salvages". "Generally taken as a memory of it" is right.
Proposed text: none

**After** (title: Control)

The third word is answered by a boat under control: "The boat responded / Gaily, to the hand expert with sail and oar," on a calm sea. Eliot sailed off Gloucester and Cape Ann, Massachusetts, in the summers of his youth, and the boat is generally taken as a memory of it; the Dry Salvages of his later poem lie off that coast. The sentence that follows is conditional, "your heart would have responded / Gaily, when invited," and the "you" is not named.

### gloss:fisher-king: The Fisher King, and Hezekiah

**Before**

Eliot's note sends you to Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, the wounded keeper of the Grail who fishes because he can do nothing else, while his land lies waste behind him. "Shall I at least set my lands in order?" is Isaiah 38:1, the prophet's message to the dying king Hezekiah, who turns his face to the wall and weeps and is given fifteen more years. It is also what a man does with his papers. From here to the end the poem stops narrating and starts stacking.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, the wounded keeper of the Grail who fishes because he can do nothing else": this is Chrétien's explanation, and Weston's chapter IX rejects it. Weston: in Chrétien and Wolfram "the name is connected with his partiality for fishing, an obviously post hoc addition"; the redactors "were at pains to obscure" the origin "by the introduction of the obviously post hoc 'motif' ... that he was called the Fisher King because of his devotion to the pastime of fishing". Her argument is that the title comes from the fish as an ancient life-symbol. A note that sends the reader to Weston's chapter should not summarise it as saying what it denies.
- "while his land lies waste behind him": Weston's chapter II ("The Task of the Hero") and V connect the king's wound with the land's waste; fine.
- Isaiah 38:1 confirmed: "Thus saith the LORD, Set thine house in order: for thou shalt die, and not live." Hezekiah "turned his face toward the wall" (38:2), "wept sore" (38:3), and was given fifteen years (38:5). The poem says "lands", not "house"; write "echoes", and the echo is a commentators' identification (Southam and others), not one of Eliot's notes.
- "It is also what a man does with his papers" and "From here to the end the poem stops narrating and starts stacking" are the voice.
Missing: what Weston's chapter actually argues.
Proposed text:
Eliot's note sends the reader to Weston's chapter IX, "The Fisher King". Weston argues that the title comes from the fish as an ancient symbol of life, and dismisses the romances' explanation, that the wounded king fishes for pastime because he cannot ride, as "an obviously post hoc addition". Line 426 echoes Isaiah 38:1, the prophet's message to the dying king Hezekiah: "Set thine house in order: for thou shalt die, and not live." Hezekiah turned his face to the wall, wept, and was given fifteen more years. The poem says "lands", not "house", and gets no answer.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "The Fisher King, and Hezekiah": keep.
- "Eliot's note sends you to Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, the wounded keeper of the Grail who fishes because he can do nothing else, while his land lies waste behind him." "Sends you" is chatty; the rest is useful. "Because he can do nothing else" compresses the romance (his wound keeps him from riding); fine if the scholar agrees.
- The Isaiah sentence: plain, sourced, and it tells me the story I need. Keep. "Is Isaiah 38:1" should be "recalls": Eliot's line is a question, Isaiah's is a command.
- "It is also what a man does with his papers." A flourish, and I do not know what it means (a dying man's affairs? Eliot's own manuscript?). Cut.
- "From here to the end the poem stops narrating and starts stacking." Named in the standard. Cut.
- Not mine to change: the quote box gives a verse of Isaiah under a source field that says "weston". The editor should know the box will say Weston over Isaiah.
Questions for the scholar: none beyond the above.
Proposed text:
Eliot's note points to Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, the wounded keeper of the Grail, who fishes while his land lies waste. "Shall I at least set my lands in order?" recalls Isaiah 38:1, the prophet's message to the dying king Hezekiah: "Set thine house in order: for thou shalt die, and not live." Hezekiah turns his face to the wall and weeps, and is given fifteen more years.

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- Weston chapter IX as now summarised is what she argues ("an obviously post hoc addition", twice). Isaiah 38:1–5 as before.
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "as "an obviously post hoc addition"": Weston's words, but a reader who does not know "post hoc" gets nothing from them. Paraphrase; the scholar may add her words after it if wanted.
- "The echo is the commentators', not one of Eliot's notes; the poem says "lands," not "house," and gets no answer." The formula again, and it is the question, not the poem, that gets no answer.
Proposed text:
Eliot's note points to Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, the wounded keeper of the Grail whose land lies waste with him. Weston argues that his title comes from the fish as an ancient symbol of life, and dismisses the romances' own explanation, that the king fishes for pastime because his wound keeps him from riding, as a later invention. "Shall I at least set my lands in order?" echoes Isaiah 38:1, the prophet's message to the dying king Hezekiah: "Set thine house in order: for thou shalt die, and not live." Hezekiah turned his face to the wall and wept, and was given fifteen more years. Eliot's notes do not mention Isaiah; the poem says "lands," not "house," and the question gets no answer.

**After**

Eliot's note points to Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, the wounded keeper of the Grail whose land lies waste with him. Weston argues that his title comes from the fish as an ancient symbol of life, and dismisses the romances' own explanation, that the king fishes for pastime because his wound keeps him from riding, as a later invention. "Shall I at least set my lands in order?" echoes Isaiah 38:1, the prophet's message to the dying king Hezekiah: "Set thine house in order: for thou shalt die, and not live." Hezekiah turned his face to the wall and wept, and was given fifteen more years. Eliot's notes do not mention Isaiah; the poem says "lands," not "house," and the question gets no answer.

### gloss:fragments: The last eight lines

**Before** (title: The last eight lines, one by one)

<b>London Bridge is falling down falling down falling down.</b> The nursery rhyme, whose verses try one material after another (wood and clay, bricks and mortar, iron and steel, silver and gold) and whose oldest versions end with setting a watchman, or, in the folklorists' reading that Frazer would have relished, burying someone in the foundations. The bridge the commuters crossed at line 62.

<b>Poi s'ascose nel foco che gli affina.</b> "Then he hid himself in the fire that refines them." <i>Purgatorio</i> XXVI, 148: Arnaut Daniel, having spoken to Dante in his own Provençal (the <i>Comedy</i>'s one passage in a vernacular other than Italian), steps back into the purifying flame. He goes willingly. Eliot's note quotes the end of Arnaut's speech, from "Ara vos prec," the words Eliot had taken for the title of his 1920 collection. Set this fire against the burning of the Fire Sermon.

<b>Quando fiam ceu chelidon.</b> "When shall I become like the swallow?" From the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>, an anonymous Latin poem of the second to fourth century celebrating the eve of Venus's spring festival, whose last stanza suddenly turns on the poet: the nightingale sings, and he, silent, asks when his own spring will come, "when shall I become like the swallow, so that I may stop being silent?" The 1922 printings have "ceu"; the Latin poem has "uti," and Eliot's later editions restored it. "O swallow swallow" is Tennyson's, from <i>The Princess</i>: "O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South." Philomel's sister, still singing.

<b>Le Prince d'Aquitaine à la tour abolie.</b> The second line of Gérard de Nerval's sonnet "El Desdichado" (1854): "I am the dark one, the widower, the unconsoled, the Prince of Aquitaine with the ruined tower." The Tower is a Tarot card (XVI, struck by lightning), and Nerval's speaker, who has "twice crossed the Acheron," is another man who has been among the dead.

<b>These fragments I have shored against my ruins.</b> The poem's own line, its method confessed in a single sentence: quotations propped like timbers against a wall that is coming down.

<b>Why then Ile fit you. Hieronymo's mad againe.</b> Kyd's <i>The Spanish Tragedy</i> (c. 1587), Act IV. Hieronimo, whose son has been murdered, is asked to write an entertainment for the court and answers "Why then, I'll fit you," meaning both "I'll oblige" and "I'll pay you out"; the play he writes is in four languages, and the murderers, acting in it, are killed for real. "Hieronimo is mad again" was the subtitle on the 1615 title page. A poem in seven languages, written as revenge.

<b>Datta. Dayadhvam. Damyata.</b> The thunder's three words, now without their glosses.

<b>Shantih shantih shantih.</b> The formal ending of an Upanishad, as Eliot's note says, "the Peace which passeth understanding" (Philippians 4:7), for which the 1922 note called the English "a feeble translation" and later notes "our equivalent." Whether the last line is a benediction or a man saying "peace, peace" to himself until he can sleep, the poem does not decide, and neither should you on a first reading.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- London Bridge: the rhyme was first printed c. 1744 (Tommy Thumb's Pretty Song Book), where the materials fail and a man is set to watch; "wood and clay, bricks and mortar" are in later versions (Halliwell, 1842). "the folklorists' reading that Frazer would have relished, burying someone in the foundations" is Alice Gomme's (Traditional Games, 1894–98), taken up by the Opies; name her rather than Frazer. Line 62 confirmed ("A crowd flowed over London Bridge").
- Purgatorio XXVI confirmed: Arnaut's speech is 140–147 in Provençal, "Ara vos prec" is 145, and 148 is "Poi s'ascose nel foco che li affina" (modern text; Eliot's "gli" is an older reading). "the Comedy's one passage in a vernacular other than Italian": right. "Ara Vos Prec" (1920) right. "He goes willingly" is a reading; fine. "Set this fire against the burning of the Fire Sermon" is an instruction.
- Pervigilium Veneris: dating "second to fourth century" is the usual range. The last stanza confirmed: "illa cantat, nos tacemus. quando ver venit meum? / quando fiam uti chelidon, ut tacere desinam?" (modern texts; the 1848 edition on Wikisource reads "ut chelidon" and "ver venit novum", so "the Latin poem has 'uti'" is true of the standard text, not of every edition). "The 1922 printings have 'ceu' ... and Eliot's later editions restored it": every printing from 1922 to the 1932 American Poems 1909–1925 reads "ceu" (I checked the 1925 Poems: "Quando fiam ceu chelidon"), and so do Eliot's autograph and typescript fair copies; "uti" first appears in Collected Poems 1909–1935 (1936), and Rainey doubts the change was Eliot's (Annotated Waste Land, quoted at languagehat.com). Give the edition and the doubt.
- Tennyson confirmed: The Princess, "O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South". Swinburne's "Itylus" ("Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow") is the other text usually cited; worth a clause.
- Nerval: "El Desdichado" was first printed in Le Mousquetaire, 10 December 1853, then in Les Chimères (1854); "1854" is fine. Line 2 confirmed. The translation is fair. "The Tower is a Tarot card (XVI, struck by lightning)": the card exists, but the link to Nerval's "tour abolie" is a commentators' association (the library record rightly says "often read as"); the gloss states it as if it followed. Mark it. "twice crossed the Acheron" confirmed.
- "These fragments ... quotations propped like timbers against a wall that is coming down": the voice.
- Kyd confirmed: IV.i, Hieronimo, "Why then, I'll fit you; say no more" (Eliot gives it in the old spelling, "Why then Ile fit you"); the play is assigned "in unknown languages ... you, my lord, in Latin, I in Greek, / You in Italian, and ... in courtly French shall all her phrases be": four languages; the printed note says it was "in sundry languages"; Lorenzo and Balthazar are killed in the performance. "'Hieronimo is mad again' was the subtitle on the 1615 title page": right, "The Spanish Tragedie: or, Hieronimo is mad againe" (1615, for I. White and T. Langley), the first edition to carry it. The pun "I'll oblige / I'll pay you out" is right.
- "A poem in seven languages, written as revenge": the count depends on what is counted. The text of the poem has six (English, German, French, Italian, Latin, Sanskrit); Greek is in the epigraph; Provençal is in the notes. "written as revenge" is unsupported by anything Eliot said. Cut both.
- Shantih: Philippians 4:7 ("the peace of God, which passeth all understanding"); Eliot's words are the Anglican blessing's. "the 1922 note called the English 'a feeble translation' and later notes 'our equivalent'": right, and the change is already in Poems 1909–1925 (1925): "'The Peace which passeth understanding' is our equivalent to this word." Give the date. "neither should you on a first reading": an instruction to the reader.
Missing: the edition of the "uti" change; Gomme's name; the 1925 date for the revised note.
Proposed text:
[Pervigilium paragraph] <b>Quando fiam ceu chelidon.</b> "When shall I become like the swallow?" From the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>, an anonymous Latin poem of the second to fourth century for the eve of Venus's spring festival. Its last stanza turns from the nightingale to the poet's own silence: "illa cantat, nos tacemus. quando ver venit meum? / quando fiam uti chelidon, ut tacere desinam?" Every printing from 1922 to 1932, and Eliot's fair copies, read "ceu" (also "like"); "uti" first appears in <i>Collected Poems 1909–1935</i> (1936), and Rainey doubts the change was Eliot's. "O swallow swallow" is Tennyson's, from <i>The Princess</i> ("O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South"); Swinburne's "Itylus" ("Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow") is also cited. Eliot's note adds "Cf. Philomela in Parts II and III."

[Nerval paragraph] <b>Le Prince d'Aquitaine à la tour abolie.</b> The second line of Nerval's sonnet "El Desdichado" (first printed December 1853; in <i>Les Chimères</i>, 1854): "I am the dark one, the widower, the unconsoled, the Prince of Aquitaine with the ruined tower." Some commentators read the tower with the Tarot's Tower, struck by lightning; Nerval does not say so. The sonnet's speaker has "twice crossed the Acheron".

[Kyd paragraph] <b>Why then Ile fit you. Hieronymo's mad againe.</b> Kyd's <i>The Spanish Tragedy</i> (c. 1587), IV.i. Hieronimo, whose son has been murdered, is asked by the murderers for a court entertainment and answers "Why then, I'll fit you": suit you, and punish you. The play he gives them is acted in four languages, Latin, Greek, Italian and French, and in it the murderers are stabbed in earnest. "Hieronimo is mad againe" is the subtitle first printed on the title page of the 1615 quarto. Six languages stand in the text of <i>The Waste Land</i> (English, German, French, Italian, Latin, Sanskrit), with Greek in the epigraph and Provençal in the notes.

[Shantih paragraph] <b>Shantih shantih shantih.</b> Eliot's note: "Repeated as here, a formal ending to an Upanishad." "The Peace which passeth understanding" is Philippians 4:7 in the words of the Anglican blessing. The 1922 note called it "a feeble translation of the content of this word"; from <i>Poems 1909–1925</i> (1925) the note reads "our equivalent to this word".

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "The last eight lines, one by one": says what the note does. Acceptable; "Lines 427 to 434" is plainer.
- London Bridge: the whole entry is one sentence with no main verb, and "in the folklorists' reading that Frazer would have relished" is a wink. "The bridge the commuters crossed at line 62." is a fragment. Make two or three plain sentences.
- Purgatorio: mostly plain and good. "He goes willingly." is short for effect but says something true that I need; let it stand. "Set this fire against the burning of the Fire Sermon." is an instruction. Cut, or say plainly that this fire purifies where the fire of Part III burns, and mark it as a reading.
- Pervigilium: clear and useful; "suddenly turns on the poet" is a little dramatic. "Philomel's sister, still singing." is a fragment tied as a bow; replace with a sentence that names Procne and points to lines 99 and 203.
- Nerval: "The Tower is a Tarot card" asserts what the library record more honestly calls "often read as". "Another man who has been among the dead" is a bow.
- "These fragments": "quotations propped like timbers against a wall that is coming down" is the standard's own example. What I need here is the meaning of "shored": propped up, as builders shore a wall. Give me that and say the line is the poem's own.
- Kyd: plain and good. "A poem in seven languages, written as revenge." is a bow and a count. Cut.
- "Datta. Dayadhvam. Damyata.": fine.
- Shantih: the facts are right and useful but the sentence is tangled ("as Eliot's note says" floats free). Reorder. "and neither should you on a first reading" is named in the standard; cut the whole last sentence, since way:Peace already puts the question.
Questions for the scholar: In which edition did the note's "a feeble translation" become "our equivalent"? "The play he writes is in four languages": the library record says "sundry"; is four right? Whose reading is the Tarot Tower for "tour abolie"? Is Frazer actually connected to the foundation-sacrifice reading of the rhyme, or is he decoration?
Proposed text:
<b>London Bridge is falling down falling down falling down.</b> The nursery rhyme. Its verses try one material after another (wood and clay, bricks and mortar, iron and steel, silver and gold), and the oldest versions end by setting a man to watch all night. Folklorists of Frazer's generation connected the rhyme with the custom of burying a victim under a bridge's foundations. The crowd of line 62 crossed this bridge.

<b>Poi s'ascose nel foco che gli affina.</b> "Then he hid himself in the fire that refines them." <i>Purgatorio</i> XXVI, 148. Arnaut Daniel, having spoken to Dante in his own Provençal (the <i>Comedy</i>'s one passage in a vernacular other than Italian), steps back into the purifying flame. He goes willingly. Eliot's note quotes the end of Arnaut's speech, from "Ara vos prec," the words Eliot took for the title of his 1920 collection.

<b>Quando fiam ceu chelidon.</b> "When shall I become like the swallow?" From the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>, an anonymous Latin poem of the second to fourth century for the eve of Venus's spring festival. In its last stanza the nightingale sings and the poet, silent, asks when his own spring will come: "when shall I become like the swallow, so that I may stop being silent?" The 1922 printings have "ceu"; the Latin poem has "uti," and Eliot's later editions restored it. "O swallow swallow" is Tennyson's, from <i>The Princess</i>: "O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South." The swallow is Procne, sister of the Philomela of lines 99 and 203.

<b>Le Prince d'Aquitaine à la tour abolie.</b> The second line of Gérard de Nerval's sonnet "El Desdichado" (1854): "I am the dark one, the widower, the unconsoled, the Prince of Aquitaine with the ruined tower." The "tour abolie" is often read as the Tower of the Tarot pack (trump XVI, struck by lightning). Later in the sonnet the speaker says he has twice crossed Acheron, the river of the dead.

<b>These fragments I have shored against my ruins.</b> The poem's own line among the quotations. To shore is to prop up, as a builder shores a wall with timbers.

<b>Why then Ile fit you. Hieronymo's mad againe.</b> Kyd's <i>The Spanish Tragedy</i> (c. 1587), Act IV. Hieronimo, whose son has been murdered, is asked to write an entertainment for the court and answers "Why then, I'll fit you," meaning both "I'll oblige" and "I'll pay you out"; the play he writes is in four languages, and the murderers, acting in it, are killed for real. "Hieronimo is mad again" was the subtitle on the 1615 title page.

<b>Datta. Dayadhvam. Damyata.</b> The thunder's three words, repeated without the passages that answered them.

<b>Shantih shantih shantih.</b> Eliot's note: the formal ending of an Upanishad. "The Peace which passeth understanding" is Philippians 4:7; the 1922 note called it "a feeble translation" of the word, and later printings of the note "our equivalent."

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "Alice Gomme, in the 1890s": The Traditional Games of England, Scotland, and Ireland, vol. I (1894), pp. 346–47, under "London Bridge": "Looking to the fact of the widespread superstition of the foundation sacrifice, it would seem that we may have here a tradition of this rite," with the story "that the stones were bespattered with the blood of little children". She adds that W. W. Newell (Games and Songs of American Children, 1883) "gives countenance to this theory". So she was not the first; "in the 1890s" is true, "1894" is exact.
- "the flame that purifies the lustful": Purgatorio XXVI is the seventh terrace, the lustful; the souls cry "Soddoma e Gomorra" and "Ne la vacca entra Pasife", Guinizelli says "Nostro peccato fu ermafrodito", and the flame is theirs. Confirmed.
- "Every printing from 1922 to 1932, and Eliot's fair copies, read 'ceu'": 1925 Poems confirmed ("Quando fiam ceu chelidon"); the rest is Rainey's textual note, as before.
- Kyd IV.i, four languages, 1615 subtitle: as before.
Proposed text:
[one word] The folklorist Alice Gomme, in 1894, connected the rhyme with the old custom of burying a victim under a bridge's foundations.

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- Pervigilium: "the standard Latin text has "uti," which first appears in Collected Poems 1909–1935 (1936)": the "which" reads as if the standard Latin text first appeared in 1936. "Rainey": a reader does not know who that is; one clause tells them. "is also cited": by whom.
- Nerval: "read the tower with the Tarot's Tower": the idiom; "connect".
- Kyd: "The text of The Waste Land itself has six languages (...), with Greek in the epigraph and Provençal in the notes." As it stands this is a count with no reason given, the old bow in plainer clothes. Either cut it or say why it is there: commentators compare Hieronimo's play with the poem.
- Shantih: add the year to Poems 1909–1925, as the way does.
- The rest (London Bridge, Purgatorio with "He goes willingly", "These fragments", Datta) reads well; keep.
Proposed text (the sentences named; the rest unchanged):
[Pervigilium] Every printing from 1922 to 1932, and Eliot's fair copies, read "ceu"; the standard Latin text has "uti," and Eliot's line was changed to "uti" in <i>Collected Poems 1909–1935</i> (1936). Lawrence Rainey, in his annotated edition of the poem, doubts that the change was Eliot's. "O swallow swallow" is Tennyson's, from <i>The Princess</i> ("O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South"); commentators also cite Swinburne's "Itylus" ("Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow").
[Nerval] Some commentators connect the tower with the Tarot's Tower, struck by lightning; Nerval does not say so.
[Kyd, last sentence] Hieronimo's play in four languages has been compared with the poem, whose text has six (English, German, French, Italian, Latin and Sanskrit), with Greek in the epigraph and Provençal in the notes.
[Shantih, end] ...and from <i>Poems 1909–1925</i> (1925) the note reads "our equivalent to this word."

**After** (title: The last eight lines)

<b>London Bridge is falling down falling down falling down.</b> The nursery rhyme, first printed about 1744. Its verses try one material after another (wood and clay, bricks and mortar, iron and steel, silver and gold), and the oldest versions end by setting a man to watch all night. The folklorist Alice Gomme, in 1894, connected the rhyme with the old custom of burying a victim under a bridge's foundations. The crowd of line 62 crossed this bridge.

<b>Poi s'ascose nel foco che gli affina.</b> "Then he hid himself in the fire that refines them." <i>Purgatorio</i> XXVI, 148. Arnaut Daniel, having spoken to Dante in his own Provençal (the <i>Comedy</i>'s one passage in a vernacular other than Italian), steps back into the flame that purifies the lustful. He goes willingly. Eliot's note quotes the end of Arnaut's speech, from "Ara vos prec," the words Eliot took for the title of his 1920 collection.

<b>Quando fiam ceu chelidon.</b> "When shall I become like the swallow?" From the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>, an anonymous Latin poem of the second to fourth century for the eve of Venus's spring festival. Its last stanza turns from the nightingale to the poet's own silence: "she sings; we are silent. When does my spring come? When shall I become like the swallow, so that I may stop being silent?" Every printing from 1922 to 1932, and Eliot's fair copies, read "ceu"; the standard Latin text has "uti," and Eliot's line was changed to "uti" in <i>Collected Poems 1909–1935</i> (1936). Lawrence Rainey, in his annotated edition of the poem, doubts that the change was Eliot's. "O swallow swallow" is Tennyson's, from <i>The Princess</i> ("O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South"); commentators also cite Swinburne's "Itylus" ("Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow"). The swallow is Procne, sister of the Philomela of lines 99 and 203.

<b>Le Prince d'Aquitaine à la tour abolie.</b> The second line of Gérard de Nerval's sonnet "El Desdichado" (1854): "I am the dark one, the widower, the unconsoled, the Prince of Aquitaine with the ruined tower." Some commentators connect the tower with the Tarot's Tower, struck by lightning; Nerval does not say so. Later in the sonnet the speaker says he has twice crossed Acheron, the river of the dead.

<b>These fragments I have shored against my ruins.</b> The poem's own line among the quotations. To shore is to prop up, as a builder shores a wall with timbers.

<b>Why then Ile fit you. Hieronymo's mad againe.</b> Kyd's <i>The Spanish Tragedy</i> (c. 1587), Act IV scene i. Hieronimo, whose son has been murdered, is asked by the murderers to write an entertainment for the court and answers "Why then, I'll fit you," meaning both "I'll oblige" and "I'll pay you out." The play he gives them is acted in four languages, Latin, Greek, Italian and French, and in it the murderers are stabbed in earnest. "Hieronimo is mad againe" is the subtitle on the title page of the 1615 quarto. Hieronimo's play in four languages has been compared with the poem, whose text has six (English, German, French, Italian, Latin and Sanskrit), with Greek in the epigraph and Provençal in the notes.

<b>Datta. Dayadhvam. Damyata.</b> The thunder's three words, repeated without the passages that answered them.

<b>Shantih shantih shantih.</b> Eliot's note: "Repeated as here, a formal ending to an Upanishad." "The Peace which passeth understanding" is Philippians 4:7 in the words of the Anglican blessing; the 1922 note called it "a feeble translation of the content of this word," and from <i>Poems 1909–1925</i> (1925) the note reads "our equivalent to this word."

### way 1 of 12: Eliot reading the thunder

**Before**

Eliot's own recordings are at their strangest here: the "DA" is spoken flat, without drama, and "Shantih" three times as a murmur. Listen to the last thirty lines before reading the notes on them.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Eliot recorded the poem in 1933 (Columbia) and in 1946 (Library of Congress; a third, 1947, at the National Gallery). "Eliot's own recordings" is right.
- "the 'DA' is spoken flat, without drama, and 'Shantih' three times as a murmur": a description of performance I cannot check from here; if it stays, it is the site's ear, and a listener may disagree. Safer to say what the recordings are and let the reader listen.
Missing: the dates of the recordings.
Proposed text:
Eliot recorded the poem in 1933 and again in 1946. Listen to the last thirty lines before reading the notes on them.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: describes the recording. Fine.
- "Eliot's own recordings are at their strangest here": a ranking, and "strangest" tells me what to feel. Say what he does: "DA" flat, "Shantih" quiet.
- "Listen to the last thirty lines before reading the notes on them." A "listen" way is there to tell me to listen; that is the genre. "Before reading the notes" is a rule about how to use the site. Cut it.
Questions for the scholar: Which recording, and what year? Is "flat, without drama" a description the scholar has heard and agrees with?
Proposed text: In his recording [year] Eliot says "DA" flat, without emphasis, and "Shantih" three times quietly. The last thirty lines are in the listening room.

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- The Library of Congress catalogue record for the 1947 reading (item 95770483, "T.S. Eliot reading his poems with comment at the National Gallery of Art, Washington, D.C., May 23, 1947") lists the contents: "The love song of J. Alfred Prufrock -- La figlia che piange -- Sweeney among the nightingales -- The waste land [V] -- Ariel poems: Journey of the magi; Animula -- Landscapes [pts. 1-5] -- Ash Wednesday [VI] -- East Coker." So the 1947 Washington reading has Part V only, as the site's listening room says; the article that calls it a complete recording (Hilltop Review, WMU) is wrong.
- "recorded the whole poem in 1933 and again in 1946": the 1933 Columbia discs and the 1946 recording made for the Library of Congress, later issued by Caedmon and HarperAudio. Right. (The listening room's "The Caedmon recording, usually dated 1946" is another thread's; it might say "made for the Library of Congress in 1946".)
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "Listen to the last thirty lines, from the thunder on." The thunder speaks at line 400, thirty-five lines from the end; the two halves of the sentence do not agree.
Proposed text:
Eliot recorded the whole poem in 1933 and again in 1946; both are in the listening room, with Part V as he read it in Washington in 1947. Listen from line 400, where the thunder speaks, to the end.

**After**

Eliot recorded the whole poem in 1933 and again in 1946; both are in the listening room, with Part V as he read it in Washington in 1947. Listen from line 400, where the thunder speaks, to the end.

### way 2 of 12: Water in Part V

**Before** (title: Water at last)

The section is the poem's longest drought and its only rain. The Water lens shows twenty-nine lines of rock and then, at line 395, "Bringing rain." Everything after that is wet.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "The section is the poem's longest drought and its only rain": not the only rain. Line 4 has "spring rain" and line 9 "With a shower of rain". The "twenty-nine lines of rock" count (331–359) is right, but the rain at 395 is thirty-six lines after they end, not straight after.
- "Everything after that is wet": no. Line 397 "Waited for rain", line 425 "the arid plain". The poem says "Bringing rain" and does not show rain falling.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
The section is the poem's longest drought. The Water lens tints the twenty-nine lines of rock (331–359), the "damp gust / Bringing rain" at 395, the leaves that still wait for it at 397, the Ganges, the boat, and the "arid plain" at 425. It is the first rain since the shower of line 9, and the poem does not say it falls.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title, "Water at last": mildly dramatic. "Water in Part V" would do.
- "The section is the poem's longest drought and its only rain." An aphorism, and "its only rain" contradicts gloss:chapel, which counts the shower at line 9.
- "twenty-nine lines of rock and then, at line 395, "Bringing rain."" The count from Ethan's own quoted sentence.
- "Everything after that is wet." False to the lines: 396 to 398 wait for rain, 425 has "the arid plain."
Questions for the scholar: Does the rain fall? (See gloss:chapel.)
Proposed text: Title: Water in Part V. The Water lens tints every line that is about water or the want of it. Here there is none from line 331 to line 394; then "Bringing rain" (395); the Ganges, sunken and waiting for it (396 to 398); the boat on a calm sea (419 to 421); the shore, with the arid plain behind it (424 to 425).

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "No rain falls from line 331 to line 394. At line 395 a gust brings rain; at 397 the leaves are still waiting for it": the first sentence implies that rain falls at 395, which the next clause denies. Say "no water".
Proposed text:
The Water lens tints every line that is about water or the want of it. From line 331 to line 394 there is no water. At line 395 a gust brings rain, and at 397 the leaves are still waiting for it; at 419 a boat answers "the hand expert with sail and oar" on a calm sea; at 425 the arid plain is behind the fisherman.

**After** (title: Water in Part V)

The Water lens tints every line that is about water or the want of it. From line 331 to line 394 there is no water. At line 395 a gust brings rain, and at 397 the leaves are still waiting for it; at 419 a boat answers "the hand expert with sail and oar" on a calm sea; at 425 the arid plain is behind the fisherman.

### way 3 of 12: Hear the hermit-thrush

**Before**

"Drip drop drip drop drop drop drop." Eliot's note names the bird and quotes the field guide. A recording is in the listening room. Play it, then read line 358. His transcription is accurate.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "Eliot's note names the bird and quotes the field guide": right (Chapman, p. 400).
- "His transcription is accurate": contradicted. The "water-dripping song" is not in Chapman, and the hermit-thrush's song, a series of flute-like phrases, is not heard by ornithologists as dripping water (Notes and Queries, March 2014). Whether "Drip drop drip drop drop drop drop" transcribes anything is for the listener; the way should not decide it.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
"Drip drop drip drop drop drop drop." Eliot's note names the bird and quotes Chapman's field guide on its song. Chapman does not call it "water-dripping"; that phrase is Eliot's. A recording is in the listening room. Play it, then read line 358, and judge.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Fine until the end. "His transcription is accurate." A verdict I should reach myself, and the three notes on the bird disagree about it ("exact", "accurate", "fair"). Cut.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "Drip drop drip drop drop drop drop." Eliot's note names the bird and quotes the field guide. A recording is in the listening room. Play it, then read line 358.

**After**

"Drip drop drip drop drop drop drop." Eliot's note names the bird and quotes Chapman's field guide on its song; "water-dripping" is not Chapman's phrase. A recording is in the listening room. Play it, then read line 358.

### way 4 of 12: The road to Emmaus, and South Georgia

**Before**

Two texts under "Who is the third who walks always beside you?": Luke 24 and Shackleton's account of crossing South Georgia in 1916, when three men each felt a fourth. Read both. The hooded figure is a risen god and a hallucination of exhausted men, and the poem will not say which.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "Shackleton's account of crossing South Georgia in 1916, when three men each felt a fourth": right (South, chapter X; 19–20 May 1916).
- "The hooded figure is a risen god and a hallucination of exhausted men, and the poem will not say which": the first is a reading (Luke, and Eliot's note on line 46 tying the Hanged Man to the hooded figure), the second is what Eliot's note says Shackleton's party had ("the constant delusion"). Fair as a summary of the two sources; "risen god" is stronger than the texts, which have a risen Christ and a Hanged God (Frazer) in Eliot's note. Say "the stranger on the road to Emmaus and the delusion of exhausted men".
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
Two texts stand under "Who is the third who walks always beside you?": Luke 24 and Shackleton's account in <i>South</i> of crossing South Georgia in May 1916, when each of three men felt a fourth. Read both. The stranger on the road to Emmaus and the delusion of exhausted men are both in the line, and the poem does not choose.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "Read both." Fine for a "source" way.
- "The hooded figure is a risen god and a hallucination of exhausted men, and the poem will not say which." The confident "is", a pair for rhythm, and a bow. Eliot's note gives both sources; that is what to say.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Two texts under "Who is the third who walks always beside you?": Luke 24, and Shackleton's account of crossing South Georgia in 1916, when three men each felt a fourth. Both are in the library. Eliot's headnote gives the first and his note on the line gives the second.

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "Two texts stand under": the timbers again. Say who gives them.
Proposed text:
Eliot gives two sources for "Who is the third who walks always beside you?": in his headnote, the journey to Emmaus of Luke 24; in his note on the line, Shackleton's account in <i>South</i> of crossing South Georgia in May 1916, when each of three men felt a fourth walking with them. Both are in the library.

**After**

Eliot gives two sources for "Who is the third who walks always beside you?": in his headnote, the journey to Emmaus of Luke 24; in his note on the line, Shackleton's account in <i>South</i> of crossing South Georgia in May 1916, when each of three men felt a fourth walking with them. Both are in the library.

### way 5 of 12: The languages of the last lines

**Before** (title: Eight lines, six languages)

The last eight lines are in English, Italian, Latin, French, English, English, Sanskrit, Sanskrit, with Provençal in the note. Switch on Tongues, read the translations, then read the lines again as a person who has none of them: as a noise made by a civilisation.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Lines 427–434 are eight lines. The languages in them are five: English (427, 431, 432, and half of 429), Italian (428), Latin (429), French (430), Sanskrit (433, 434). Provençal is only in Eliot's note. The body says as much ("with Provençal in the note") and the title contradicts it; the "first" path at 427 says "five languages". Make them agree: five.
- "as a noise made by a civilisation" is the voice, but it is the site's suggestion for a way of reading and can stay if the reader accepts it.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
[title] Eight lines, five languages
[body] The last eight lines are in English, Italian, Latin, French, English, English, Sanskrit, Sanskrit; Eliot's note on line 428 adds Provençal. Switch on Tongues, read the translations, then read the lines again without them.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: counts to be clever, and the count fights path:first/427 ("five languages"). The lines have five; the sixth is in a note. Retitle: "The languages of the last lines."
- The first sentence is plain and useful. Keep.
- "then read the lines again as a person who has none of them: as a noise made by a civilisation." Tells me how to experience the lines, and ends in a metaphor. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Title: The languages of the last lines. The last eight lines are in English, Italian, Latin, French, English, English, Sanskrit, Sanskrit, with Provençal in Eliot's note. The Tongues lens gives the translations.

**After** (title: The languages of the last lines)

The last eight lines are in English, Italian, Latin, French, English, English, Sanskrit, Sanskrit; Eliot's note on line 428 adds Provençal. The Tongues lens gives the translations.

### way 6 of 12: The fragments, one by one

**Before** (title: Unstack the fragments)

"These fragments I have shored against my ruins." The Fragments view pulls the last lines apart one at a time, each back to its source, so that you can see the wall the poet says he propped them against.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: sound
Findings:
- No fact to fault. "the wall the poet says he propped them against" is the site's image on Eliot's own metaphor; harmless.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: "Unstack" is the timbers metaphor turned into a verb. "The fragments, one by one" is plainer.
- "so that you can see the wall the poet says he propped them against." The metaphor about the poem again. Cut after "source."
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Title: The fragments, one by one. "These fragments I have shored against my ruins." The Fragments view takes the last lines one at a time and gives each its source.

**After** (title: The fragments, one by one)

"These fragments I have shored against my ruins." The Fragments view takes the last lines one at a time and gives each its source.

### way 7 of 12: The fable in the Upanishad

**Before** (title: What the thunder actually said)

Read the Upanishad's fable: one syllable, three hearers, three meanings. Then notice that Eliot changes the order of the commands and confesses, after each, how it was not kept.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: sound
Findings:
- "one syllable, three hearers, three meanings": right (Upanishad V.2). "Eliot changes the order of the commands and confesses, after each, how it was not kept": the reordering is fact; "confesses ... how it was not kept" is the usual reading, and a way may put it so.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title: "actually" is a joke on the section title. Retitle: "The fable in the Upanishad."
- "one syllable, three hearers, three meanings." The counted list of three.
- "Then notice that Eliot changes the order of the commands and confesses, after each, how it was not kept." "Notice that" is an instruction; "confesses ... how it was not kept" is a reading as fact.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Title: The fable in the Upanishad. The library has the passage of the Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad that Eliot's note cites: the thunder says "Da," and gods, men and demons each hear a different word in it. Eliot changes the order of the three words and follows each with a passage of his own.

**After** (title: The fable in the Upanishad)

The library has the passage of the <i>Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad</i> that Eliot's note cites: the thunder says "Da," and gods, men and demons each hear a different word in it. Eliot changes the order of the three words and follows each with a passage of his own.

### way 8 of 12: The places in Part V

**Before** (title: From Margate to the Ganges)

The section's places are Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna, London, the Ganges and the Himalaya. The world map draws the poem's journey east in a single line.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "The section's places are Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna, London, the Ganges and the Himalaya": the text also has London Bridge (427), Aquitaine (430), and, in the notes, Quebec County, South Georgia, Emmaus, Pisa (Ugolino's tower).
- Margate is Part III (line 300), not Part V. If the way is about the whole map, say so; if about the section, drop Margate from the title.
- "draws the poem's journey east in a single line": Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna, London is not a journey east; it runs east to west and then jumps to the Ganges. Say what the map does, not what the sequence is not.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
The section names Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna and London in two lines, then the Ganges and the Himalaya, then London Bridge and Aquitaine; Eliot's notes add Quebec, South Georgia, Emmaus and Pisa. The world map shows them all.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: Margate is in Part III, not here; the title reaches outside the section for a flourish. Retitle: "The places in Part V."
- "The world map draws the poem's journey east in a single line." The poem makes no journey; the map draws a line. Say the map shows them.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Title: The places in Part V. The places named in this section are Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna and London, the Ganges and the Himalaya. The world map shows them.

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- Right as far as it goes; the notes also bring in South Georgia and Emmaus, but a way need not list everything.
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "two places of Eliot's own that the notes bring in: Quebec ... and Cape Ann": Quebec is in Eliot's note; Cape Ann is not, it is the commentators' (see gloss:damyata). Question for the editor: does the map in fact show these two? If not, cut the clause.
Proposed text:
The section names Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna and London in two lines, then the Ganges and the Himalaya, then London Bridge and Aquitaine. The world map shows the cities, the river and the mountain, and two places from Eliot's life: Quebec, where his note says he heard the hermit-thrush, and Cape Ann, where he sailed as a boy.

**After** (title: The places in Part V)

The section names Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna and London in two lines, then the Ganges and the Himalaya, then London Bridge and Aquitaine. The world map shows the cities, the river and the mountain, and two places from Eliot's life: Quebec, where his note says he heard the hermit-thrush, and Cape Ann, where he sailed as a boy.

### way 9 of 12: Every 'nothing'

**Before**

"I knew nothing," "Nothing again nothing," "Do you know nothing?," "Nothing with nothing," "expect / Nothing." The pathway follows the word through the poem to its last appearance.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- The quotations are right: "I knew nothing" (40), "Nothing again nothing" (120), "Do you know nothing?" (121–122), "Nothing with nothing" (302), "expect / Nothing" (304–305).
- But the word does not occur in Part V. Its last appearance is line 305. "The pathway follows the word through the poem to its last appearance" is true of the pathway as a whole and misleading at the head of Part V, where the stop (402, "what have we given?") has no "nothing" in it.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
"I knew nothing", "Nothing again nothing", "Do you know nothing?", "Nothing with nothing", "expect / Nothing". The word is not said in Part V; the pathway ends at line 305 and comes here only to ask what the thunder's "what have we given?" answers.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- The list of quotations is the pathway's content; fine.
- "The pathway follows the word through the poem to its last appearance." The word does not occur anywhere in lines 322 to 434 (I looked), and the pathway's Part V stop is on "what have we given?" So this way, on the Part V list, promises a last appearance that is not here.
Questions for the scholar: Where is the last "nothing" in the poem? If it is in Part III, should this way be on the Part V list, and what is the stop at 402 for?
Proposed text: (first sentence as is.) The pathway follows the word through the poem. Its last stop is at line 402.

**After**

"I knew nothing," "Nothing again nothing," "Do you know nothing?," "Nothing with nothing," "expect / Nothing." The pathway follows the word through the poem. It is not said in Part V; the last stop, at line 402, is the thunder's question, "what have we given?"

### way 10 of 12: OK from here on

**Before**

Pound's note at the head of the section reads "OK from here on I think." He cut almost nothing. Eliot wrote it in a few weeks at Lausanne and said afterwards it was "the only part that justifies the whole, at all."

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "Pound's note at the head of the section reads 'OK from here on I think'": not verified (see "Could not verify"). "He cut almost nothing": supported (Spectator review of the facsimile, 1 January 1972: "hardly a Poundian mark upon it").
- "Eliot wrote it in a few weeks at Lausanne": 22 November 1921 to 2 January 1922. Right.
- "said afterwards it was 'the only part that justifies the whole, at all'": the letter (to Russell, 15 October 1923) reads "the only part which justifies the whole, at all".
Missing: to whom and when he said it.
Proposed text:
Pound left the typescript of Part V almost untouched. Eliot wrote it at Lausanne between late November 1921 and the New Year, and told Bertrand Russell in October 1923 that it was "the only part which justifies the whole, at all".

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: keep
Findings:
- Plain and sourced. Only "said afterwards" should say to whom and when (to Bertrand Russell, October 1923), as gloss:torchlight does.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

**After**

Pound's note at the head of the typescript of Part V reads "OK from here on I think," and he cut almost nothing. Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne between late November 1921 and the New Year, and told Bertrand Russell in October 1923 that it was "the only part which justifies the whole, at all."

### way 11 of 12: The last voices

**Before**

The Fisher King, a nursery rhyme, Dante, an anonymous Latin poet, Nerval, the poet himself, Kyd's mad Hieronimo, an Upanishad. Voices colours each of the last eight lines differently. Read them as a chorus and as a man talking to himself.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Nine voices are listed for "each of the last eight lines". If the Fisher King is line 426, the span is nine lines; if the last eight (427–434), drop him. Line 433 and 434 are both "an Upanishad" (or the thunder); "the poet himself" is 431 and, arguably, 432's "Why then Ile fit you". Nothing false; the count does not match.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
A nursery rhyme, Dante, an anonymous Latin poet and Tennyson, Nerval, the poet, Kyd's Hieronimo, the thunder, an Upanishad. Voices colours the last eight lines differently. Read them as a chorus, then as one man.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- The opening list has no verb.
- "Read them as a chorus and as a man talking to himself." Tells me how to read. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: The Voices lens colours each of the last eight lines by its speaker: the Fisher King, a nursery rhyme, Dante, an anonymous Latin poet, Nerval, the poet himself, Kyd's Hieronimo, an Upanishad.

**The reader, round 3**

Findings:
- Not voice, consistency: the way says the lens's note lists "a nursery rhyme, Dante, an anonymous Latin poet, Nerval, the poet himself, Kyd's Hieronimo", and the proposal to the site thread now puts Tennyson into that note. If the proposal is taken, the way must match it; if not, the way is right as it stands.
Proposed text (if the proposal is taken):
Its note on the fragments lists them in order: a nursery rhyme, Dante, a Latin poet and Tennyson, Nerval, the poet himself, Kyd's Hieronimo.

**After**

The Voices lens gives lines 424 to 426 to the Fisher King, lines 427 to 432 to the fragments, and the last two lines to the Upanishad. The fragments are, in order, a nursery rhyme, Dante, a Latin poet and Tennyson, Nerval, the poet himself, Kyd's Hieronimo.

### way 12 of 12: The last word

**Before** (title: Peace, or the word for it)

"Shantih shantih shantih." A benediction, or a man saying "peace" until he sleeps? Eliot's 1922 note called the English "a feeble translation." Read the last line once as a blessing and once as a lullaby, and keep the one that lets you close the book.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "Eliot's 1922 note called the English 'a feeble translation'": right; and from 1925 "our equivalent to this word". The rest is an instruction ("keep the one that lets you close the book"); the reader's business, but a way may propose a reading.
Missing: the 1925 revision, since the way rests on the 1922 wording.
Proposed text:
"Shantih shantih shantih." Eliot's note calls it "a formal ending to an Upanishad"; in 1922 he called "The Peace which passeth understanding" a feeble translation, and from 1925 "our equivalent". Read the last line once as a blessing and once as a man saying the word to himself.

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title: a turn of phrase. "The last word" or "Shantih."
- "Read the last line once as a blessing and once as a lullaby, and keep the one that lets you close the book." The second half is named in the standard; the first is an exercise in how to feel. An "ask" way asks a question; keep the question and stop.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Title: The last word. "Shantih shantih shantih." Eliot's note says it is the formal ending of an Upanishad and that "The Peace which passeth understanding" is "a feeble translation" of it. Is the line a blessing on the poem, or a man saying the word to himself?

**After** (title: The last word)

"Shantih shantih shantih." Eliot's note calls it the formal ending of an Upanishad; in 1922 he called "The Peace which passeth understanding" a feeble translation of the word, and from 1925 "our equivalent." Is the line a blessing on the poem, or a man saying the word to himself?

### draft:ok-from-here: "OK from here on I think"

**Before**

At the head of Part V Pound wrote "OK from here on I think," and cut almost nothing. Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne in November and December 1921, fast, and later said it had come "in a trance." Three of the poem's passages are older than the rest: the woman with the long black hair and the bats with baby faces (lines 378–385) come from a fragment beginning "So through the evening, through the violet air"; the "red rock" of Part I came from "The Death of Saint Narcissus" (1915); and Phlebas from "Dans le Restaurant" (1918).

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "At the head of Part V Pound wrote 'OK from here on I think,' and cut almost nothing": the cutting is confirmed by readers of the facsimile (Spectator, 1 January 1972: "hardly a Poundian mark upon it"); the words of the note I could not verify online (see "Could not verify").
- "Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne in November and December 1921": right (22 November 1921 to 2 January 1922).
- "and later said it had come 'in a trance'": I can find no statement of Eliot's with this word. What he said: in the 1931 essay on Pascal, that "some forms of illness are extremely favourable, not only to religious illumination, but to artistic and literary composition. A piece of writing meditated, apparently without progress, for months or years, may suddenly take shape and word; and in this state long passages may be produced which require little or no retouch", a passage critics (and Valerie Eliot, as usually reported) apply to Part V; and in the Paris Review interview (1959), "In The Waste Land, I wasn't even bothering whether I understood what I was saying", about the poem as a whole. "In a trance" appears in popular summaries (e.g. interestingliterature.com) as a paraphrase. Remove the quotation marks and say what he said.
- "Three of the poem's passages are older than the rest": an undercount. Besides the three named (the violet-air fragment for 378–385; "The Death of Saint Narcissus" (1915) for the red rock of lines 25–29; "Dans le Restaurant" (1918) for Phlebas), the facsimile prints "After the turning of the inspired days", from which lines 322–330 are built, and "The Death of the Duchess", from which the opening of the dialogue in Part II is built. I could not check the facsimile pages online; Ricks and McCue print both fragments. Confirm and count them, or say "several".
- "The Death of Saint Narcissus (1915)": set in type for Poetry in 1915 and withdrawn; fine. "Dans le Restaurant (1918)": Little Review, September 1918; fine.
- The 1971 facsimile is in copyright; the record quotes only first lines, which is within the rule.
Missing: the fragment behind lines 322–330; that Part V's manuscript is in Eliot's hand (pencil) as well as typescript, which is why the facsimile shows two states of it (to confirm against the facsimile).
Proposed text:
Pound left the typescript of Part V almost untouched; his cuts fell on Parts III and IV. Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne between 22 November 1921 and 2 January 1922. In 1931, in an essay on Pascal, he wrote that in some forms of illness "long passages may be produced which require little or no retouch"; readers have applied the sentence to Part V, though he did not. Several passages of the poem are older than 1921. The woman with the long black hair and the bats with baby faces (lines 378–385) come from a fragment beginning "So through the evening, through the violet air"; the opening of Part V draws on another, "After the turning of the inspired days"; the red rock of Part I came from "The Death of Saint Narcissus" (1915), and Phlebas from "Dans le Restaurant" (1918).

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: Pound's words. Fine.
- "Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne in November and December 1921, fast, and later said it had come "in a trance."" "Fast," set between commas is a rhythm tic; "in a trance" needs a source and a date.
- The rest is plain. The last two items are Parts I and IV; fine on a drafts page.
Questions for the scholar: Where and when did Eliot say "in a trance"? Is the full first line of the fragment within the "phrase" rule for the 1971 facsimile? (Also quoted in gloss:bats.)
Proposed text: At the head of Part V Pound wrote "OK from here on I think," and cut almost nothing. Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne in November and December 1921, and later said [where, when] that it had come "in a trance." (Then the sentence on the three older passages, as it stands.)

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept, with one check outstanding
Findings:
- "while under treatment by Roger Vittoz": right; Eliot went to Lausanne on Ottoline Morrell's recommendation of Vittoz and saw him through his stay.
- "In an essay on Pascal in 1931": the introduction to the Everyman Pensées (1931), "The 'Pensées' of Pascal"; the sentence is quoted rightly.
- "Lines 322 to 330 draw on a fragment beginning 'After the turning of the inspired days' ... printed in the 1971 facsimile": the fragment is in the facsimile (p. 108, with "I am the Resurrection and the Life" at p. 110 and "So through the evening, through the violet air" at p. 112; the listing is in the edition's contents). That lines 322–330 draw on it rests on my memory of its text ("And the torchlight red on sweaty faces", "And frosty vigil kept in withered gardens", "After the praying and the silence and the crying") and on Valerie Eliot's note; the facsimile and Ricks and McCue could not be opened online. I am confident, but it should be ticked against p. 108 when the book is to hand.
- "Pound ... cut almost nothing; his cuts had fallen on the earlier parts": Keane and the Spectator, as above.
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "his cuts had fallen on the earlier parts": "fallen on" is literary; say he cut heavily there.
- "while under treatment by Roger Vittoz": who? One word tells me.
- "a sentence often applied to Part V": the scholar's "though Eliot did not" is worth keeping; it is the caution a reader needs.
Proposed text (the first three sentences; the rest unchanged):
At the head of the typescript of Part V Pound wrote "OK from here on I think," and cut almost nothing; he had cut heavily in the earlier parts. Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne between 22 November 1921 and 2 January 1922, while under treatment by Dr Roger Vittoz. In an essay on Pascal in 1931 he wrote that in some forms of illness "long passages may be produced which require little or no retouch," a sentence readers have applied to Part V, though Eliot did not.

**After**

At the head of the typescript of Part V Pound wrote "OK from here on I think," and cut almost nothing; he had cut heavily in the earlier parts. Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne between 22 November 1921 and 2 January 1922, while under treatment by Dr Roger Vittoz. In an essay on Pascal in 1931 he wrote that in some forms of illness "long passages may be produced which require little or no retouch," a sentence readers have applied to Part V, though Eliot did not. Several passages of the poem are older than 1921. Lines 322 to 330 draw on a fragment beginning "After the turning of the inspired days," and lines 378 to 385 on another beginning "So through the evening, through the violet air," both printed in the 1971 facsimile; the "red rock" of Part I came from "The Death of Saint Narcissus" (1915), and Phlebas from "Dans le Restaurant" (1918).

### draft:manuscript-fate: What happened to the papers

**Before**

Eliot gave the typescript, with Pound's and Vivien's pencil on it, to John Quinn, the New York lawyer and collector who had handled his American publishing, in 1922; Quinn insisted on paying $140 for it. After Quinn's death in 1924 it vanished. His niece sold it to the New York Public Library in 1958, and the library announced the acquisition in 1968, three years after Eliot's death. Valerie Eliot's facsimile edition of 1971 printed every page. The papers are in the Berg Collection; the cuts, and Pound's pencil, are the reason the poem is 434 lines long and not 800.

**The scholar, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "Eliot gave the typescript, with Pound's and Vivien's pencil on it, to John Quinn ... in 1922; Quinn insisted on paying $140 for it": the $140 was not for The Waste Land. Eliot offered Quinn the manuscript as a gift, and separately the notebook of early poems (the "Inventions of the March Hare" notebook, with loose leaves); Quinn accepted the Waste Land papers as a gift on condition that he could buy the notebook, which he did, for $140 (Menand, NYRB, 15 May 1997, drawing on Ricks's edition of the notebook). The parcel was posted on 23 October 1922 and reached Quinn on 13 January 1923. "who had handled his American publishing": Quinn negotiated the Boni and Liveright contract; fine.
- "After Quinn's death in 1924 it vanished": it passed to his sister, Julia Anderson, and then to her daughter, Mary Conroy (Mrs Thomas F. Conroy), who found it in storage in the early 1950s. "Vanished" is what the world thought, not what happened; say "was thought lost".
- "His niece sold it to the New York Public Library in 1958, and the library announced the acquisition in 1968": right (Berg Collection; sale 1958; announced October 1968; Valerie Eliot was told in April 1968). "three years after Eliot's death": Eliot died 4 January 1965; nearly four years.
- "Valerie Eliot's facsimile edition of 1971 printed every page": fine.
- "the reason the poem is 434 lines long and not 800": the 800 is Eliot's own figure, in a recorded conversation with Leslie Paul in 1958 ("reduced a mess of some eight hundred lines to about half its size"); in his 1946 essay on Pound he said "reduced to about half its size". Attribute the figure to him; the drafts with the discarded poems run longer.
Missing: whose figure 800 is; the notebook.
Proposed text:
In October 1922 Eliot sent the drafts, with Pound's and Vivien's pencil on them, to John Quinn, the New York lawyer and collector who had negotiated his American contract, as a gift; Quinn accepted them on condition that he could buy the notebook of Eliot's early poems, for which he paid $140. After Quinn's death in 1924 the papers passed to his sister and then to her daughter, Mary Conroy, and were thought lost. She found them in storage in the early 1950s and sold them to the Berg Collection of the New York Public Library in 1958; the library announced the acquisition in October 1968, nearly four years after Eliot's death. Valerie Eliot's facsimile edition of 1971 printed every page. Eliot said in 1958 that Pound had "reduced a mess of some eight hundred lines to about half its size".

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: plain. Keep.
- "After Quinn's death in 1924 it vanished." Dramatic; what is meant is that its whereabouts were unknown.
- "the cuts, and Pound's pencil, are the reason the poem is 434 lines long and not 800." The counting bow, and I do not know that 800 is right.
- The rest is plain and useful.
Questions for the scholar: How long was the draft? Are "$140" and "insisted on paying" sourced?
Proposed text: (as it stands until "in 1922;" then:) Quinn insisted on paying $140 for it. After Quinn's death in 1924 the whereabouts of the typescript were unknown. His niece sold it to the New York Public Library in 1958, and the library announced the acquisition in 1968, three years after Eliot's death. Valerie Eliot's facsimile edition of 1971 printed every page. The papers are in the Berg Collection.

**The scholar, round 2**

Verdict: accept
Findings:
- "who had negotiated his American contract": Quinn handled the Boni and Liveright contract for The Waste Land in 1922 (and the Knopf contracts before it); the Beinecke's account of the Dial dispute has Watson telling Seldes to "see Quinn". Right; "contracts" would cover both.
- "Eliot himself said in 1946 that the poem had left Pound's hands 'reduced to about half its size'": the essay is "Ezra Pound", Poetry 68 (September 1946). The sentence, as quoted by the Eliot estate and by Britannica: "It was in 1922 that I placed before him in Paris the manuscript of a sprawling chaotic poem called The Waste Land which left his hands, reduced to about half its size, in the form in which it appears in print." I could not open Poetry's own page (it is behind a script wall), so the words are confirmed at one remove.
Proposed text: none

**The reader, round 2**

Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "with Pound's and Vivien's pencil on them": a reader does not know who Vivien is.
- "sent the drafts ... to John Quinn, the New York lawyer and collector who had negotiated his American contract, as a gift": "as a gift" arrives after a long detour; "gave" says it at once.
Proposed text (the first sentence; the rest unchanged):
In October 1922 Eliot gave the drafts, with Pound's pencil and his wife Vivien's on them, to John Quinn, the New York lawyer and collector who had negotiated his American contract; Quinn accepted them on condition that he could buy the notebook of Eliot's early poems, for which he paid $140.

**After**

In October 1922 Eliot gave the drafts, with Pound's pencil and his wife Vivien's on them, to John Quinn, the New York lawyer and collector who had negotiated his American contracts; Quinn accepted them on condition that he could buy the notebook of Eliot's early poems, for which he paid $140. After Quinn's death in 1924 the papers passed to his sister and then to her daughter, Mary Conroy, and were thought lost. She found them in storage in the early 1950s and sold them to the Berg Collection of the New York Public Library in 1958; the library announced the acquisition in October 1968, nearly four years after Eliot's death. Valerie Eliot's facsimile edition of 1971 printed every page. Eliot himself said in 1946 that the poem had left Pound's hands "reduced to about half its size."

### D1. Pathway intros

**The reader, round 1**



### pathway:first

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings: "Each stop is a place to stand, not an explanation" is the "where the poem is standing" idiom; "Read the lines, read the note, move on" is a list of three imperatives.
Proposed text: Twelve stops for a first reading. Each gives a line or two and a short note.

### pathway:water

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings: "Follow the water and you follow the argument: water is what the waste land needs, water is what drowns you, and the two are never told apart." The twin of Ethan's quoted sentence: "X and you Y the argument:" then a list of three.
Proposed text: Every line in which water is present or missing, from the shower of line 9 to the rain of line 395. The poem wants water and is drowned by it; the pathway shows both.

### pathway:dead

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings: "and the dead never quite stay in it" is a joke to end on.
Proposed text: Burials, drownings, bones and the crowd that death has undone. The poem's first section is called The Burial of the Dead, from the Book of Common Prayer; the dead appear in every part.

### pathway:women

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings: "Most of them speak; several are quotations; two are queens; one is a bird." A counted list for rhythm. The first two sentences are plain and give me the test; stop there.
Proposed text: the first two sentences as they stand.

### pathway:cities

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings: "and five capitals falling in two lines": a counting flourish. Cut the clause.
Proposed text: The poem's geography, in the order the poem gives it. It begins in Munich and ends on the Ganges, with London at its centre.

### pathway:birds

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Findings: "Every bird in the poem is a musician, and every song is a complaint." An aphorism.
Proposed text: Every bird named in the poem, in order. Recordings, where they exist, are in the listening room.

Findings: The list is the pathway's content and it is plain. No fault.
Proposed text: none

### pathway:eyes

Verdict: trim

### pathway:eliot

Verdict: trim

### source:weston

**The reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Findings: "It does not matter." A fragment-aphorism; whether the theory stands does matter to me. "Read chapters II ..., and you have the poem's skeleton; read the rest and you have a very good bad book." An instruction ending in a joke. The rest (what Weston argued, Eliot's note, his 1956 regret in a phrase) is plain and useful.
Proposed text: replace the two flagged sentences with: "Almost no medievalist now accepts the argument; the poem used the book anyway." and "The chapters the poem draws on most are II (The Task of the Hero), IX (The Fisher King) and XIII (The Perilous Chapel)."

- cities: "It begins in Munich": the first place named is the Starnbergersee (line 8), then the Hofgarten (line 10), which is in Munich. "Begins at the Starnbergersee" is exact; "Munich" is acceptable shorthand.

[water] Every line in which water is present or wanted, from the spring rain of line 4 to the gust of line 395.
