## The agents' closing sections, by round

**The scholar, round 1**

## C1. Glosses

### gloss:torchlight
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- John 18:3 confirmed: Judas comes "with lanterns and torches and weapons" (KJV). The garden and the agony are Luke 22:39–46 ("being in an agony he prayed more earnestly", 22:44). "Prison and palace" as Caiaphas's house and Pilate's is a reading; the texts have "the palace of the high priest, who was called Caiaphas" (Matthew 26:3, 26:57) and Pilate the governor (27:2). Keep it, but as a reading.
- "the thunder and earthquake at the crucifixion" is wrong on the thunder. Matthew 27:51: "the veil of the temple was rent in twain from the top to the bottom; and the earth did quake, and the rocks rent." Mark and Luke have the darkness and the veil (Mark 15:33–38, Luke 23:44–45), no earthquake. John has none of it. The only thunder near the Passion in the Gospels is John 12:29, where the crowd hears a voice from heaven and "said that it thundered", days earlier. The poem's "reverberation / Of thunder of spring" is Eliot's own.
- "It is Good Friday, told in the past tense by people who were there" and "No Easter follows" are readings. The second is fair (the section's Christ is dead and the Emmaus stranger is never recognised); say it as a reading.
- Headnote quoted correctly.
- "He wrote the section at Lausanne in a few weeks": right. Eliot was at the Hotel Ste Luce, Lausanne, from 22 November 1921 to 2 January 1922, under Vittoz, and Part V was written there; Parts I and II were drafted in London and Part III at Margate and Lausanne.
- "Pound barely touched it": right in substance. The reviewer of the facsimile in the Spectator (1 January 1972) wrote that Part V "has hardly a Poundian mark upon it. At most he nips out the redundant articles from 'The sweat is dry and the feet are in the sand'". The words "OK from here on I think" I could not verify online (see "Could not verify"); the phrase is widely quoted from the facsimile but I cannot give the page. Until someone checks the facsimile, say what is certain: Pound left Part V almost untouched.
- "Eliot told Bertrand Russell in October 1923 that Part V was 'not only the best part, but the only part that justifies the whole, at all'": the date is right (15 October 1923). The words are "It gives me very great pleasure to know that you like the Waste Land, and especially Part V which in my opinion is not only the best part, but the only part which justifies the whole, at all" (quoted from the Letters in Leithauser, "Bertrand Russell and T. S. Eliot: their dialogue"). Change "that" to "which".
Missing: The facsimile prints an earlier fragment, "After the turning of the inspired days", in which "the torchlight red on sweaty faces", the frosty gardens and the crying already stand; lines 322–330 are built from it. I could not check the facsimile pages online (see "Could not verify"), but if the drafts record is going to say that some passages predate the poem, this is the one a reader of line 322 wants. Add only once confirmed against the facsimile (or Ricks and McCue, who print the fragment).
Proposed text:
Lines 322–330 follow the Passion without naming it: the torches of the arrest (John 18:3, "lanterns and torches and weapons"), the garden and the agony (Luke 22:39–46), the trial, the crucifixion. The earthquake is Matthew's (27:51); no Gospel has thunder at the crucifixion, and the "thunder of spring" is the poem's own. No resurrection follows. Eliot's headnote names the section's three themes: "the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous (see Miss Weston's book) and the present decay of eastern Europe." He wrote Part V at Lausanne between 22 November 1921 and 2 January 1922, and Pound, who had cut Parts III and IV hard, left it almost untouched. Eliot wrote to Bertrand Russell on 15 October 1923 that Part V was "not only the best part, but the only part which justifies the whole, at all."

### gloss:no-water
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- The counts hold: lines 331–359 are twenty-nine lines; in the 1922 text they carry no punctuation but hyphens and no full stop; "rock" occurs nine times and "water" eleven.
- "It is the road to Emmaus, the approach to the Grail chapel through a waste land": these two are Eliot's headnote. "the retreat through the Carpathians of a beaten army" is nobody's that I can find; it is not in the headnote, the notes, or Hesse, and no critic I know of says Carpathians. Cut it or name its source.
- "the poem's only stretch of pure longing" is the site's reading, stated as fact.
- Eliot's note is quoted correctly ("which I have heard in Quebec County"; Chapman on "purity and sweetness of tone"). Chapman's Handbook of Birds of Eastern North America (1895), p. 400, entry "759b. Turdus aonalaschkae pallasii (Cab.). Hermit Thrush", confirmed: "it is most at home in secluded woodland and thickety retreats"; "Its notes are not remarkable for variety or volume, but in purity and sweetness of tone and exquisite modulation they are unequaled."
- But "water-dripping song" is not Chapman's. The phrase occurs nowhere in the Handbook (checked the full text). Eliot's note presents it as celebrated; a Notes and Queries article (March 2014, "Did Eliot Mis-ascribe 'the water-dripping song' in The Waste Land?") finds it in no ornithological writing and reports that the hermit-thrush's song, a series of flute-like phrases, does not sound like dripping water. So "Listen to a recording and the poem's onomatopoeia turns out to be exact" is contradicted by the ornithology and should go. What can be said: the note's phrase is Eliot's, not the field guide's.
- Whitman: "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd" confirmed ("Solitary the thrush, / The hermit withdrawn to himself"; "Song of the bleeding throat"; "he sang the carol of death"). The connection with lines 2 and 357 is a critics' reading, not Eliot's note; Eliot wrote in 1928 that he "did not read Whitman until much later in life" (introduction to Pound's Selected Poems). Attribute the reading to commentators and let the reader weigh it.
- "the most personal in the apparatus" is opinion; harmless, but it is the voice.
Missing: the year and page of Chapman (1895, p. 400); that Eliot's copy was a gift from his mother on his fourteenth birthday, 1902 (his own later inscription, quoted on the Eliot estate's Gloucester page), which is what makes the note personal.
Proposed text:
Lines 331–359 have no punctuation but hyphens and no full stop. Two words carry them: "rock" nine times, "water" eleven. Eliot's headnote gives the journey to Emmaus and the approach to the Chapel Perilous through a waste land; the dry mouth of line 339 is the poem's own. Lines 346–358 turn to a sound of water that is not there, and end on the hermit-thrush.

Eliot's note names the bird, <i>Turdus aonalaschkae pallasii</i>, "which I have heard in Quebec County", and quotes Chapman's <i>Handbook of Birds of Eastern North America</i> (1895), p. 400, on the "purity and sweetness of tone" of its song. Eliot's copy of Chapman was a birthday present from his mother in 1902. The phrase "water-dripping song", which the note calls "justly celebrated", is not in Chapman; the hermit-thrush's song is a series of flute-like phrases, and ornithologists do not describe it as dripping water. Commentators also hear behind the line the hermit-thrush of Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd", the elegy behind line 2, which sings "the carol of death" in the swamp. Eliot said in 1928 that he did not read Whitman until late.

### gloss:third
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Luke 24 confirmed: "that same day" (24:13), i.e. the day of the resurrection, "to day is the third day" (24:21); "their eyes were holden that they should not know him" (24:16); "he expounded unto them in all the scriptures" (24:27); "he took bread, and blessed it, and brake, and gave to them. And their eyes were opened, and they knew him; and he vanished out of their sight" (24:30–31). The summary is accurate.
- Shackleton confirmed, South (1919), chapter X, "Across South Georgia": "I know that during that long and racking march of thirty-six hours over the unnamed mountains and glaciers of South Georgia it seemed to me often that we were four, not three. I said nothing to my companions on the point, but afterwards Worsley said to me, 'Boss, I had a curious feeling on the march that there was another person with us.' Crean confessed to the same idea." The crossing began at 2 a.m. on Friday 19 May 1916 and ended at Stromness on the 20th. "May 1916", "Shackleton, Worsley and Crean", "end of the Endurance expedition" all right. "each of the three men privately felt a fourth" is a fair paraphrase.
- "The hooded figure ... is the Hanged Man that Madame Sosostris could not find, and also Tiresias, and also nobody." Three claims of identity, of unequal standing. The Hanged Man is Eliot's own association, in the note on line 46: "I associate him with the hooded figure in the passage of the disciples to Emmaus in Part V." Tiresias is a critics' suggestion (the line "I do not know whether a man or a woman" recalls the seer of two sexes) and has no note behind it. "Nobody" is a reading of line 361. Say which is which.
- "as in the Grail castle, not answered": in the Grail story the fault is that the question is not asked; here it is asked and not answered. The inversion is a reading; fine if marked.
Missing: the note on line 46 should be quoted, since it is Eliot's only word on who the third is.
Proposed text:
Luke 24:13–31: on the day of the resurrection two disciples walking to Emmaus are joined by a stranger, "but their eyes were holden that they should not know him"; he expounds the scriptures to them, breaks bread, and "vanished out of their sight". Eliot's note gives the second source from memory ("I forget which, but I think one of Shackleton's"): the thirty-six-hour crossing of South Georgia by Shackleton, Worsley and Crean on 19–20 May 1916, at the end of the <i>Endurance</i> expedition, told in <i>South</i> (1919): "it seemed to me often that we were four, not three." Worsley and Crean told him afterwards that they had felt the same. Eliot's note on line 46 says he associates the Hanged Man of the Tarot "with the hooded figure in the passage of the disciples to Emmaus". Some readers also hear Tiresias in "I do not know whether a man or a woman"; that is a reading, not a note. The question of line 360 is not answered.

### gloss:hordes
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Hesse, Blick ins Chaos (Bern: Seldwyla, 1920): the title page reads "Drei Aufsätze". The three are "Die Brüder Karamasoff oder Der Untergang Europas", "Gedanken zu Dostojewskis 'Idiot'", and "Gespräch über die Neutöner", a dialogue about the young poets (checked in the 1920 text on archive.org). So "three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky" is right. The passage Eliot quotes is the close of the Karamazov essay; Hesse's text reads "in heiligem Wahn" and "Über"; Eliot's "im heiligem" is his misprint, kept in Poems 1909–1925.
- In Sight of Chaos, translated by Stephen Hudson (Sydney Schiff), Zurich: Seldwyla, 1923, contains the two Dostoevsky essays only. Say so if the English title is given.
- "Eliot admired the book enough to visit Hesse at Montagnola in May 1922": Eliot wrote to Hesse on 13 March 1922 praising the book and visited him at Montagnola, near Lugano, on 28 May 1922 (Letters, vol. I, p. 509 n., as reported on Rickard Parker's Hesse page; I did not see the Letters themselves). "and to print him in the first number of The Criterion that October, alongside the poem": right; the piece was "Recent German Poetry" (Criterion I.1, October 1922), a translation of the third essay.
- "The 'hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains' are the refugees and armies of the collapse of the Russian and Austro-Hungarian empires": the headnote says "the present decay of eastern Europe" and the note quotes Hesse on "the half of eastern Europe ... on the way to chaos"; the empires are the site's gloss. State it as the usual reading of the headnote.
- "the 'maternal lamentation' is the women at the cross ('Daughters of Jerusalem, weep not for me,' Luke 23:28) and every mother in Europe in 1919": Luke 23:27–28 confirmed ("women, which also bewailed and lamented him ... Daughters of Jerusalem, weep not for me"). The identification is a reading; "every mother in Europe in 1919" is rhetoric.
- "the cities that were each in turn the centre of the world" is a reading (Vienna especially). "the word from line 60 now standing on its own": "Unreal City" is lines 60 and 207; fine.
- The quote field reproduces Eliot's misprint "im heiligem"; the library record corrects it. Either is defensible; be consistent about which text the quote field is.
- The translation "singing drunken hymns" compresses "singt betrunken und hymnisch" (sings drunkenly and hymn-like); acceptable, but "sings, drunk and hymn-like, as Dmitri Karamazov sang" is closer.
Missing: nothing beyond the above.
Proposed text:
Hermann Hesse's <i>Blick ins Chaos</i> (Bern, 1920) is three essays, two on Dostoevsky and one on the new poets. Eliot's note quotes the close of the first, "The Brothers Karamazov, or the Downfall of Europe"; Stephen Hudson's English version, <i>In Sight of Chaos</i> (Zurich, 1923), has the two Dostoevsky essays. Eliot wrote to Hesse in March 1922 to praise the book, visited him at Montagnola on 28 May 1922, and printed him ("Recent German Poetry") in the first number of <i>The Criterion</i>, October 1922, beside <i>The Waste Land</i>. The headnote gives "the present decay of eastern Europe" as the section's third theme, and the "hooded hordes" are usually read with it. "Maternal lamentation" has been read with the women who "bewailed and lamented" Christ on the way to the cross (Luke 23:27–28). "Jerusalem Athens Alexandria / Vienna London": five cities, then "Unreal", the word of lines 60 and 207, now with no city after it.

### gloss:bats
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "These eight lines predate the rest of the poem. They come from a fragment in Eliot's papers that begins 'So through the evening, through the violet air,' printed in the facsimile edition": the fragment exists and is printed in the facsimile (pp. 112–15 as usually cited) and by Ricks and McCue; its images (the woman fiddling on her hair, the bats with baby faces, the towers upside down, the bells, the voices from cisterns and wells) are the source of lines 378–385. I could not reach the facsimile to confirm its date; Valerie Eliot placed it among the early fragments, and the usual dating is 1914 or thereabouts, but that needs the page (see "Could not verify"). "Predate the rest of the poem" overstates: other passages also predate 1921 (see draft:ok-from-here).
- "bells that 'kept the hours' like St Mary Woolnoth's": line 67 confirmed ("Saint Mary Woolnoth kept the hours").
- "which is Jeremiah's 'broken cisterns, that can hold no water'": Jeremiah 2:13 confirmed in those words. But "is" claims an identity; the echo is a reading, and I know of no note of Eliot's for it. Write "recalls".
- "The violet hour of Part III has become 'violet light'": lines 215 and 220, fine.
- "The poem's phrase for the whole passage would be ... 'withered stumps of time'": line 104; the application is the site's.
- "Gothic and painterly" is the voice.
Missing: nothing a reader needs beyond a sourced date for the fragment.
Proposed text:
Lines 378–385 rework an earlier fragment that begins "So through the evening, through the violet air", printed in the 1971 facsimile among the poems that fed the drafts; the woman fiddling on her hair, the bats with baby faces, the inverted towers, the bells and the voices from cisterns and wells are already there. "Kept the hours" repeats line 67, where Saint Mary Woolnoth kept them; the "violet hour" of lines 215 and 220 becomes "violet light". "Empty cisterns and exhausted wells" recalls Jeremiah 2:13, "broken cisterns, that can hold no water"; that is a reading, not one of Eliot's notes.

### gloss:chapel
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Weston, chapter XIII, "The Perilous Chapel", confirmed: "sometimes there is a Dead Body laid on the altar; sometimes a Black Hand extinguishes the tapers; there are strange and threatening voices"; Gawain "is overtaken by a terrible storm" and takes shelter; the black hand comes through the window and puts out the taper "while a voice makes lamentation loud and dire". The summary is accurate. "survive it, and he is fit for the Grail" compresses Weston's argument that the chapel is the test of initiation; acceptable.
- "Eliot's chapel is worse than perilous ... the test already over" is a reading (a common one); mark it.
- "Co co rico" as the French cock-crow (cocorico): right.
- "the cock that crowed for Peter's denial": Matthew 26:74–75 ("immediately the cock crew"); Mark 14:72 has it twice. "the 'bird of dawning' that sends ghosts back to their graves in the first scene of Hamlet": Hamlet I.i, Horatio, "The cock, that is the trumpet to the morn"; Marcellus, "The bird of dawning singeth all night long; / And then, they say, no spirit dare stir abroad". Both are commentators' associations, not Eliot's notes; say so.
- "'a damp gust / Bringing rain.' Three words.": "Bringing rain" is two words; the whole is five. The count is wrong, and the water path (395) says "two words". Drop the counting.
- "the first rain in the poem since the shower over the Starnbergersee at line 9": line 9 is "With a shower of rain" (the lake is line 8); line 4 has "spring rain". So "first rain since line 9" is right. But the poem does not say the rain falls: "Bringing rain", then "the limp leaves / Waited for rain" (396–397) and "the arid plain" (425). Say that.
Missing: that the rain is brought, not shown falling.
Proposed text:
Weston's chapter XIII, "The Perilous Chapel", gathers the adventure in which the hero, sheltering in a chapel from a storm, finds a dead body on the altar or sees a black hand put out the tapers and hears "strange and threatening voices"; she reads it as a test of initiation. Eliot's chapel is empty and windowless, with a swinging door and dry bones. "Co co rico" is the French cock-crow. Commentators set beside it the cock that crowed at Peter's denial (Matthew 26:74) and the cock of <i>Hamlet</i> I.i, "the trumpet to the morn", at whose crowing "no spirit dare stir abroad". Then "a damp gust / Bringing rain": the first rain since the shower of line 9. The poem does not say that it falls; at line 397 the leaves are still waiting.

### gloss:ganga
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Ganga is the Ganges; Himavant (Himavat, "having snow") is the personified Himalaya, and in the Ramayana (Bala Kanda) and the Puranas he is the father of Ganga and of Parvati. "the mountain father of the goddess Ganga in the Puranas" is right.
- "a language Eliot had studied at Harvard for two years under Charles Lanman": confirmed by Eliot himself, After Strange Gods (1934): "Two years spent in the study of Sanskrit under Charles Lanman, and a year in the mazes of Patanjali's metaphysics under the guidance of James Woods, left me in a state of enlightened mystification." The gloss quotes the last phrase correctly. Note the year of Patanjali was under Woods, not Lanman; the gloss does not say otherwise but could be read so.
- "never pretended to command" is the site's gloss on that sentence; "the whole subcontinent crouched" is the voice.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
Ganga is the Ganges; Himavant is the Himalaya, in the Ramayana and the Puranas the mountain who is Ganga's father. The scene moves from the chapel to India before the monsoon breaks. Eliot studied Sanskrit at Harvard for two years (1911–13) under Charles Lanman. In <i>After Strange Gods</i> (1934) he wrote that those years, and a year of "Patanjali's metaphysics" under James Woods, "left me in a state of enlightened mystification".

### gloss:da
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- The passage is Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad V.2 (the second Brāhmaṇa of the fifth Adhyāya). Confirmed in Max Müller's translation (Sacred Books of the East, vol. XV, 1884): "The threefold descendants of Prajâpati, gods, men, and Asuras (evil spirits), dwelt as Brahmakârins (students) with their father Prajâpati. Having finished their studentship the gods said: 'Tell us (something), Sir.' He told them the syllable Da. Then he said: 'Did you understand?' They said: 'We did understand. You told us "Dâmyata," Be subdued.' ... men ... "Datta," Give ... Asuras ... "Dayadham," Be merciful ... The divine voice of thunder repeats the same, Da Da Da, that is, Be subdued, Give, Be merciful." Eliot's note says "5, 1", in 1922 and still in Poems 1909–1925; the note's number is a slip. The order in the Upanishad (gods, men, demons: control, give, sympathise) and Eliot's reordering are as the gloss says.
- "Eliot's note gives Deussen's German translation as his source" is not what the note says. The note names the Upanishad as where "the fable of the meaning of the Thunder is found" and adds "A translation is found in Deussen's Sechzig Upanishads des Veda, p. 489." Deussen is offered as a translation, not as the source.
- "paraphrased here from the Sanskrit": by whom? If the site made the paraphrase, say so, or give Müller.
- "each hearer understands the instruction he most needs": that is the traditional commentary's reading (Śaṅkara: the gods self-indulgent, men greedy, the demons cruel); it is not in the text itself. Attribute or soften.
- "each word is followed by a confession ... of how the instruction has been failed": a common reading; mark it.
- "The section's title, and the poem's ending, come from a fable about hearing what you are able to hear": the bow.
Missing: the slip "5, 1" for V.2; Müller as the English source of the wording.
Proposed text:
<i>Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad</i> V.2. Eliot's note says "5, 1"; the fable is the second section of the fifth chapter. Gods, men and demons ask Prajāpati for teaching. To each he says "Da", and each hears a different word: the gods <i>dāmyata</i>, restrain yourselves; men <i>datta</i>, give; the demons <i>dayadhvam</i>, be compassionate. "The divine voice of thunder repeats the same, Da Da Da" (Max Müller's translation, 1884). The commentators' explanation, that each hears the command it most needs, is not in the text. Eliot's note glosses the three as "Give, sympathise, control" and adds that a translation is in Deussen's <i>Sechzig Upanishads des Veda</i> (1897), p. 489. The poem takes the words in the order <i>datta, dayadhvam, damyata</i>, and what follows each has usually been read as an admission of failure.

### gloss:datta
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Webster, The White Devil, V.vi confirmed (Gutenberg text): after the stage direction "Flamineo riseth", "O men, / That lie upon your death-beds, and are haunted / With howling wives! ne'er trust them; they'll re-marry / Ere the worm pierce your winding-sheet, ere the spider / Make a thin curtain for your epitaphs." Act, scene, speaker and gist ("how quickly widows remarry") are right; Eliot's note gives "V, vi".
- "the will read out by 'the lean solicitor' after the seals are broken": a reading of line 409; fair, but say "the will opened after a death" rather than "read out".
- "This is the poem's one statement about what a life is for, and it is made in the vocabulary of probate": the site's reading, and the bow. Cut or mark.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
"The awful daring of a moment's surrender" is the one thing given, and "by this, and this only, we have existed". It is not in the obituaries, nor in "memories draped by the beneficent spider", which Eliot's note refers to Flamineo in Webster's <i>The White Devil</i>, V.vi, on widows who remarry "ere the spider / Make a thin curtain for your epitaphs", nor "under seals broken by the lean solicitor", the will opened after a death.

### gloss:dayadhvam
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Inferno XXXIII.46–48 confirmed (Wikisource, Petrocchi text): "e io senti' chiavar l'uscio di sotto / a l'orribile torre; ond'io guardai / nel viso a' mie' figliuoi sanza far motto." Eliot's note quotes the older reading "ed io sentii ... all'orribile torre". "chiavar" can mean to nail up (chiavo, nail) or to lock (chiave, key); translators divide, and Eliot's "key" takes the second. The gloss's "nailing up" is one reading; give both.
- Ugolino was imprisoned in 1288 with two sons and two grandsons; Dante calls them all "figliuoi". "sons and grandsons" is right.
- Bradley, Appearance and Reality (1893), p. 346 confirmed: the words Eliot quotes are on that page in the archive.org copy ("My external sensations are no less private to myself than are my thoughts or my feelings. In either case my experience falls within my own circle, a circle closed on the outside; and, with all its elements alike, every sphere is opaque to the others which surround it ... In brief, regarded as an existence which appears in a soul, the whole world for each is peculiar and private to that soul"). "the philosopher on whom he wrote his Harvard doctoral thesis": right (completed 1916, published 1964 as Knowledge and Experience in the Philosophy of F. H. Bradley).
- "A broken Coriolanus, Shakespeare's general who could not bend": a gloss; fine.
- "Sympathy is the instruction; solitude is the report": the bow.
Missing: the two senses of "chiavar".
Proposed text:
<i>Inferno</i> XXXIII.46–48: Ugolino, shut in the tower at Pisa with his sons and grandsons to starve, hears the door below "chiavar", nailed up or locked; the verb allows both, and Eliot's "key" takes the second. Eliot's note adds F. H. Bradley, <i>Appearance and Reality</i> (1893), p. 346, on experience as "a circle closed on the outside"; Eliot's Harvard dissertation (1916) was on Bradley. The key is thought of, not turned. "A broken Coriolanus" is Shakespeare's general, revived "for a moment" by "aetherial rumours".

### gloss:damyata
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "the poem's only memory of things going well": a reading (the hyacinth garden is another candidate); say "the one passage in which something answers as it should", or mark it.
- "Eliot had sailed off Gloucester and Cape Ann as a boy, and the Dry Salvages of his later poem lie off that coast": the Eliots summered at Eastern Point, Gloucester, and he sailed there; the Dry Salvages are off Cape Ann. Right. "as a boy" and "the summers of his boyhood" (path eliot/419) are fair; he sailed there into his twenties too.
- "Would have." The fragment is the voice; the observation (the sentence is conditional) is right.
Missing: the draft read "your heart responded"; the conditional "would have responded" is Eliot's revision, and two lines after "To controlling hands" were cut (facsimile, typescript of Part V). This is the fact a reader of these lines wants and it is missing. I could not reach the facsimile to confirm the words; add once confirmed (Ricks and McCue's textual notes will have it).
Proposed text:
The third command is answered by a boat that "responded / Gaily, to the hand expert with sail and oar". Eliot sailed off Gloucester and Cape Ann in his youth; the Dry Salvages of his later poem lie off that coast. Then the tense changes: "your heart would have responded". [If confirmed against the facsimile: The typescript read "your heart responded"; the conditional is Eliot's revision.]

### gloss:fisher-king
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, the wounded keeper of the Grail who fishes because he can do nothing else": this is Chrétien's explanation, and Weston's chapter IX rejects it. Weston: in Chrétien and Wolfram "the name is connected with his partiality for fishing, an obviously post hoc addition"; the redactors "were at pains to obscure" the origin "by the introduction of the obviously post hoc 'motif' ... that he was called the Fisher King because of his devotion to the pastime of fishing". Her argument is that the title comes from the fish as an ancient life-symbol. A note that sends the reader to Weston's chapter should not summarise it as saying what it denies.
- "while his land lies waste behind him": Weston's chapter II ("The Task of the Hero") and V connect the king's wound with the land's waste; fine.
- Isaiah 38:1 confirmed: "Thus saith the LORD, Set thine house in order: for thou shalt die, and not live." Hezekiah "turned his face toward the wall" (38:2), "wept sore" (38:3), and was given fifteen years (38:5). The poem says "lands", not "house"; write "echoes", and the echo is a commentators' identification (Southam and others), not one of Eliot's notes.
- "It is also what a man does with his papers" and "From here to the end the poem stops narrating and starts stacking" are the voice.
Missing: what Weston's chapter actually argues.
Proposed text:
Eliot's note sends the reader to Weston's chapter IX, "The Fisher King". Weston argues that the title comes from the fish as an ancient symbol of life, and dismisses the romances' explanation, that the wounded king fishes for pastime because he cannot ride, as "an obviously post hoc addition". Line 426 echoes Isaiah 38:1, the prophet's message to the dying king Hezekiah: "Set thine house in order: for thou shalt die, and not live." Hezekiah turned his face to the wall, wept, and was given fifteen more years. The poem says "lands", not "house", and gets no answer.

### gloss:fragments
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- London Bridge: the rhyme was first printed c. 1744 (Tommy Thumb's Pretty Song Book), where the materials fail and a man is set to watch; "wood and clay, bricks and mortar" are in later versions (Halliwell, 1842). "the folklorists' reading that Frazer would have relished, burying someone in the foundations" is Alice Gomme's (Traditional Games, 1894–98), taken up by the Opies; name her rather than Frazer. Line 62 confirmed ("A crowd flowed over London Bridge").
- Purgatorio XXVI confirmed: Arnaut's speech is 140–147 in Provençal, "Ara vos prec" is 145, and 148 is "Poi s'ascose nel foco che li affina" (modern text; Eliot's "gli" is an older reading). "the Comedy's one passage in a vernacular other than Italian": right. "Ara Vos Prec" (1920) right. "He goes willingly" is a reading; fine. "Set this fire against the burning of the Fire Sermon" is an instruction.
- Pervigilium Veneris: dating "second to fourth century" is the usual range. The last stanza confirmed: "illa cantat, nos tacemus. quando ver venit meum? / quando fiam uti chelidon, ut tacere desinam?" (modern texts; the 1848 edition on Wikisource reads "ut chelidon" and "ver venit novum", so "the Latin poem has 'uti'" is true of the standard text, not of every edition). "The 1922 printings have 'ceu' ... and Eliot's later editions restored it": every printing from 1922 to the 1932 American Poems 1909–1925 reads "ceu" (I checked the 1925 Poems: "Quando fiam ceu chelidon"), and so do Eliot's autograph and typescript fair copies; "uti" first appears in Collected Poems 1909–1935 (1936), and Rainey doubts the change was Eliot's (Annotated Waste Land, quoted at languagehat.com). Give the edition and the doubt.
- Tennyson confirmed: The Princess, "O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South". Swinburne's "Itylus" ("Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow") is the other text usually cited; worth a clause.
- Nerval: "El Desdichado" was first printed in Le Mousquetaire, 10 December 1853, then in Les Chimères (1854); "1854" is fine. Line 2 confirmed. The translation is fair. "The Tower is a Tarot card (XVI, struck by lightning)": the card exists, but the link to Nerval's "tour abolie" is a commentators' association (the library record rightly says "often read as"); the gloss states it as if it followed. Mark it. "twice crossed the Acheron" confirmed.
- "These fragments ... quotations propped like timbers against a wall that is coming down": the voice.
- Kyd confirmed: IV.i, Hieronimo, "Why then, I'll fit you; say no more" (Eliot gives it in the old spelling, "Why then Ile fit you"); the play is assigned "in unknown languages ... you, my lord, in Latin, I in Greek, / You in Italian, and ... in courtly French shall all her phrases be": four languages; the printed note says it was "in sundry languages"; Lorenzo and Balthazar are killed in the performance. "'Hieronimo is mad again' was the subtitle on the 1615 title page": right, "The Spanish Tragedie: or, Hieronimo is mad againe" (1615, for I. White and T. Langley), the first edition to carry it. The pun "I'll oblige / I'll pay you out" is right.
- "A poem in seven languages, written as revenge": the count depends on what is counted. The text of the poem has six (English, German, French, Italian, Latin, Sanskrit); Greek is in the epigraph; Provençal is in the notes. "written as revenge" is unsupported by anything Eliot said. Cut both.
- Shantih: Philippians 4:7 ("the peace of God, which passeth all understanding"); Eliot's words are the Anglican blessing's. "the 1922 note called the English 'a feeble translation' and later notes 'our equivalent'": right, and the change is already in Poems 1909–1925 (1925): "'The Peace which passeth understanding' is our equivalent to this word." Give the date. "neither should you on a first reading": an instruction to the reader.
Missing: the edition of the "uti" change; Gomme's name; the 1925 date for the revised note.
Proposed text:
[Pervigilium paragraph] <b>Quando fiam ceu chelidon.</b> "When shall I become like the swallow?" From the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>, an anonymous Latin poem of the second to fourth century for the eve of Venus's spring festival. Its last stanza turns from the nightingale to the poet's own silence: "illa cantat, nos tacemus. quando ver venit meum? / quando fiam uti chelidon, ut tacere desinam?" Every printing from 1922 to 1932, and Eliot's fair copies, read "ceu" (also "like"); "uti" first appears in <i>Collected Poems 1909–1935</i> (1936), and Rainey doubts the change was Eliot's. "O swallow swallow" is Tennyson's, from <i>The Princess</i> ("O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South"); Swinburne's "Itylus" ("Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow") is also cited. Eliot's note adds "Cf. Philomela in Parts II and III."

[Nerval paragraph] <b>Le Prince d'Aquitaine à la tour abolie.</b> The second line of Nerval's sonnet "El Desdichado" (first printed December 1853; in <i>Les Chimères</i>, 1854): "I am the dark one, the widower, the unconsoled, the Prince of Aquitaine with the ruined tower." Some commentators read the tower with the Tarot's Tower, struck by lightning; Nerval does not say so. The sonnet's speaker has "twice crossed the Acheron".

[Kyd paragraph] <b>Why then Ile fit you. Hieronymo's mad againe.</b> Kyd's <i>The Spanish Tragedy</i> (c. 1587), IV.i. Hieronimo, whose son has been murdered, is asked by the murderers for a court entertainment and answers "Why then, I'll fit you": suit you, and punish you. The play he gives them is acted in four languages, Latin, Greek, Italian and French, and in it the murderers are stabbed in earnest. "Hieronimo is mad againe" is the subtitle first printed on the title page of the 1615 quarto. Six languages stand in the text of <i>The Waste Land</i> (English, German, French, Italian, Latin, Sanskrit), with Greek in the epigraph and Provençal in the notes.

[Shantih paragraph] <b>Shantih shantih shantih.</b> Eliot's note: "Repeated as here, a formal ending to an Upanishad." "The Peace which passeth understanding" is Philippians 4:7 in the words of the Anglican blessing. The 1922 note called it "a feeble translation of the content of this word"; from <i>Poems 1909–1925</i> (1925) the note reads "our equivalent to this word".

**The scholar, round 1**

## C2. Ways

### way:Eliot reading the thunder
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Eliot recorded the poem in 1933 (Columbia) and in 1946 (Library of Congress; a third, 1947, at the National Gallery). "Eliot's own recordings" is right.
- "the 'DA' is spoken flat, without drama, and 'Shantih' three times as a murmur": a description of performance I cannot check from here; if it stays, it is the site's ear, and a listener may disagree. Safer to say what the recordings are and let the reader listen.
Missing: the dates of the recordings.
Proposed text:
Eliot recorded the poem in 1933 and again in 1946. Listen to the last thirty lines before reading the notes on them.

### way:Water at last
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "The section is the poem's longest drought and its only rain": not the only rain. Line 4 has "spring rain" and line 9 "With a shower of rain". The "twenty-nine lines of rock" count (331–359) is right, but the rain at 395 is thirty-six lines after they end, not straight after.
- "Everything after that is wet": no. Line 397 "Waited for rain", line 425 "the arid plain". The poem says "Bringing rain" and does not show rain falling.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
The section is the poem's longest drought. The Water lens tints the twenty-nine lines of rock (331–359), the "damp gust / Bringing rain" at 395, the leaves that still wait for it at 397, the Ganges, the boat, and the "arid plain" at 425. It is the first rain since the shower of line 9, and the poem does not say it falls.

### way:Hear the hermit-thrush
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "Eliot's note names the bird and quotes the field guide": right (Chapman, p. 400).
- "His transcription is accurate": contradicted. The "water-dripping song" is not in Chapman, and the hermit-thrush's song, a series of flute-like phrases, is not heard by ornithologists as dripping water (Notes and Queries, March 2014). Whether "Drip drop drip drop drop drop drop" transcribes anything is for the listener; the way should not decide it.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
"Drip drop drip drop drop drop drop." Eliot's note names the bird and quotes Chapman's field guide on its song. Chapman does not call it "water-dripping"; that phrase is Eliot's. A recording is in the listening room. Play it, then read line 358, and judge.

### way:The road to Emmaus, and South Georgia
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "Shackleton's account of crossing South Georgia in 1916, when three men each felt a fourth": right (South, chapter X; 19–20 May 1916).
- "The hooded figure is a risen god and a hallucination of exhausted men, and the poem will not say which": the first is a reading (Luke, and Eliot's note on line 46 tying the Hanged Man to the hooded figure), the second is what Eliot's note says Shackleton's party had ("the constant delusion"). Fair as a summary of the two sources; "risen god" is stronger than the texts, which have a risen Christ and a Hanged God (Frazer) in Eliot's note. Say "the stranger on the road to Emmaus and the delusion of exhausted men".
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
Two texts stand under "Who is the third who walks always beside you?": Luke 24 and Shackleton's account in <i>South</i> of crossing South Georgia in May 1916, when each of three men felt a fourth. Read both. The stranger on the road to Emmaus and the delusion of exhausted men are both in the line, and the poem does not choose.

### way:Eight lines, six languages
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Lines 427–434 are eight lines. The languages in them are five: English (427, 431, 432, and half of 429), Italian (428), Latin (429), French (430), Sanskrit (433, 434). Provençal is only in Eliot's note. The body says as much ("with Provençal in the note") and the title contradicts it; the "first" path at 427 says "five languages". Make them agree: five.
- "as a noise made by a civilisation" is the voice, but it is the site's suggestion for a way of reading and can stay if the reader accepts it.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
[title] Eight lines, five languages
[body] The last eight lines are in English, Italian, Latin, French, English, English, Sanskrit, Sanskrit; Eliot's note on line 428 adds Provençal. Switch on Tongues, read the translations, then read the lines again without them.

### way:Unstack the fragments
Verdict: sound
Findings:
- No fact to fault. "the wall the poet says he propped them against" is the site's image on Eliot's own metaphor; harmless.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text: none

### way:What the thunder actually said
Verdict: sound
Findings:
- "one syllable, three hearers, three meanings": right (Upanishad V.2). "Eliot changes the order of the commands and confesses, after each, how it was not kept": the reordering is fact; "confesses ... how it was not kept" is the usual reading, and a way may put it so.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text: none

### way:From Margate to the Ganges
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "The section's places are Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna, London, the Ganges and the Himalaya": the text also has London Bridge (427), Aquitaine (430), and, in the notes, Quebec County, South Georgia, Emmaus, Pisa (Ugolino's tower).
- Margate is Part III (line 300), not Part V. If the way is about the whole map, say so; if about the section, drop Margate from the title.
- "draws the poem's journey east in a single line": Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna, London is not a journey east; it runs east to west and then jumps to the Ganges. Say what the map does, not what the sequence is not.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
The section names Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna and London in two lines, then the Ganges and the Himalaya, then London Bridge and Aquitaine; Eliot's notes add Quebec, South Georgia, Emmaus and Pisa. The world map shows them all.

### way:Every 'nothing'
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- The quotations are right: "I knew nothing" (40), "Nothing again nothing" (120), "Do you know nothing?" (121–122), "Nothing with nothing" (302), "expect / Nothing" (304–305).
- But the word does not occur in Part V. Its last appearance is line 305. "The pathway follows the word through the poem to its last appearance" is true of the pathway as a whole and misleading at the head of Part V, where the stop (402, "what have we given?") has no "nothing" in it.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
"I knew nothing", "Nothing again nothing", "Do you know nothing?", "Nothing with nothing", "expect / Nothing". The word is not said in Part V; the pathway ends at line 305 and comes here only to ask what the thunder's "what have we given?" answers.

### way:OK from here on
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "Pound's note at the head of the section reads 'OK from here on I think'": not verified (see "Could not verify"). "He cut almost nothing": supported (Spectator review of the facsimile, 1 January 1972: "hardly a Poundian mark upon it").
- "Eliot wrote it in a few weeks at Lausanne": 22 November 1921 to 2 January 1922. Right.
- "said afterwards it was 'the only part that justifies the whole, at all'": the letter (to Russell, 15 October 1923) reads "the only part which justifies the whole, at all".
Missing: to whom and when he said it.
Proposed text:
Pound left the typescript of Part V almost untouched. Eliot wrote it at Lausanne between late November 1921 and the New Year, and told Bertrand Russell in October 1923 that it was "the only part which justifies the whole, at all".

### way:The last voices
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- Nine voices are listed for "each of the last eight lines". If the Fisher King is line 426, the span is nine lines; if the last eight (427–434), drop him. Line 433 and 434 are both "an Upanishad" (or the thunder); "the poet himself" is 431 and, arguably, 432's "Why then Ile fit you". Nothing false; the count does not match.
Missing: nothing.
Proposed text:
A nursery rhyme, Dante, an anonymous Latin poet and Tennyson, Nerval, the poet, Kyd's Hieronimo, the thunder, an Upanishad. Voices colours the last eight lines differently. Read them as a chorus, then as one man.

### way:Peace, or the word for it
Verdict: fix
Findings:
- "Eliot's 1922 note called the English 'a feeble translation'": right; and from 1925 "our equivalent to this word". The rest is an instruction ("keep the one that lets you close the book"); the reader's business, but a way may propose a reading.
Missing: the 1925 revision, since the way rests on the 1922 wording.
Proposed text:
"Shantih shantih shantih." Eliot's note calls it "a formal ending to an Upanishad"; in 1922 he called "The Peace which passeth understanding" a feeble translation, and from 1925 "our equivalent". Read the last line once as a blessing and once as a man saying the word to himself.

**The scholar, round 1**

**The scholar, round 1**

## C4. Drafts

### draft:ok-from-here
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "At the head of Part V Pound wrote 'OK from here on I think,' and cut almost nothing": the cutting is confirmed by readers of the facsimile (Spectator, 1 January 1972: "hardly a Poundian mark upon it"); the words of the note I could not verify online (see "Could not verify").
- "Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne in November and December 1921": right (22 November 1921 to 2 January 1922).
- "and later said it had come 'in a trance'": I can find no statement of Eliot's with this word. What he said: in the 1931 essay on Pascal, that "some forms of illness are extremely favourable, not only to religious illumination, but to artistic and literary composition. A piece of writing meditated, apparently without progress, for months or years, may suddenly take shape and word; and in this state long passages may be produced which require little or no retouch", a passage critics (and Valerie Eliot, as usually reported) apply to Part V; and in the Paris Review interview (1959), "In The Waste Land, I wasn't even bothering whether I understood what I was saying", about the poem as a whole. "In a trance" appears in popular summaries (e.g. interestingliterature.com) as a paraphrase. Remove the quotation marks and say what he said.
- "Three of the poem's passages are older than the rest": an undercount. Besides the three named (the violet-air fragment for 378–385; "The Death of Saint Narcissus" (1915) for the red rock of lines 25–29; "Dans le Restaurant" (1918) for Phlebas), the facsimile prints "After the turning of the inspired days", from which lines 322–330 are built, and "The Death of the Duchess", from which the opening of the dialogue in Part II is built. I could not check the facsimile pages online; Ricks and McCue print both fragments. Confirm and count them, or say "several".
- "The Death of Saint Narcissus (1915)": set in type for Poetry in 1915 and withdrawn; fine. "Dans le Restaurant (1918)": Little Review, September 1918; fine.
- The 1971 facsimile is in copyright; the record quotes only first lines, which is within the rule.
Missing: the fragment behind lines 322–330; that Part V's manuscript is in Eliot's hand (pencil) as well as typescript, which is why the facsimile shows two states of it (to confirm against the facsimile).
Proposed text:
Pound left the typescript of Part V almost untouched; his cuts fell on Parts III and IV. Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne between 22 November 1921 and 2 January 1922. In 1931, in an essay on Pascal, he wrote that in some forms of illness "long passages may be produced which require little or no retouch"; readers have applied the sentence to Part V, though he did not. Several passages of the poem are older than 1921. The woman with the long black hair and the bats with baby faces (lines 378–385) come from a fragment beginning "So through the evening, through the violet air"; the opening of Part V draws on another, "After the turning of the inspired days"; the red rock of Part I came from "The Death of Saint Narcissus" (1915), and Phlebas from "Dans le Restaurant" (1918).

### draft:manuscript-fate
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "Eliot gave the typescript, with Pound's and Vivien's pencil on it, to John Quinn ... in 1922; Quinn insisted on paying $140 for it": the $140 was not for The Waste Land. Eliot offered Quinn the manuscript as a gift, and separately the notebook of early poems (the "Inventions of the March Hare" notebook, with loose leaves); Quinn accepted the Waste Land papers as a gift on condition that he could buy the notebook, which he did, for $140 (Menand, NYRB, 15 May 1997, drawing on Ricks's edition of the notebook). The parcel was posted on 23 October 1922 and reached Quinn on 13 January 1923. "who had handled his American publishing": Quinn negotiated the Boni and Liveright contract; fine.
- "After Quinn's death in 1924 it vanished": it passed to his sister, Julia Anderson, and then to her daughter, Mary Conroy (Mrs Thomas F. Conroy), who found it in storage in the early 1950s. "Vanished" is what the world thought, not what happened; say "was thought lost".
- "His niece sold it to the New York Public Library in 1958, and the library announced the acquisition in 1968": right (Berg Collection; sale 1958; announced October 1968; Valerie Eliot was told in April 1968). "three years after Eliot's death": Eliot died 4 January 1965; nearly four years.
- "Valerie Eliot's facsimile edition of 1971 printed every page": fine.
- "the reason the poem is 434 lines long and not 800": the 800 is Eliot's own figure, in a recorded conversation with Leslie Paul in 1958 ("reduced a mess of some eight hundred lines to about half its size"); in his 1946 essay on Pound he said "reduced to about half its size". Attribute the figure to him; the drafts with the discarded poems run longer.
Missing: whose figure 800 is; the notebook.
Proposed text:
In October 1922 Eliot sent the drafts, with Pound's and Vivien's pencil on them, to John Quinn, the New York lawyer and collector who had negotiated his American contract, as a gift; Quinn accepted them on condition that he could buy the notebook of Eliot's early poems, for which he paid $140. After Quinn's death in 1924 the papers passed to his sister and then to her daughter, Mary Conroy, and were thought lost. She found them in storage in the early 1950s and sold them to the Berg Collection of the New York Public Library in 1958; the library announced the acquisition in October 1968, nearly four years after Eliot's death. Valerie Eliot's facsimile edition of 1971 printed every page. Eliot said in 1958 that Pound had "reduced a mess of some eight hundred lines to about half its size".

**The scholar, round 1**

**The scholar, round 1**

## Could not verify

- Pound's note at the head of Part V, "OK from here on I think": not found in any text I could reach. The facsimile (1971) is not online. The Spectator review of the facsimile (1 January 1972) confirms only that Part V "has hardly a Poundian mark upon it" and that Pound removed articles from line 337.
- That Eliot said Part V came "in a trance": no such words of Eliot's found. The documented statements are the 1931 Pascal essay ("little or no retouch") and the 1959 Paris Review remark. I could not confirm that Valerie Eliot applies the Pascal passage to Part V in the facsimile introduction, though it is usually reported so.
- The date of the fragment "So through the evening, through the violet air" (and of "After the turning of the inspired days"): the facsimile pages (usually cited as 108–15) and Ricks and McCue were not reachable. The fragments' existence and content I am confident of; the date should be taken from the facsimile before it is printed.
- The draft reading "your heart responded" for line 421, and the two lines cut after 423: from memory of the facsimile; confirm before use.
- Eliot's Temple Classics Dante reading "gli affina".
- That the Bṛhadāraṇyaka was among the texts Eliot read with Lanman in 1911–13 (his Harvard courses were elementary Sanskrit and Pali).
- Whether Southam (or Grover Smith) is the first to cite Isaiah 38:1, Jeremiah 2:13, Hamlet I.i and Whitman's thrush; I could not open the books. These are standard identifications, and the notes may say "commentators" safely.
- Who first proposed the Tarot Tower for Nerval's "tour abolie".
- The manner of Eliot's reading of "DA" and "Shantih" in the 1933 and 1946 recordings.
- That Hesse's Criterion piece "Recent German Poetry" is a translation of "Gespräch über die Neutöner": reported by Rickard Parker's site from the Letters (vol. I, pp. 509–11, 542–43); I did not see the Letters.
- The exact channel of the Russell quotation: I have it from Leithauser's article quoting the Letters, not from the Letters themselves; the wording ("which justifies") is hers from the printed letter.

**The scholar, round 1**
