**The reader, round 1**

## C1. Glosses

### gloss:torchlight
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title, "The Passion, in nine lines, without the resurrection": the standard's own example of a tour-guide heading. It counts, and it withholds. Replace with a plain name for what the note covers: "The Passion".
- "Gethsemane by torchlight (the arrest, John 18:3), "the frosty silence in the gardens," "the agony in stony places," the shouting of the crowd, "Prison and palace" (Caiaphas's house, Pilate's), the thunder and earthquake at the crucifixion." A sentence with no verb and nine items, some quoted from the poem and some glossed, so I cannot tell which words are the poem's and which are the explanation. I cannot say back what "the frosty silence in the gardens" refers to, because the note only repeats the phrase. Write sentences that say: this phrase refers to this event.
- "It is Good Friday, told in the past tense by people who were there." Turned for sound, and not quite right: "After the ..." is not a tense, lines 328 to 330 are in the present, and the arrest was the night before. The plain point is that the lines look back on the events as over.
- "No Easter follows." A two-word fragment placed for effect. I am also not sure it is true: Eliot's headnote says the first theme of this part is the journey to Emmaus, which is an Easter story. Say that these lines stop at the death and let the note on 360 take Emmaus.
- The Lausanne, Pound and Russell sentences are plain and sourced. Keep them. "unlike everything before it" is a small overstatement (Pound cut heavily, but not everything); "Pound, who had cut heavily in the earlier parts" is what is meant.
Questions for the scholar: (1) Is thunder in the gospel accounts of the crucifixion? The earthquake is; I do not remember thunder, and "reverberation / Of thunder of spring over distant mountains" may not be a crucifixion detail at all. (2) Is "frosty" from John 18:18, where the servants make a fire because "it was cold"? If so the note should say so; that is the kind of detail a reader can use. (3) "Stony places": Gethsemane, Golgotha, or neither?
Proposed text:
Title: The Passion
The lines follow the arrest, trial and crucifixion of Christ. The torchlight is the arrest in the garden at night (John 18:3); the gardens are Gethsemane; "The shouting and the crying" is the crowd; "Prison and palace" are the high priest's house and Pilate's; the "agony in stony places" and the "reverberation / Of thunder" belong to the crucifixion [scholar to confirm both]. The lines look back on all of it as finished, and they stop at the death: "He who was living is now dead." Eliot's headnote to Part V names three themes for what follows: "the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous (see Miss Weston's book) and the present decay of eastern Europe." He wrote the section at Lausanne in November and December 1921. Pound, who had cut heavily in the earlier parts, wrote at its head "OK from here on I think." Eliot told Bertrand Russell in October 1923 that Part V was "not only the best part, but the only part that justifies the whole, at all."

### gloss:no-water
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "Twenty-nine lines with almost no punctuation and no full stops, driven by the two words "rock" and "water" as if the speaker could think of nothing else, because he can't." A count, then a fragment, then a joke ("because he can't"). The fact under it, that there is no full stop from 331 to 359 and the lines keep returning to "rock" and "water", is worth one plain sentence.
- "It is the road to Emmaus, the approach to the Grail chapel through a waste land, the retreat through the Carpathians of a beaten army, and a man's own dry mouth: ..." The "X is a, b, c and d" sentence, four readings asserted with "is". Two are Eliot's (Emmaus, the Chapel Perilous); one adds specifics on top of Eliot's "present decay of eastern Europe" that I cannot find anywhere in the materials (the Carpathians, a beaten army); one is the writer's own. Give Eliot's three themes as his and say the road can be read as any of them.
- "The hymn of "If there were water" that follows (lines 346–358) is the poem's only stretch of pure longing, and it is built on a sound the speaker cannot hear." "Only stretch of pure longing" ranks and tells me how to feel; "built on a sound the speaker cannot hear" is a phrase turned for itself. Say what the lines do: imagine water, and end with a bird whose song sounds like it.
- "Eliot's note on the hermit-thrush is the most personal in the apparatus". A ranking. Say what the note says: that he heard the bird himself.
- "The thrush is also Whitman's, from the lilac poem behind line 2, the bird that sings the carol of death in the swamp." Assumes I already know Whitman is "behind line 2". Name the poem, and mark the connection as one critics make; Eliot's notes do not.
- "Listen to a recording and the poem's onomatopoeia turns out to be exact." An instruction and a confident verdict. Say a recording is in the listening room and stop.
- Title, "The road through the mountains": plain. Keep.
Questions for the scholar: Where do "the Carpathians" and "a beaten army" come from? If from Hesse or a critic, name it; if not, cut. Is the Whitman connection anywhere in Eliot, or is it the critics'? Whose?
Proposed text:
From here to line 359 there is no full stop, and the lines keep returning to two words, "rock" and "water." Eliot's headnote gives the first part of the section three themes: the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous, and "the present decay of eastern Europe." The road through the mountains can be read as any of the three. Lines 346 to 358 imagine the water that is not there, and end with the sound of a bird.

Eliot's note on line 357 names the bird, the hermit-thrush, "which I have heard in Quebec County," and quotes Chapman's <i>Handbook of Birds of Eastern North America</i> on the "purity and sweetness" of its notes; its "water-dripping song," the note says, "is justly celebrated." Line 358 is Eliot's rendering of it. The hermit thrush is also the bird of Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd," the elegy critics have set behind the lilacs of line 2; Eliot's notes do not mention it. A recording is in the listening room.

### gloss:third
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "Emmaus, and the men on South Georgia": names the two sources. Fine.
- The first two sentences (Luke 24; Eliot's note and Shackleton) are plain, sourced, and exactly what I want here. Keep as they are.
- "The hooded figure, "I do not know whether a man or a woman," is the Hanged Man that Madame Sosostris could not find, and also Tiresias, and also nobody: "When I count, there are only you and I together."" The confident "is" on a list of three that ends in a flourish ("and also nobody"). The Hanged Man link is Eliot's own, from his note on line 46, and should be given as his. Tiresias is a reading; say whose or drop it. "Nobody" is a joke on line 361; cut.
- "The question is asked and, as in the Grail castle, not answered." A closing bow that also assumes I know about the unasked question in the Grail story. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: Whose reading is Tiresias here, and is it worth keeping?
Proposed text: (first two sentences unchanged, then:)
Eliot's note on the Tarot pack at line 46 says he associates the Hanged Man "with the hooded figure in the passage of the disciples to Emmaus in Part V"; this is the card Madame Sosostris could not find. "I do not know whether a man or a woman" has also been read as pointing to Tiresias [whose reading].

### gloss:hordes
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "Eastern Europe, 1920": plain. Keep.
- "Hermann Hesse's Blick ins Chaos (1920; ...), three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky, quoted in Eliot's note." Facts I want, in a sentence with no main verb. Give it one.
- The Montagnola and Criterion sentence: factual and useful. Keep.
- "The "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" are the refugees and armies of the collapse of the Russian and Austro-Hungarian empires". A reading stated as fact with "are". Eliot's headnote says "the present decay of eastern Europe" and his note quotes Hesse; give those and let the rest be a reading.
- "the "maternal lamentation" is the women at the cross ("Daughters of Jerusalem, weep not for me," Luke 23:28) and every mother in Europe in 1919." "Is" again, and "every mother in Europe in 1919" is written for the sound. Offer Luke 23:28 as a reading; drop the flourish.
- "Then the roll-call: "Jerusalem Athens Alexandria / Vienna London," the cities that were each in turn the centre of the world, each "Unreal," the word from line 60 now standing on its own with no city after it." "Roll-call" is a metaphor about the poem; "each in turn the centre of the world" is a claim I cannot check (Vienna? Alexandria?). The cross-reference to "Unreal City" at line 60 is the useful part.
- "London is the last name on the list." The standard's example. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: Is "Unreal City" also in Part III (line 207)? If so cite both. Is Luke 23:28 a critic's reading or the site's?
Proposed text:
Eliot's note on these lines quotes Hermann Hesse's <i>Blick ins Chaos</i> (1920; in English as <i>In Sight of Chaos</i>, 1923), three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky, on a Europe "on the way to chaos." Eliot visited Hesse at Montagnola in May 1922 and printed him in the first number of <i>The Criterion</i> that October, alongside the poem. His headnote calls this theme "the present decay of eastern Europe." The "maternal lamentation" has been read with the women who weep for Christ on the way to the cross (Luke 23:28). Lines 375 to 377 name five cities and then "Unreal," the word of line 60, "Unreal City," this time with no city after it.

### gloss:bats
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title, "A nightmare from an older notebook": "nightmare" tells me how to feel and "notebook" is a picturesque guess at what the papers are. Say what it is.
- "These eight lines predate the rest of the poem." Plain, but by how much? A date would help.
- "the images are Gothic and painterly: the woman fiddling on her own hair, the bats with baby faces, the towers upside down in air, bells that "kept the hours" like St Mary Woolnoth's, and voices "singing out of empty cisterns and exhausted wells," which is Jeremiah's "broken cisterns, that can hold no water."" One sentence, six items, most of which only repeat the poem back to me. The useful parts (the bells recall St Mary Woolnoth in Part I; the cisterns may recall Jeremiah) are buried in it, and "which is Jeremiah's" is a confident "is" for an echo Eliot did not note.
- "The violet hour of Part III has become "violet light."" Plain and useful. Keep, with line numbers.
- "The poem's phrase for the whole passage would be the one it used of the pictures beside Philomel's: "withered stumps of time," pictures on the wall of the mind." A metaphor about the poem, borrowed from the poem, tied in a bow. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: When is the "So through the evening" fragment dated? The rights rule allows a phrase from the 1971 facsimile: is a whole first line a phrase? (The same line is quoted in draft:ok-from-here.) Which lines are St Mary Woolnoth (67?) and the violet hour (215, 220?), so the note can cite them? Is Jeremiah 2:13 an editor's note or the site's own?
Proposed text:
Title: From an earlier fragment
These lines are older than the rest of the poem. They come from a fragment among Eliot's papers, printed in the 1971 facsimile, beginning "So through the evening, through the violet air" [date]. The bells that "kept the hours" recall St Mary Woolnoth, which "kept the hours" in Part I [line]; "violet light" recalls the "violet hour" of Part III [lines]; "empty cisterns and exhausted wells" may recall Jeremiah's "broken cisterns, that can hold no water" (Jeremiah 2:13).

### gloss:chapel
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "The Chapel Perilous": Weston's and Eliot's term. Keep.
- The Weston summary is clear and useful; "survive it, and he is fit for the Grail" is brisk but true. Keep.
- "Eliot's chapel is worse than perilous: it is empty, windowless, the door swinging, "Dry bones can harm no one," the test already over." "Worse than perilous" is a turn; "the test already over" is a reading given as fact, and not obviously what the lines say. Say: in the poem the chapel is empty and nothing happens in it.
- "the cock that crowed for Peter's denial and the "bird of dawning" that sends ghosts back to their graves in the first scene of Hamlet." Two associations given with "the cock that ...", as if settled. Say "has been heard as".
- "Lightning, "a damp gust / Bringing rain." Three words." The counting fragment, and the count disagrees with path:water/395 ("Two words."). Cut.
- "It is the first rain in the poem since the shower over the Starnbergersee at line 9." Useful. Keep.
Questions for the scholar: Does rain fall at 395? Lines 396 to 398 have the leaves still waiting for it and 425 has "the arid plain"; way:Water at last says "Everything after that is wet." Which is it? Is the summary of Weston's chapter (dead body on the altar, black hand, storms) accurate to chapter XIII?
Proposed text:
Weston's chapter "The Perilous Chapel" gathers the episode in which the knight must pass a night in a chapel in a waste place: a dead body lies on the altar, a black hand puts out the candle, voices and a storm assail him; if he survives he may go on to the Grail. In the poem the chapel is empty and nothing happens in it: "Dry bones can harm no one." The cock crows "Co co rico," the French spelling of the sound; readers have heard in it the cock that crowed at Peter's denial and the "bird of dawning" of the first scene of <i>Hamlet</i>. The "damp gust / Bringing rain" at line 395 is the first rain in the poem since the shower at line 9.

### gloss:ganga
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "The river and the mountain": plain. Keep. First sentence: plain and useful. Keep.
- "The scene moves in a line from the chapel in the mountains to the Indian monsoon, the whole subcontinent crouched "humped in silence" waiting for the rains, and then the thunder speaks, in the syllable of a language Eliot had studied at Harvard for two years under Charles Lanman and never pretended to command." One sentence doing four jobs. "The whole subcontinent crouched" enlarges the poem's "The jungle crouched"; "never pretended to command" is a phrase standing in for a fact. Split it and drop the enlargement.
- The <i>After Strange Gods</i> sentence: factual, sourced, a phrase quoted. Keep.
Questions for the scholar: Is "Himavant, 'the snowy'" right, and does the line need the Puranas? The library record says Lanman 1911 to 1913; "two years" is consistent.
Proposed text:
Ganga is the Ganges; Himavant, "the snowy," is the Himalaya, in the Puranas the mountain father of the goddess Ganga. The scene moves from the chapel in the mountains to India before the monsoon. Then the thunder speaks, in Sanskrit, which Eliot studied for two years at Harvard under Charles Lanman. In <i>After Strange Gods</i> (1934) he said the Sanskrit and a year of Patañjali's metaphysics had left him "in a state of enlightened mystification."

### gloss:da
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "What the thunder said": the section's own title; as a note title it is fine.
- The first two sentences are plain and useful. Keep.
- "each word is followed by a confession in the poem's own voice of how the instruction has been failed." A reading stated as fact; "confession" decides for me. Say each word is followed by a passage that responds to it, and that in the usual reading each instruction is shown not kept.
- "The section's title, and the poem's ending, come from a fable about hearing what you are able to hear." An aphorism to end on. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: Eliot's note says "5, 1"; the gloss and the library record say 5.2. Which is right, and should the note say that Eliot's number differs? The note says Eliot "changes the order" but not from what; the quote box gives it (gods, men, demons: restrain, give, be compassionate), so the body could say so in a clause.
Proposed text:
<i>Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad</i> 5.2, paraphrased here from the Sanskrit; Eliot's note (which numbers it 5, 1) gives Deussen's German translation as his source. The thunder says one syllable, and each of the three hearers takes it as the instruction meant for him: the gods hear "restrain yourselves," the men "give," the demons "be compassionate." Eliot puts the words in the order Datta, Dayadhvam, Damyata (give, sympathise, control), and follows each with a passage in the poem's own voice; in the usual reading each passage shows the instruction not kept.

### gloss:datta
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "Give": keep.
- The first two sentences walk me through lines 403 to 410 and put the Webster gloss where it is needed. Keep; this is the kind of note I want.
- "This is the poem's one statement about what a life is for, and it is made in the vocabulary of probate." The bow: a superlative ("the poem's one statement") and a turned phrase ("the vocabulary of probate"). Cut.
Questions for the scholar: "the will read out by 'the lean solicitor'": the poem says "seals broken by the lean solicitor"; is "will" the settled reading? Fine if so.
Proposed text: the first two sentences as they stand; nothing after "after the seals are broken."

### gloss:dayadhvam
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "Sympathise: Ugolino's tower, and Bradley's circle": crowded but it names both sources. "Sympathise: Ugolino and Bradley" is lighter.
- The Ugolino sentence and the Bradley sentence: plain, sourced. Keep.
- "Each of us in a prison, thinking of a key, and the thinking of the key is what confirms the prison." A verbless restatement of lines 414 and 415 in the poem's own rhythm. It adds nothing I cannot read myself. Cut, or make it one sentence that connects Bradley to the lines.
- ""A broken Coriolanus," Shakespeare's general who could not bend, is revived "for a moment" by "aetherial rumours" at nightfall, and only for a moment." "And only for a moment" repeats the quotation for effect. "Who could not bend" is so compressed it is a riddle for someone who does not know the play.
- "Sympathy is the instruction; solitude is the report." Named in the standard. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: What should a reader be told about Coriolanus in one clause so that "broken" makes sense? Is "aetherial rumours" glossed anywhere, or left alone?
Proposed text: (first two sentences unchanged, then:)
Bradley's closed circle is the "prison" of lines 414 and 415. Coriolanus is Shakespeare's Roman general, [one plain clause on why "broken"]; here he is revived only "for a moment," by "aetherial rumours," at nightfall.

### gloss:damyata
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title, "Control: a boat off Cape Ann": puts a biographical guess in the title as if it were in the poem. "Control" is enough.
- "The third instruction gets the poem's only memory of things going well". "Gets" is chatty; "the poem's only memory of things going well" is a claim I could argue with (the hyacinth garden, the sled) and it tells me how to feel about the lines.
- "Eliot had sailed off Gloucester and Cape Ann as a boy, and the Dry Salvages of his later poem lie off that coast." The first half is worth having if it is marked as a reading; the Dry Salvages is an aside about another poem.
- "Would have." Named in the standard. Cut.
- "The most intimate address in the poem, to a "you" who is never named, is a sentence about what did not happen." A superlative and a bow. The plain part, that the "you" is not named and the sentence is conditional, takes one line.
Questions for the scholar: Who connects this boat with Eliot's boyhood sailing? A letter, or a biographer's reading?
Proposed text:
Title: Control
The third word is answered by a boat under control: "The boat responded / Gaily, to the hand expert with sail and oar," on a calm sea. Eliot sailed off Cape Ann, Massachusetts, as a boy, and the boat has been read as a memory of it [whose reading]. The sentence that follows is conditional: "your heart would have responded / Gaily, when invited." The "you" is not named.

### gloss:fisher-king
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "The Fisher King, and Hezekiah": keep.
- "Eliot's note sends you to Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, the wounded keeper of the Grail who fishes because he can do nothing else, while his land lies waste behind him." "Sends you" is chatty; the rest is useful. "Because he can do nothing else" compresses the romance (his wound keeps him from riding); fine if the scholar agrees.
- The Isaiah sentence: plain, sourced, and it tells me the story I need. Keep. "Is Isaiah 38:1" should be "recalls": Eliot's line is a question, Isaiah's is a command.
- "It is also what a man does with his papers." A flourish, and I do not know what it means (a dying man's affairs? Eliot's own manuscript?). Cut.
- "From here to the end the poem stops narrating and starts stacking." Named in the standard. Cut.
- Not mine to change: the quote box gives a verse of Isaiah under a source field that says "weston". The editor should know the box will say Weston over Isaiah.
Questions for the scholar: none beyond the above.
Proposed text:
Eliot's note points to Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, the wounded keeper of the Grail, who fishes while his land lies waste. "Shall I at least set my lands in order?" recalls Isaiah 38:1, the prophet's message to the dying king Hezekiah: "Set thine house in order: for thou shalt die, and not live." Hezekiah turns his face to the wall and weeps, and is given fifteen more years.

### gloss:fragments
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title, "The last eight lines, one by one": says what the note does. Acceptable; "Lines 427 to 434" is plainer.
- London Bridge: the whole entry is one sentence with no main verb, and "in the folklorists' reading that Frazer would have relished" is a wink. "The bridge the commuters crossed at line 62." is a fragment. Make two or three plain sentences.
- Purgatorio: mostly plain and good. "He goes willingly." is short for effect but says something true that I need; let it stand. "Set this fire against the burning of the Fire Sermon." is an instruction. Cut, or say plainly that this fire purifies where the fire of Part III burns, and mark it as a reading.
- Pervigilium: clear and useful; "suddenly turns on the poet" is a little dramatic. "Philomel's sister, still singing." is a fragment tied as a bow; replace with a sentence that names Procne and points to lines 99 and 203.
- Nerval: "The Tower is a Tarot card" asserts what the library record more honestly calls "often read as". "Another man who has been among the dead" is a bow.
- "These fragments": "quotations propped like timbers against a wall that is coming down" is the standard's own example. What I need here is the meaning of "shored": propped up, as builders shore a wall. Give me that and say the line is the poem's own.
- Kyd: plain and good. "A poem in seven languages, written as revenge." is a bow and a count. Cut.
- "Datta. Dayadhvam. Damyata.": fine.
- Shantih: the facts are right and useful but the sentence is tangled ("as Eliot's note says" floats free). Reorder. "and neither should you on a first reading" is named in the standard; cut the whole last sentence, since way:Peace already puts the question.
Questions for the scholar: In which edition did the note's "a feeble translation" become "our equivalent"? "The play he writes is in four languages": the library record says "sundry"; is four right? Whose reading is the Tarot Tower for "tour abolie"? Is Frazer actually connected to the foundation-sacrifice reading of the rhyme, or is he decoration?
Proposed text:
<b>London Bridge is falling down falling down falling down.</b> The nursery rhyme. Its verses try one material after another (wood and clay, bricks and mortar, iron and steel, silver and gold), and the oldest versions end by setting a man to watch all night. Folklorists of Frazer's generation connected the rhyme with the custom of burying a victim under a bridge's foundations. The crowd of line 62 crossed this bridge.

<b>Poi s'ascose nel foco che gli affina.</b> "Then he hid himself in the fire that refines them." <i>Purgatorio</i> XXVI, 148. Arnaut Daniel, having spoken to Dante in his own Provençal (the <i>Comedy</i>'s one passage in a vernacular other than Italian), steps back into the purifying flame. He goes willingly. Eliot's note quotes the end of Arnaut's speech, from "Ara vos prec," the words Eliot took for the title of his 1920 collection.

<b>Quando fiam ceu chelidon.</b> "When shall I become like the swallow?" From the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>, an anonymous Latin poem of the second to fourth century for the eve of Venus's spring festival. In its last stanza the nightingale sings and the poet, silent, asks when his own spring will come: "when shall I become like the swallow, so that I may stop being silent?" The 1922 printings have "ceu"; the Latin poem has "uti," and Eliot's later editions restored it. "O swallow swallow" is Tennyson's, from <i>The Princess</i>: "O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South." The swallow is Procne, sister of the Philomela of lines 99 and 203.

<b>Le Prince d'Aquitaine à la tour abolie.</b> The second line of Gérard de Nerval's sonnet "El Desdichado" (1854): "I am the dark one, the widower, the unconsoled, the Prince of Aquitaine with the ruined tower." The "tour abolie" is often read as the Tower of the Tarot pack (trump XVI, struck by lightning). Later in the sonnet the speaker says he has twice crossed Acheron, the river of the dead.

<b>These fragments I have shored against my ruins.</b> The poem's own line among the quotations. To shore is to prop up, as a builder shores a wall with timbers.

<b>Why then Ile fit you. Hieronymo's mad againe.</b> Kyd's <i>The Spanish Tragedy</i> (c. 1587), Act IV. Hieronimo, whose son has been murdered, is asked to write an entertainment for the court and answers "Why then, I'll fit you," meaning both "I'll oblige" and "I'll pay you out"; the play he writes is in four languages, and the murderers, acting in it, are killed for real. "Hieronimo is mad again" was the subtitle on the 1615 title page.

<b>Datta. Dayadhvam. Damyata.</b> The thunder's three words, repeated without the passages that answered them.

<b>Shantih shantih shantih.</b> Eliot's note: the formal ending of an Upanishad. "The Peace which passeth understanding" is Philippians 4:7; the 1922 note called it "a feeble translation" of the word, and later printings of the note "our equivalent."

**The reader, round 1**

## C2. Ways in

The list title, "Twelve ways into What the Thunder Said": "ways into" is a tour-guide idiom, but it is a functional label shared by all five parts and it is mild. Leave it and move on.

### way:Eliot reading the thunder
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: describes the recording. Fine.
- "Eliot's own recordings are at their strangest here": a ranking, and "strangest" tells me what to feel. Say what he does: "DA" flat, "Shantih" quiet.
- "Listen to the last thirty lines before reading the notes on them." A "listen" way is there to tell me to listen; that is the genre. "Before reading the notes" is a rule about how to use the site. Cut it.
Questions for the scholar: Which recording, and what year? Is "flat, without drama" a description the scholar has heard and agrees with?
Proposed text: In his recording [year] Eliot says "DA" flat, without emphasis, and "Shantih" three times quietly. The last thirty lines are in the listening room.

### way:Water at last
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title, "Water at last": mildly dramatic. "Water in Part V" would do.
- "The section is the poem's longest drought and its only rain." An aphorism, and "its only rain" contradicts gloss:chapel, which counts the shower at line 9.
- "twenty-nine lines of rock and then, at line 395, "Bringing rain."" The count from Ethan's own quoted sentence.
- "Everything after that is wet." False to the lines: 396 to 398 wait for rain, 425 has "the arid plain."
Questions for the scholar: Does the rain fall? (See gloss:chapel.)
Proposed text: Title: Water in Part V. The Water lens tints every line that is about water or the want of it. Here there is none from line 331 to line 394; then "Bringing rain" (395); the Ganges, sunken and waiting for it (396 to 398); the boat on a calm sea (419 to 421); the shore, with the arid plain behind it (424 to 425).

### way:Hear the hermit-thrush
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Fine until the end. "His transcription is accurate." A verdict I should reach myself, and the three notes on the bird disagree about it ("exact", "accurate", "fair"). Cut.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "Drip drop drip drop drop drop drop." Eliot's note names the bird and quotes the field guide. A recording is in the listening room. Play it, then read line 358.

### way:The road to Emmaus, and South Georgia
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "Read both." Fine for a "source" way.
- "The hooded figure is a risen god and a hallucination of exhausted men, and the poem will not say which." The confident "is", a pair for rhythm, and a bow. Eliot's note gives both sources; that is what to say.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Two texts under "Who is the third who walks always beside you?": Luke 24, and Shackleton's account of crossing South Georgia in 1916, when three men each felt a fourth. Both are in the library. Eliot's headnote gives the first and his note on the line gives the second.

### way:Eight lines, six languages
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: counts to be clever, and the count fights path:first/427 ("five languages"). The lines have five; the sixth is in a note. Retitle: "The languages of the last lines."
- The first sentence is plain and useful. Keep.
- "then read the lines again as a person who has none of them: as a noise made by a civilisation." Tells me how to experience the lines, and ends in a metaphor. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Title: The languages of the last lines. The last eight lines are in English, Italian, Latin, French, English, English, Sanskrit, Sanskrit, with Provençal in Eliot's note. The Tongues lens gives the translations.

### way:Unstack the fragments
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: "Unstack" is the timbers metaphor turned into a verb. "The fragments, one by one" is plainer.
- "so that you can see the wall the poet says he propped them against." The metaphor about the poem again. Cut after "source."
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Title: The fragments, one by one. "These fragments I have shored against my ruins." The Fragments view takes the last lines one at a time and gives each its source.

### way:What the thunder actually said
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title: "actually" is a joke on the section title. Retitle: "The fable in the Upanishad."
- "one syllable, three hearers, three meanings." The counted list of three.
- "Then notice that Eliot changes the order of the commands and confesses, after each, how it was not kept." "Notice that" is an instruction; "confesses ... how it was not kept" is a reading as fact.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Title: The fable in the Upanishad. The library has the passage of the Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad that Eliot's note cites: the thunder says "Da," and gods, men and demons each hear a different word in it. Eliot changes the order of the three words and follows each with a passage of his own.

### way:From Margate to the Ganges
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: Margate is in Part III, not here; the title reaches outside the section for a flourish. Retitle: "The places in Part V."
- "The world map draws the poem's journey east in a single line." The poem makes no journey; the map draws a line. Say the map shows them.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Title: The places in Part V. The places named in this section are Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna and London, the Ganges and the Himalaya. The world map shows them.

### way:Every 'nothing'
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- The list of quotations is the pathway's content; fine.
- "The pathway follows the word through the poem to its last appearance." The word does not occur anywhere in lines 322 to 434 (I looked), and the pathway's Part V stop is on "what have we given?" So this way, on the Part V list, promises a last appearance that is not here.
Questions for the scholar: Where is the last "nothing" in the poem? If it is in Part III, should this way be on the Part V list, and what is the stop at 402 for?
Proposed text: (first sentence as is.) The pathway follows the word through the poem. Its last stop is at line 402.

### way:OK from here on
Verdict: keep
Findings:
- Plain and sourced. Only "said afterwards" should say to whom and when (to Bertrand Russell, October 1923), as gloss:torchlight does.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

### way:The last voices
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- The opening list has no verb.
- "Read them as a chorus and as a man talking to himself." Tells me how to read. Cut.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: The Voices lens colours each of the last eight lines by its speaker: the Fisher King, a nursery rhyme, Dante, an anonymous Latin poet, Nerval, the poet himself, Kyd's Hieronimo, an Upanishad.

### way:Peace, or the word for it
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- Title: a turn of phrase. "The last word" or "Shantih."
- "Read the last line once as a blessing and once as a lullaby, and keep the one that lets you close the book." The second half is named in the standard; the first is an exercise in how to feel. An "ask" way asks a question; keep the question and stop.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Title: The last word. "Shantih shantih shantih." Eliot's note says it is the formal ending of an Upanishad and that "The Peace which passeth understanding" is "a feeble translation" of it. Is the line a blessing on the poem, or a man saying the word to himself?

**The reader, round 1**

## C3. Pathway stops

A stop is a caption. The good ones here quote the line and add one plain fact; the bad ones count, joke, or tell me how to feel.

### path:first/331
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "Rock and no water for twenty-nine lines." A count. "Let it be as dry as it is." Tells me how to feel. "The rain, when it comes at line 395, is two words long." A count.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: No water from here to line 359. Rain comes at line 395.

### path:first/427
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "a stack of quotations in five languages": the stack metaphor; the count disagrees with way:Eight lines, six languages. "Do not look them up yet. Read them as a noise": instructions on how to experience the lines.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: The last eight lines are mostly quotations, in five languages. The Fragments view gives each its source.

### path:water/331
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "The longest drought." A label where a fact would serve.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "Here is no water but only rock." No water from here to line 394.

### path:water/353
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "A hymn to a sound that is not there": a phrase turned for effect. "Ending in a bird that imitates water" is the useful part.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "If there were the sound of water only." Lines 353 to 359 imagine the sound of water and end with a bird whose song is like it.

### path:water/395
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "Two words." Named in the standard. "The only rain that falls." Conflicts with the shower at line 9 (gloss:chapel) and with 396 to 398, where the leaves are still waiting.
Questions for the scholar: does the rain fall here? (See gloss:chapel.)
Proposed text: "Bringing rain." The first rain since line 9.

### path:water/396
Verdict: keep
Findings:
- Plain; "the monsoon" is a fair name for black clouds gathering over Himavant.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

### path:water/419
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "Water at last as something that answers a hand." Turned.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "The boat responded." A calm sea, and a boat under control.

### path:water/424
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "The last water and the last drought, in one sentence." A bow.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "I sat upon the shore / Fishing, with the arid plain behind me." The last water in the poem.

### path:dead/328
Verdict: keep
Findings:
- The quotation alone. Nothing to fault.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

### path:dead/388
Verdict: keep
Findings:
- Plain: the quotation, the chapel's name, the line.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

### path:dead/407
Verdict: keep
Findings:
- "What the dead leave: memories, wills, empty rooms." A list of three, but it is what lines 407 to 410 contain. Acceptable as it stands.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

### path:dead/412
Verdict: keep
Findings:
- Plain.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

### path:women/368
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "and the mothers of Europe": a flourish. "The women at the cross" is a reading, unmarked.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "Murmur of maternal lamentation." Mothers mourning; read with the women weeping on the way to the cross (Luke 23:28), and with Hesse's Europe in Eliot's note.

### path:women/378
Verdict: keep
Findings:
- A plain paraphrase; "plays it" is fair for "fiddled whisper music on those strings."
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

### path:cities/375
Verdict: keep
Findings:
- The quotation alone.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

### path:cities/396
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "The poem's last places are a river and a mountain." A mild bow where a gloss would help more.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: Ganga and Himavant: the Ganges and the Himalaya, the last places the poem names.

### path:birds/354
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "Another singer that is not a bird and not water." A riddle for rhythm.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "Not the cicada." Not a bird: the dry sound that is there instead of water.

### path:birds/357
Verdict: keep
Findings:
- Plain. "Listen to it" is what a birds pathway with recordings is for.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

### path:birds/380
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "Not birds. Included for the wings." Two fragments and a joke.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "bats with baby faces." Not birds, but they whistle and beat their wings.

### path:birds/392
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "Peter's cock, Hamlet's, and the French farmyard." A list of three with a cute ending; the readings unmarked.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "Co co rico": the French spelling of the crow. Readers have heard in it the cock of Peter's denial and the cock of <i>Hamlet</i> I.i.

### path:birds/429
Verdict: keep
Findings:
- Plain enough. "At the end" could go; "see lines 99 and 203" would help more.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: none

### path:nothing/402
Verdict: rewrite
Findings:
- "The question that answers the word." I cannot say what this means. Which word, "nothing" or "Datta"? And "nothing" is not in the line, or anywhere in Part V.
Questions for the scholar: What is this stop for? (See way:Every 'nothing'.)
Proposed text: "what have we given?" The last stop: the word "nothing" does not occur in Part V, and the pathway ends at the thunder's first question.

### path:eyes/360
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "Eyes that do not know him." An allusion to Luke 24:16 without saying so.
Questions for the scholar: none
Proposed text: "Who is the third who walks always beside you?" Luke 24:16: "their eyes were holden that they should not know him."

### path:eliot/419
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- "A boat off Cape Ann, the summers of his boyhood." A fragment, and biography given as fact.
Questions for the scholar: whose reading? (See gloss:damyata.)
Proposed text: Eliot sailed off Cape Ann, Massachusetts, as a boy; the boat may be a memory of it.

**The reader, round 1**

## C4. Drafts records

### draft:ok-from-here
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: Pound's words. Fine.
- "Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne in November and December 1921, fast, and later said it had come "in a trance."" "Fast," set between commas is a rhythm tic; "in a trance" needs a source and a date.
- The rest is plain. The last two items are Parts I and IV; fine on a drafts page.
Questions for the scholar: Where and when did Eliot say "in a trance"? Is the full first line of the fragment within the "phrase" rule for the 1971 facsimile? (Also quoted in gloss:bats.)
Proposed text: At the head of Part V Pound wrote "OK from here on I think," and cut almost nothing. Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne in November and December 1921, and later said [where, when] that it had come "in a trance." (Then the sentence on the three older passages, as it stands.)

### draft:manuscript-fate
Verdict: trim
Findings:
- Title: plain. Keep.
- "After Quinn's death in 1924 it vanished." Dramatic; what is meant is that its whereabouts were unknown.
- "the cuts, and Pound's pencil, are the reason the poem is 434 lines long and not 800." The counting bow, and I do not know that 800 is right.
- The rest is plain and useful.
Questions for the scholar: How long was the draft? Are "$140" and "insisted on paying" sourced?
Proposed text: (as it stands until "in 1922;" then:) Quinn insisted on paying $140 for it. After Quinn's death in 1924 the whereabouts of the typescript were unknown. His niece sold it to the New York Public Library in 1958, and the library announced the acquisition in 1968, three years after Eliot's death. Valerie Eliot's facsimile edition of 1971 printed every page. The papers are in the Berg Collection.

**The reader, round 1**

## D. Secondary material (proposals for the site thread)

### D1. Pathway intros

### pathway:first
Verdict: trim
Findings: "Each stop is a place to stand, not an explanation" is the "where the poem is standing" idiom; "Read the lines, read the note, move on" is a list of three imperatives.
Proposed text: Twelve stops for a first reading. Each gives a line or two and a short note.

### pathway:water
Verdict: rewrite
Findings: "Follow the water and you follow the argument: water is what the waste land needs, water is what drowns you, and the two are never told apart." The twin of Ethan's quoted sentence: "X and you Y the argument:" then a list of three.
Proposed text: Every line in which water is present or missing, from the shower of line 9 to the rain of line 395. The poem wants water and is drowned by it; the pathway shows both.

### pathway:dead
Verdict: trim
Findings: "and the dead never quite stay in it" is a joke to end on.
Proposed text: Burials, drownings, bones and the crowd that death has undone. The poem's first section is called The Burial of the Dead, from the Book of Common Prayer; the dead appear in every part.

### pathway:women
Verdict: trim
Findings: "Most of them speak; several are quotations; two are queens; one is a bird." A counted list for rhythm. The first two sentences are plain and give me the test; stop there.
Proposed text: the first two sentences as they stand.

### pathway:cities
Verdict: trim
Findings: "and five capitals falling in two lines": a counting flourish. Cut the clause.
Proposed text: The poem's geography, in the order the poem gives it. It begins in Munich and ends on the Ganges, with London at its centre.

### pathway:birds
Verdict: rewrite
Findings: "Every bird in the poem is a musician, and every song is a complaint." An aphorism.
Proposed text: Every bird named in the poem, in order. Recordings, where they exist, are in the listening room.

### pathway:nothing
Verdict: keep
Findings: The list is the pathway's content and it is plain. No fault.
Proposed text: none

### pathway:eyes
Verdict: trim
Findings: "The poem's people cannot see, or see too much." A bow. The list before it is the pathway's content.
Proposed text: the first sentence as it stands.

### pathway:eliot
Verdict: trim
Findings: "Not a key to the poem, but a map of where he was standing." The flagged idiom.
Proposed text: Places and dates from Eliot's own life in 1921 and 1922 that appear in the poem.

### D2. Library records

### source:weston
Verdict: trim
Findings: "It does not matter." A fragment-aphorism; whether the theory stands does matter to me. "Read chapters II ..., and you have the poem's skeleton; read the rest and you have a very good bad book." An instruction ending in a joke. The rest (what Weston argued, Eliot's note, his 1956 regret in a phrase) is plain and useful.
Proposed text: replace the two flagged sentences with: "Almost no medievalist now accepts the argument; the poem used the book anyway." and "The chapters the poem draws on most are II (The Task of the Hero), IX (The Fisher King) and XIII (The Perilous Chapel)."

### source:dante-purg26
Verdict: keep
Findings: Plain, sourced, tells me where each borrowing comes from, and notes the textual variant. No fault.
Proposed text: none

### source:dante-inf33
Verdict: trim
Findings: "everyone in a tower, listening for the key." A metaphor about the poem to end on.
Proposed text: end with: "Eliot's note pairs the passage with Bradley on the privacy of experience; see the note on line 412."

### source:whitman-lilacs
Verdict: trim
Findings: "He rarely admitted to reading Whitman. The lilacs and the thrush say otherwise." A bow, and it settles an inference as if it were a fact. Question: is "rarely admitted" right (he wrote on Whitman later)? Whose connection is this?
Proposed text: end with: "Eliot's notes do not mention Whitman; the connection is the critics' [whose]."

Verdict: trim
Findings: "In the valley the bones stand up. In the poem they do not." Two short sentences for rhythm; the contrast is real and can be one plain sentence.
Proposed text: end with: "In Ezekiel the bones live again; in the poem, 'Dry bones can harm no one.'"

### source:isaiah
Verdict: keep
Findings: "offers the prophet's shelter without the prophet's promise of water" is a turned sentence but it says a true and useful thing; "asks the question and gets no answer" is plain. No fault worth a fight.

The swallow song in <i>The Princess</i> gives Eliot the English half of a Latin line. He called Tennyson's 'the finest ear of any English poet since Milton' (1936)."

**The reader, round 1**

## Summary

Most often: the bow at the end (twelve of the thirteen glosses end on an aphorism or an image), the count that stands in for a point (and the counts disagree with each other), the confident "is" for what is one reading, and instructions on how to read or feel in the ways and the path stops. The material under the flourishes is mostly good: the sources are right, the cross-references are useful, and the middles of gloss:third, gloss:hordes, gloss:datta, gloss:dayadhvam and most of gloss:fragments can stand as they are once their last sentences go.

I would rewrite from scratch: gloss:torchlight (title and first half), gloss:no-water, gloss:bats, gloss:damyata; way:Water at last, way:What the thunder actually said, way:Peace, or the word for it; path:first/331, path:first/427, path:water/395, path:nothing/402; and the two site-wide sentences.

I would keep untouched: gloss:third's first two sentences, way:OK from here on, and the plain stops (dead/328, dead/388, dead/407, dead/412, women/378, cities/375, birds/357, birds/429, water/396), and in the library dante-purg26, isaiah, webster, hesse.

Open questions that need the scholar before wording can be settled: whether rain falls at 395; the Upanishad's numbering (5, 1 or 5.2); thunder at the crucifixion; where "the Carpathians" and "a beaten army" come from; whose readings Tiresias, Luke 23:28, Jeremiah 2:13 and the Tarot Tower are; the "nothing" pathway's Part V stop; and how much of the 1971 facsimile may be quoted.

**The scholar, round 2**

## Answers to the reader's round-1 questions not answered before
- "Stony places": neither Gethsemane nor Golgotha is named; the phrase is Matthew 13:5 and 13:20 (the parable of the sower). An echo a note may mention, not one of Eliot's.
- "aetherial rumours": no note of Eliot's and no settled gloss; leave.
- "the will": the usual reading; hedged as it now is.
- The boat and Cape Ann: the sailing is documented in Eliot's letters and by Crawford; the link to line 419 is the commentators'.
- Jeremiah 2:13, Tiresias, Luke 23:27–28, the Tarot Tower: all commentators' associations without a first proposer I can name; "commentators" and "some readers" are the honest words.
- Quoting a first line from the 1971 facsimile: Ricks and McCue use first lines as the titles of these fragments, so quoting one identifies the poem rather than quoting it; within the rule, in my view, but the rule is the editor's.
- Whitman "rarely admitted": see gloss:no-water; he reviewed Holloway's Whitman in December 1926.
- The last "nothing": line 305.

**The scholar, round 2**

## Still unsettled
- The Russell letter, "which" or "that". The printed Letters (vol. 2, 2009) are not reachable; HathiTrust's full-text search is behind a script wall; Google Books' API quota was exhausted. The only transcription I can reach is Leithauser and Dyer, "Bertrand Russell and T. S. Eliot: their dialogue", Russell (summer 1982), written at McMaster from the originals before the Letters were published: "especially Part V which in my opinion is not only the best part, but the only part which justifies the whole, at all". Alan Wood's 1957 biography of Russell, which the article cites near by, does not print the letter (checked the full text). "Which" is the only verifiable reading; anyone with the Letters should confirm it in a minute.
- Pound's "OK from here on I think": the words are now attested twice (Keane 2011; Spectator 1972 for the substance) but the facsimile page is unrecorded.
- The text of "After the turning of the inspired days" and the facsimile's note tying it to lines 322–330: from memory; check p. 108.
- The date of the two fragments: still not printable; the rewrite rightly does not give one.
- The 1946 Poetry essay: words confirmed only through two quotations of it.

**The scholar, round 2**

**The reader, round 2**

## C1. Glosses

### gloss:torchlight
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "The earthquake at the crucifixion is Matthew's (27:51). No Gospel has thunder there; the "thunder of spring over distant mountains" is the poem's own." The earthquake sentence explains something that is not in the poem; it is left over from the round-1 argument about the thunder. Fold it into the thunder sentence as the nearest thing the Gospels have.
- "Pound, who had cut hard in the earlier parts": "cut hard" is a turn; "cut heavily" is what a person says.
- Length: thirteen sentences is long for a card, but this is the head of the section and each sentence does a job. If it must be shorter, cut the Russell sentence; the "OK from here on" way carries it.
Proposed text:
Lines 322 to 330 look back on the arrest, trial and death of Christ without naming him. The torchlight is the arrest in the garden at night, when Judas comes "with lanterns and torches and weapons" (John 18:3). The gardens are Gethsemane, where he prayed "being in an agony" (Luke 22:44) and where, John says, "it was cold" (18:18). "The shouting and the crying" is the crowd; "Prison and palace" has been read as the high priest's house and Pilate's. No Gospel has thunder at the crucifixion (Matthew has an earthquake, 27:51); the "thunder of spring over distant mountains" is the poem's own. The lines stop at the death: "He who was living is now dead." Eliot's headnote to Part V names three themes for what follows: "the journey to Emmaus, the approach to the Chapel Perilous (see Miss Weston's book) and the present decay of eastern Europe." He wrote the section at Lausanne between late November 1921 and the first days of 1922. Pound, who had cut heavily in the earlier parts, wrote at the head of the typescript "OK from here on I think." Eliot wrote to Bertrand Russell on 15 October 1923 that Part V was "not only the best part, but the only part which justifies the whole, at all."

### gloss:no-water
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "there is no punctuation and no full stop": a full stop is punctuation; say it once.
- "Commentators also hear behind the line the hermit thrush of Whitman's ..., the elegy whose lilacs are often set behind line 2": "behind" twice, meaning "as a source for"; a reader may not know the idiom. Also "hermit thrush" here against "hermit-thrush" two sentences earlier; use Eliot's hyphen throughout except inside a quotation.
- The rest is clear, and the birthday present is the kind of thing a person would tell you. Keep.
Proposed text (the first sentence and the Whitman sentence; the rest unchanged):
From line 331 to line 359 there is no punctuation, and the lines keep returning to two words, "rock" and "water."
[...]
Commentators also point to the hermit-thrush of Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd," the elegy often cited for the lilacs of line 2; Eliot said in 1928 that he had not read Whitman until late in life.

### gloss:third
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "he expounds the scriptures to them, breaks bread at supper, and "vanished out of their sight."" The quotation is in the past tense inside a present-tense sentence; give it its own clause.
- "that is a reading, and Eliot's notes do not make it": stiff; see the note at the head.
Proposed text:
In Luke 24, on the day of the resurrection, two disciples walking to Emmaus are joined by a stranger, "but their eyes were holden that they should not know him"; he expounds the scriptures to them, breaks bread at supper, and is gone: "he vanished out of their sight." Eliot's note gives the second source from memory ("I forget which, but I think one of Shackleton's"): the crossing of South Georgia by Shackleton, Worsley and Crean on 19 and 20 May 1916, at the end of the <i>Endurance</i> expedition, told in Shackleton's <i>South</i> (1919). Each of the three men felt that a fourth was walking with them, and said nothing of it until afterwards. Eliot's note on the Tarot pack at line 46 says he associates the Hanged Man "with the hooded figure in the passage of the disciples to Emmaus in Part V"; that is the card Madame Sosostris could not find. Some readers also hear Tiresias in "I do not know whether a man or a woman"; Eliot's notes do not say so.

### gloss:hordes
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "Stephen Hudson's English version, In Sight of Chaos (1923), has the two Dostoevsky essays." Bibliography, not help with the line; it belongs in the library record, whose title already gives the English name. Cut here and add there (see source:hesse-blick-ins-chaos below).
- "are usually read with it" and "has been read with the women": the idiom; say what is meant.
Proposed text:
Eliot's note on these lines quotes Hermann Hesse's <i>Blick ins Chaos</i> (Bern, 1920), three essays, two of them on Dostoevsky; the passage is the close of the first, "The Brothers Karamazov, or the Downfall of Europe." Eliot wrote to Hesse in March 1922 to praise the book, visited him at Montagnola on 28 May 1922, and printed him in the first number of <i>The Criterion</i>, October 1922, beside <i>The Waste Land</i>. The headnote calls this theme "the present decay of eastern Europe," and the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" are usually taken to refer to it. "Maternal lamentation" has been connected with the women who "bewailed and lamented" Christ on the way to the cross (Luke 23:27–28). Lines 375 to 377 name five cities and then "Unreal," the word of "Unreal City" at lines 60 and 207, this time with no city after it.

### gloss:bats
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "printed in the 1971 facsimile of the drafts among the poems that fed them": "fed" is a metaphor, and the clause is hard to parse.
- "that is a commentators' echo, not one of Eliot's notes": the formula; say it plainly.
Proposed text:
These lines rework an earlier fragment among Eliot's papers, beginning "So through the evening, through the violet air," which is printed in the 1971 facsimile of the drafts. The woman fiddling on her hair, the bats with baby faces, the towers upside down, the bells and the voices from cisterns and wells are already there. The bells that "kept the hours" repeat line 67, where Saint Mary Woolnoth kept them; the "violet hour" of lines 215 and 220 becomes "violet light." Commentators hear Jeremiah 2:13, "broken cisterns, that can hold no water," in "empty cisterns and exhausted wells"; Eliot's notes do not mention it.

### gloss:chapel
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "Weston's chapter ... gathers the adventure in which": "gathers" is odd with "adventure"; she collects versions of it.
- "and nothing happens in it": a reading (the scholar marked "the test already over" as one), stated as fact; and something does happen, the cock crows. Cut the clause; the quotation carries the point.
- "Commentators have set beside it": the idiom; "compare".
- "Then lightning and "a damp gust / Bringing rain," the first rain ...": begins without a verb.
Proposed text:
Weston's chapter "The Perilous Chapel" collects the versions of an adventure in which the knight, sheltering in a chapel in a waste place, finds a dead body on the altar, or sees a black hand put out the candle and hears "strange and threatening voices"; she reads it as a test of initiation on the way to the Grail. In the poem the chapel is empty, without windows, its door swinging: "Dry bones can harm no one." "Co co rico" is the French spelling of a cock-crow. Commentators compare the cock that crowed at Peter's denial (Matthew 26:74) and the cock of <i>Hamlet</i> I.i, at whose crowing "no spirit dare stir abroad." The "damp gust / Bringing rain" is the first rain in the poem since the shower of line 9. The poem does not say that it falls; at line 397 the leaves are still waiting for it.

### gloss:datta
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- The second sentence nests the Webster citation inside a "nor ... nor ... nor" list; I lose the thread by the third "nor". Split it.
Proposed text:
The one thing given is "The awful daring of a moment's surrender / Which an age of prudence can never retract," and "by this, and this only, we have existed." It is not in the obituaries, nor in "memories draped by the beneficent spider," nor "under seals broken by the lean solicitor," the will opened after a death. For the spider Eliot's note quotes Flamineo in Webster's <i>The White Devil</i>, Act V scene vi, on widows who remarry "ere the spider / Make a thin curtain for your epitaphs."

### gloss:dayadhvam
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "adds a page of F. H. Bradley's Appearance and Reality (1893), the philosopher on whom he wrote his Harvard dissertation": as written, "the philosopher" is in apposition to the book. Reorder.
Proposed text (the second sentence; the rest unchanged):
Eliot's note adds a page of <i>Appearance and Reality</i> (1893) by F. H. Bradley, on whom Eliot wrote his Harvard dissertation: "My external sensations are no less private to myself than are my thoughts or my feelings . . . the whole world for each is peculiar and private to that soul."

### gloss:fisher-king
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "as "an obviously post hoc addition"": Weston's words, but a reader who does not know "post hoc" gets nothing from them. Paraphrase; the scholar may add her words after it if wanted.
- "The echo is the commentators', not one of Eliot's notes; the poem says "lands," not "house," and gets no answer." The formula again, and it is the question, not the poem, that gets no answer.
Proposed text:
Eliot's note points to Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, the wounded keeper of the Grail whose land lies waste with him. Weston argues that his title comes from the fish as an ancient symbol of life, and dismisses the romances' own explanation, that the king fishes for pastime because his wound keeps him from riding, as a later invention. "Shall I at least set my lands in order?" echoes Isaiah 38:1, the prophet's message to the dying king Hezekiah: "Set thine house in order: for thou shalt die, and not live." Hezekiah turned his face to the wall and wept, and was given fifteen more years. Eliot's notes do not mention Isaiah; the poem says "lands," not "house," and the question gets no answer.

### gloss:fragments
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- Pervigilium: "the standard Latin text has "uti," which first appears in Collected Poems 1909–1935 (1936)": the "which" reads as if the standard Latin text first appeared in 1936. "Rainey": a reader does not know who that is; one clause tells them. "is also cited": by whom.
- Nerval: "read the tower with the Tarot's Tower": the idiom; "connect".
- Kyd: "The text of The Waste Land itself has six languages (...), with Greek in the epigraph and Provençal in the notes." As it stands this is a count with no reason given, the old bow in plainer clothes. Either cut it or say why it is there: commentators compare Hieronimo's play with the poem.
- Shantih: add the year to Poems 1909–1925, as the way does.
- The rest (London Bridge, Purgatorio with "He goes willingly", "These fragments", Datta) reads well; keep.
Proposed text (the sentences named; the rest unchanged):
[Pervigilium] Every printing from 1922 to 1932, and Eliot's fair copies, read "ceu"; the standard Latin text has "uti," and Eliot's line was changed to "uti" in <i>Collected Poems 1909–1935</i> (1936). Lawrence Rainey, in his annotated edition of the poem, doubts that the change was Eliot's. "O swallow swallow" is Tennyson's, from <i>The Princess</i> ("O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South"); commentators also cite Swinburne's "Itylus" ("Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow").
[Nerval] Some commentators connect the tower with the Tarot's Tower, struck by lightning; Nerval does not say so.
[Kyd, last sentence] Hieronimo's play in four languages has been compared with the poem, whose text has six (English, German, French, Italian, Latin and Sanskrit), with Greek in the epigraph and Provençal in the notes.
[Shantih, end] ...and from <i>Poems 1909–1925</i> (1925) the note reads "our equivalent to this word."

**The reader, round 2**

## C2. Ways

### way:Eliot reading the thunder
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "Listen to the last thirty lines, from the thunder on." The thunder speaks at line 400, thirty-five lines from the end; the two halves of the sentence do not agree.
Proposed text:
Eliot recorded the whole poem in 1933 and again in 1946; both are in the listening room, with Part V as he read it in Washington in 1947. Listen from line 400, where the thunder speaks, to the end.

### way:Water in Part V
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "No rain falls from line 331 to line 394. At line 395 a gust brings rain; at 397 the leaves are still waiting for it": the first sentence implies that rain falls at 395, which the next clause denies. Say "no water".
Proposed text:
The Water lens tints every line that is about water or the want of it. From line 331 to line 394 there is no water. At line 395 a gust brings rain, and at 397 the leaves are still waiting for it; at 419 a boat answers "the hand expert with sail and oar" on a calm sea; at 425 the arid plain is behind the fisherman.

### way:The road to Emmaus, and South Georgia
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "Two texts stand under": the timbers again. Say who gives them.
Proposed text:
Eliot gives two sources for "Who is the third who walks always beside you?": in his headnote, the journey to Emmaus of Luke 24; in his note on the line, Shackleton's account in <i>South</i> of crossing South Georgia in May 1916, when each of three men felt a fourth walking with them. Both are in the library.

### way:The places in Part V
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "two places of Eliot's own that the notes bring in: Quebec ... and Cape Ann": Quebec is in Eliot's note; Cape Ann is not, it is the commentators' (see gloss:damyata). Question for the editor: does the map in fact show these two? If not, cut the clause.
Proposed text:
The section names Jerusalem, Athens, Alexandria, Vienna and London in two lines, then the Ganges and the Himalaya, then London Bridge and Aquitaine. The world map shows the cities, the river and the mountain, and two places from Eliot's life: Quebec, where his note says he heard the hermit-thrush, and Cape Ann, where he sailed as a boy.

**The reader, round 2**

## C3. Paths

### path:women/368
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- ""Murmur of maternal lamentation." Read with the women who lamented ...": the caption begins with an imperative; I read it as an order to read the line alongside Luke.
Proposed text:
"Murmur of maternal lamentation." Commentators connect it with the women who lamented on the way to the cross (Luke 23:27) and with the headnote's "present decay of eastern Europe."

**The reader, round 2**

## C4. Drafts

### draft:ok-from-here
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "his cuts had fallen on the earlier parts": "fallen on" is literary; say he cut heavily there.
- "while under treatment by Roger Vittoz": who? One word tells me.
- "a sentence often applied to Part V": the scholar's "though Eliot did not" is worth keeping; it is the caution a reader needs.
Proposed text (the first three sentences; the rest unchanged):
At the head of the typescript of Part V Pound wrote "OK from here on I think," and cut almost nothing; he had cut heavily in the earlier parts. Eliot wrote the section at Lausanne between 22 November 1921 and 2 January 1922, while under treatment by Dr Roger Vittoz. In an essay on Pascal in 1931 he wrote that in some forms of illness "long passages may be produced which require little or no retouch," a sentence readers have applied to Part V, though Eliot did not.

### draft:manuscript-fate
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "with Pound's and Vivien's pencil on them": a reader does not know who Vivien is.
- "sent the drafts ... to John Quinn, the New York lawyer and collector who had negotiated his American contract, as a gift": "as a gift" arrives after a long detour; "gave" says it at once.
Proposed text (the first sentence; the rest unchanged):
In October 1922 Eliot gave the drafts, with Pound's pencil and his wife Vivien's on them, to John Quinn, the New York lawyer and collector who had negotiated his American contract; Quinn accepted them on condition that he could buy the notebook of Eliot's early poems, for which he paid $140.

**The reader, round 2**

## D. Proposals for the site thread

### site:water-lens-intro
Verdict: accept
Findings:
- "Tints every line that is wet, dry or burning." does the job. The second sentence is a plain fact, but a lens intro is a line, and this makes it two; if the site thread wants one line, the first sentence alone will do.
Proposed text: none

### pathway:water
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "The poem wants water and is drowned by it; the pathway shows both." My own sentence from round 1, and reading it again it is a contrast turned for sound with a small bow at the end. Cut it.
Proposed text:
Every line in which water is present or wanted, from the shower of line 9 to the gust of line 395.

### source:bradley
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "the thunder's 'Dayadhvam,' sympathise, is answered with it": I cannot tell what "it" is or who answers.
Proposed text (the last sentence):
Eliot's note sets the page beside Ugolino's tower, in the lines that answer the thunder's "Dayadhvam," sympathise.

### source:hesse-blick-ins-chaos
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- If the Hudson sentence leaves gloss:hordes, it belongs here, where the title already gives the English name.
Proposed text (add after the first sentence of the note):
Stephen Hudson's English version, <i>In Sight of Chaos</i> (1923), has the two Dostoevsky essays.

### voice:poem
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "It is never quite a person." An aphorism to end on. "speaks the thunder's glosses": "glosses" is our word for the notes, not the poem's.
Proposed text:
The poem's own voice, not a character: it opens the poem, describes the room in Part II, speaks the elegy for Phlebas, and answers the thunder's three words.

### voice:pilgrim
Verdict: accept
Findings:
- The note is plain. The label "The pilgrim on the road" decides among Eliot's three themes (Emmaus, the Grail quest, eastern Europe); "The walker on the road" would not. For the site thread to weigh; I do not press it.
Proposed text: none

### voice:seer
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "the nightmare": tells me how to feel about lines 378 to 385, which the gloss no longer does.
Proposed text:
Lines 367 to 395: the hooded hordes, the falling towers, the woman and the bats, the empty chapel.

### voice:thunder
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "DA. Three times." Two fragments and a count.
Proposed text:
The thunder's one syllable, at lines 401, 411 and 418.

### voice:fisherking
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- "Lines 424–426, and the fragments that follow": but way:The last voices says the lens gives 427 to 432 to the fragments, not to the Fisher King. One of the two is wrong; the note should say what the lens does.
Proposed text:
Lines 424 to 426: "I sat upon the shore / Fishing, with the arid plain behind me."

### voice:fragments
Verdict: accept
Findings:
- Plain. "Kyd" could be "Kyd's Hieronimo," to match the way; not worth a fight.
Proposed text: none

### voice:upanishad
Verdict: amend
Findings:
- The note is only the two lines quoted; it tells me nothing the page does not.
Proposed text:
Lines 433 and 434: the thunder's three words repeated, and the word that ends an Upanishad.

**The reader, round 2**

## Accepted without comment
