**The reader, round 2**

## Still unsettled

1. The language count in the Kyd paragraph (gloss:fragments). The scholar's proposed text states the six languages of the poem as a bare fact at the end of the paragraph; to me that is the old "A poem in seven languages" bow with the number corrected. I will take the count only inside a stated comparison, as in my sentence above; if the scholar will not have the comparison, the sentence should go.
2. Instructions in the ways. The scholar's proposed texts keep "Read both", "Play it, then read line 358, and judge", "Read them as a chorus, then as one man", and "Read the last line once as a blessing and once as a man saying the word to himself". The editor cut them and I hold to that: a "do" way may say what to press ("Play it, then read line 358"), not how to hear it. Settled by the editor unless the scholar reopens it.
3. "the phrase is Eliot's, not Chapman's" (gloss:no-water and the thrush way). What the scholar established is that the phrase is not in Chapman and not found in ornithological writing; that it is Eliot's own is an inference. I would write "is not Chapman's" and stop. Substance, so the scholar's call; I record it.
4. "nothing happens in it" (gloss:chapel). The editor's phrase, not the scholar's; I have cut it above. If the scholar wants the reading kept, it should be marked as one: "Readers have taken the empty chapel to mean that the test is already over."
5. Two questions for the editor rather than disagreements: whether the world map shows Quebec and Cape Ann (way:The places in Part V), and which of way:The last voices and voice:fisherking describes what the Voices lens actually does with lines 427 to 432.

**The scholar, round 3**

## Unsettled
Nothing here blocks printing; each is a statement I believe true but could not check against the page that decides it.
1. Russell's letter: "which" or "that". Site (way "OK from here on"): Eliot told Russell in October 1923 that Part V was "the only part which justifies the whole, at all." Settles it: The Letters of T. S. Eliot, vol. 2, 1923–1925 (Faber, 2009), the letter to Russell of 15 October 1923. My only transcription is Leithauser and Dyer in Russell (summer 1982), which prints "which".
2. Pound's pencil note. Site (torchlight gloss, the way, the draft note): "OK from here on I think" at the head of the Part V typescript. Settles it: The Waste Land: A Facsimile and Transcript (1971), the first leaf of the Part V typescript. Keane (Numéro Cinq, 2011) and the Spectator review of 1 January 1972 report the words; I have not seen the page.
3. The older fragments. Site (draft note): lines 322–330 draw on "After the turning of the inspired days", lines 378–385 on "So through the evening, through the violet air"; "Several passages of the poem are older than 1921." Settles it: facsimile pp. 108–9 and 112–13, and Valerie Eliot's editorial notes at the back, which date the fragments; Ricks and McCue, The Poems of T. S. Eliot, vol. I (2015), commentary on lines 322 and 378.
4. Source of "water-dripping song". Site: see the block above. Settles it: Ricks and McCue, vol. I, commentary on the note to line 357; Chandran (ELN 43.1, 2005); the N&Q note (61.1, March 2014); Irmscher (Partial Answers 12.2, 2014).
5. The 1946 essay. Site (draft note manuscript-fate): Eliot said in 1946 that the poem left Pound's hands "reduced to about half its size." Settles it: "Ezra Pound", Poetry 68.6 (September 1946), 326–38. The words are confirmed only through two quotations of the essay.
6. The visit to Hesse. Site (Hesse gloss): Eliot "visited him at Montagnola on 28 May 1922". Settles it: Letters, vol. 1 (rev. 2009), the letter to Hesse of 13 March 1922 and its note; Ricks and McCue on lines 366–376.
7. In Sight of Chaos. Site (proposal for source hesse-blick-ins-chaos): Hudson's 1923 version "has the two Dostoevsky essays". Settles it: the contents of the Seldwyla volume of 1923 (a library catalogue record will do).
8. The second complete recording. Site (way "Listen to Part V"): "recorded the whole poem in 1933 and again in 1946"; the listening room says "usually dated 1946". Settles it: the Library of Congress record for the reading in its Archive of Recorded Poetry and Literature, or the Caedmon sleeve notes (TC 1326).

**The scholar, round 3**

## Sign-off
Satisfied with the part as a whole once the "water-dripping" attribution is softened as above: every other statement in draft3, and in the final/ files as they now stand, is true and honestly attributed as far as the sources let me check.

**The reader, round 3**

## If it were my call
- gloss:torchlight: shorten by cutting the Russell sentence; the "OK from here on" way carries it, and the card is thirteen sentences long.
- gloss:damyata: cut "the Dry Salvages of his later poem lie off that coast"; an aside about another poem.
- gloss:fragments, Kyd: cut the last sentence, the language comparison; it is now honest, but it helps little with the line.
- path:nothing/402: a stop that exists only because the build cannot remove it; remove it when the build thread can.
- Nothing else I would throw out.

**The reader, round 3**

## Unsettled
None on wording between the scholar and me. On fact, the scholar's own list stands (the Russell letter's "which", the facsimile page for Pound's note, p. 108 for the fragment behind lines 322 to 330), and none of it blocks printing.

**The reader, round 3**

## Sign-off
Satisfied. Read as a whole, Part V now says what the lines refer to, what is known and how, and whose reading a reading is, in sentences a person would say; the two examples Ethan quoted, and everything of their kind, are gone.

## The editor between rounds

### Round 1, the editor: decisions and the rewrites

The rewritten files are in scratchpad/work/draft1/ (glosses.txt, ways.txt, paths.txt, drafts.txt). Fixed fields are unchanged; a script checked them. What follows is what I took from whom, and why, where the two reports differed or where a fact was in doubt.

Substance, from the scholar:
- torchlight: no thunder at the crucifixion in any Gospel; the earthquake is Matthew 27:51. The note now says so and calls the thunder the poem's own. John 18:18 ("it was cold") added for "frosty", as the reader asked.
- torchlight and the OK-from-here way and draft: Pound's "OK from here on I think" could not be found online by the scholar; I found it quoted from the facsimile in Patrick Keane's essay on the manuscript (Numéro Cinq, 2011: "he had marked, 'OK from here on I think'"). Kept.
- torchlight, way, draft: the Russell letter (15 October 1923). The site had "the only part that justifies the whole, at all"; the scholar's source (Leithauser's article, quoting the Letters) has "which". I have gone with the cited source and list it under unsettled; the printed Letters should be checked before anyone quotes it again.
- no-water, the thrush way, the water/353 stop: "water-dripping song" is Eliot's phrase, not Chapman's (the scholar read the whole Handbook); "exact" and "accurate" are gone. One clause keeps the ornithologists' description so the reader knows what to expect from the recording.
- fisher-king: Weston's chapter IX rejects the explanation the gloss put in her mouth. Rewritten to say what she argues.
- da: V.2, with Eliot's "5, 1" noted; Deussen is a translation the note points to, not its source; Müller quoted for the thunder's sentence.
- dayadhvam and the dead/412 stop: "chiavar" is nailed up or locked; both given, and Eliot's "key" placed.
- hordes: the quote field now reads Hesse's text ("in heiligem Wahn", "Über"), as the library record does; the body gives the three essays, the English version's contents, and the visit of 28 May 1922.
- fragments: Gomme named for the foundation-sacrifice reading; "uti" dated to Collected Poems 1909–1935 with Rainey's doubt; Swinburne added; the Tarot Tower marked as a reading; four languages in Kyd's play; six languages in the text of the poem; the Shantih note's 1925 revision dated.
- drafts: "in a trance" dropped (no such words of Eliot's found); the 1931 Pascal sentence put in its place, marked as often applied to Part V. The $140 bought the notebook of early poems, not the drafts, which were a gift (Menand, NYRB 1997, from Ricks); the papers passed through Quinn's family and were thought lost, not "vanished"; "800 lines" replaced by Eliot's own 1946 phrase, "reduced to about half its size". "After the turning of the inspired days" added as the fragment behind lines 322 to 330.
- counts: "Bringing rain" is two words, and the poem never shows the rain fall (397 "Waited for rain", 425 "the arid plain"); the last eight lines are in five languages; "nothing" is not said in Part V; the poem's last places are London Bridge and Aquitaine, not the Ganges. Every count that was there to sound clever is gone; the ones that remain are there because a reader can use them (no full stop from 331 to 359; the first rain since line 9).
- the voices way said the lens colours each of the last eight lines differently; it does not (427 to 432 are one voice, "the fragments"). Rewritten to what the lens does.

Wording, from the reader:
- every closing aphorism cut; every "X is a Y:" list broken into sentences with verbs; every instruction on how to feel cut; every reading marked as one ("has been read as", "commentators", "usually read with").
- titles: "The Passion" (was "The Passion, in nine lines, without the resurrection"); "From an earlier fragment" (was "A nightmare from an older notebook"); "Sympathise: Ugolino and Bradley"; "Control" (was "Control: a boat off Cape Ann"); "The last eight lines" (was "The last eight lines, one by one"). Ways: "Water in Part V", "The languages of the last lines", "The fragments, one by one", "The fable in the Upanishad", "The places in Part V", "The last word".
- the list title "Twelve ways into What the Thunder Said" is shared by the five parts and stays; the reader found the pattern mild.

Left out because unverified: the date of the two fragments; the draft reading "your heart responded" for line 421 and the lines cut after 423 (the scholar's memory of the facsimile); the manner of Eliot's "DA" in the recordings (the way now gives the recordings' dates instead).

Fixed fields the build thread should know about: the fisher-king card's quote box shows a verse of Isaiah under the source "weston" (source is a fixed field); the pathway stop nothing/402 cannot be removed without the build thread, so its text now says plainly that the word is absent from Part V.

### Round 2, the editor: what was taken

From the reader, every amendment (28): the idiom "read with", "set beside", "stand under", "hear behind" replaced by plain words; the tag marking a reading as not Eliot's replaced by "Eliot's notes do not mention it" or "do not say so"; the Hudson sentence moved from the hordes gloss to the library-record proposal; "nothing happens in it" cut from the chapel gloss; the Webster citation given its own sentence in the datta gloss; the Bradley appositive reordered; "post hoc" paraphrased as "a later invention"; the ceu/uti sentence untangled and Rainey identified; the Kyd language count kept only inside the comparison the reader asked for; "Dr" before Vittoz; "his wife Vivien"; "though Eliot did not" restored in the drafts record; the recording way now says "from line 400, where the thunder speaks, to the end"; the water way says "no water", not "no rain falls".

From the scholar, both corrections of fact in the rewrites: John 18:18 ("it was cold") is the high priest's courtyard after the arrest, not the garden, and the sentence now says so; Coriolanus was banished for refusing to bend to the people but yields to his mother at the end, so "unable to bend" is gone and the sentence tells the story. Also: the Hamlet clause now quotes "the trumpet to the morn" and says the ghost fades at the crowing, instead of running two passages together; "Patañjali's metaphysics" (Eliot's word); Gomme dated 1894; "American contracts"; the water pathway intro starts from the spring rain of line 4; the voice note on the fragments now names Tennyson at 429; the Whitman library record adds the 1926 review.

Answers to the reader's two questions: the world map does show Quebec and Cape Ann (both are in the site's list of world places, at lines 357 and 419), so the places way keeps them; the Voices lens gives lines 427 to 432 to "the fragments", so the way is right and the voice note on the Fisher King is what needs changing, and the proposal to the site thread says so.

Unsettled after two rounds, on substance (the scholar's call): the Russell letter's "which" stands on the one reachable transcription; "the phrase is Eliot's, not Chapman's" stays, since the note presents the phrase as a quotation and no source for it has been found. On wording (the reader's call): the ways keep "Play it, then read line 358" and "Listen from line 400" as things to do, and no instruction on how to hear them.

### Round 3, the editor: the final text

Taken from the reader: the John 18:18 sentence split in two, so the cold belongs to the night and not to Peter's standing; the voices way now lists the fragments itself ("a Latin poet and Tennyson") instead of promising what the lens's note says, so it stays true whether or not the site thread takes the voice-note proposal; "and Tennyson" in that proposal. Of the reader's "if it were my call" list, one taken: the Russell sentence is cut from the torchlight gloss, which was thirteen sentences long, and stays in the "OK from here on" way. Not taken: the Dry Salvages clause in the damyata gloss, because it is the route by which commentators connect the boat to Cape Ann; the Kyd comparison, which the scholar wanted and the reader made honest; the nothing/402 stop, which the build thread must remove, not this one.

Taken from the scholar: "the phrase is Eliot's, not Chapman's" softened in the gloss, the way and the library-record proposal to "not Chapman's, and no source for it is agreed", since sources have been proposed for it and none accepted.

Both signed off on the part as a whole.

## What was not settled

Nothing that follows blocks printing. Each is a statement both sides believe true that could not be checked against the page that decides it; the scholar names the page.

1. Eliot's letter to Russell of 15 October 1923: "the only part which justifies the whole, at all" (the site had "that"). Settles it: The Letters of T. S. Eliot, vol. 2 (2009). The one reachable transcription (Leithauser and Dyer, Russell, 1982, from the McMaster originals) reads "which".
2. Pound's pencil note "OK from here on I think" at the head of the Part V typescript. Attested by Keane (Numéro Cinq, 2011) and, for the substance, the Spectator's review of the facsimile (1 January 1972); the page of the 1971 facsimile is unrecorded.
3. The two older fragments behind lines 322 to 330 and 378 to 385 ("After the turning of the inspired days"; "So through the evening, through the violet air"). Their existence and content are certain; their dates are not printed here. Settles it: facsimile pp. 108 to 113 and Valerie Eliot's notes; Ricks and McCue on lines 322 and 378.
4. The source of "water-dripping song" in Eliot's note. Not Chapman (full text checked); Henley and a saw-whet-owl story have been proposed and neither accepted. The site now says "not Chapman's, and no source for it is agreed".
5. Eliot's 1946 words on Pound's cutting, "reduced to about half its size": confirmed through two quotations of the Poetry essay (September 1946), not the page.
6. The visit to Hesse at Montagnola on 28 May 1922: from the Letters at second hand.
7. That Hudson's In Sight of Chaos (1923) contains the two Dostoevsky essays only: from a catalogue description.
8. That Eliot's second complete recording is 1946: the listening room says "usually dated 1946"; the Library of Congress record would settle it.

On wording, nothing is unsettled between the two sides. The reader would still, if it were the reader's call, cut the Dry Salvages clause from the "Control" gloss, the Kyd language comparison from the last gloss, and the pathway stop at line 402 (which exists only because the build cannot drop a stop without the build thread); the editor kept the first two and left the third to the build thread.

Two things for the build thread, because they are fixed fields: the Fisher King card's quotation box shows a verse of Isaiah under the source "weston"; and the "nothing" pathway's stop at line 402 is on a line that does not contain the word, which the stop's text now says.

## Summary of the change

Of 51 items in Part V, 44 were rewritten: all 13 glosses (5 retitled), all 12 ways in (6 retitled), 17 of 24 pathway stops, both drafts records. Seven stops were already plain and stand as they were.

Corrected on fact: thunder at the crucifixion (no Gospel has it); "water-dripping song" attributed to Chapman (not in his book); Weston's Fisher King chapter summarised as saying what it denies; the $140 (paid for the notebook of early poems, not the drafts, which were a gift); the papers "vanished" (they were in Quinn's family); "800 lines" (replaced by Eliot's own "reduced to about half its size"); "in a trance" (no such words of Eliot's found; the 1931 Pascal sentence put in its place); "three words" of rain (two); "six languages" in the last eight lines (five); "the only rain" and "everything after that is wet" (the poem never shows the rain fall); "the poem's last places are a river and a mountain" (London Bridge and Aquitaine follow); the Voices lens "colours each of the last eight lines differently" (it gives six of them one colour); the "nothing" pathway's "last appearance" in Part V (the word is not there); Deussen as Eliot's "source" (a translation his note points to); the Hesse quotation's "im heiligem" (Hesse wrote "in heiligem"); John 18:18 in the garden (the high priest's courtyard); Coriolanus "unable to bend" (he yields to his mother). Marked as readings, with whose they are: the Hanged Man, Tiresias and "nobody" for the third; the refugees and armies; the women at the cross; Jeremiah's cisterns; Peter's cock and Hamlet's; the Tarot Tower for Nerval; the will of the lean solicitor; the boat off Cape Ann; Isaiah for "set my lands in order".

Thrown out for voice: every closing aphorism (twelve of thirteen glosses had one); every count that was there to sound clever; every "X is a Y:" opening with its list of three; every instruction on how to feel or when to look something up; the fragments for rhythm ("Would have."; "Three words."; "No Easter follows."; "Not birds. Included for the wings."); the metaphors about the poem ("stops narrating and starts stacking"; "propped like timbers against a wall"); the tour-guide and joke titles ("The Passion, in nine lines, without the resurrection"; "A nightmare from an older notebook"; "What the thunder actually said"; "From Margate to the Ganges"; "Peace, or the word for it").

## Afterwards

The build thread pulled part5 on 20 September 2026 (evening) and made two changes that only it could: the Fisher King gloss now carries a caption under its quotation, "Isaiah 38:1", so the verse is no longer captioned Weston; and the Nothing pathway's stop at line 402 is dropped, so that pathway ends at line 305, on "expect / Nothing". The card notes' production remarks ("No accent", a file name) came from the build's parsing of the artist's notes and are stripped on the site. The same two edits were made in commentary/part5/ so that the delivered files match the site.
